7-3-1 Hongo, Bunkyo-ku, Tokyo, 113-0033, Japan

=== PDF PAGE 4 ===


=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
v
Abstract
The Higgs boson was discovered by the A TLAS and CMS Collaborations in 2012 using the proton-
proton collisions at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC). The corresponding Higgs ﬁeld is in charge
of giving masses of bosons and fermions in the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics. Since the
Higgs sector is not well-understood, probing the Higgs sector is important to understand physics
in electroweak phase transition in the SM. While the SM provides the successful description in
particle physics, given that the SM cannot explain remaining problems such as the hierarchy prob-
lem, new physics beyond the SM (BSM) is demanded. If the Higgs sector would be extended as
solutions for these problems, the fact could be observed by the properties of the Higgs boson such
as cross-section, which might be modiﬁed from the prediction in the SM. Since the extension of
Higgs sector is discussed in wide-ranged major BSM physics theories, it is motivated to use the
Higgs boson in physics analysis for probing the BSM physics. Therefore, measurements of the
Higgs boson properties with high precision are being conducted at the LHC.
The diﬀerential cross-section of the Higgs boson production associated with a Z/Wboson (VH
production), and the coupling strength of the Higgs boson and the bottom quarks are two of the
sensitive observables to BSM physics. If signiﬁcant deviations from the SM ( ∼5%) are observed,
they can imply new physics in energy scale of 1 - 3 TeV , and determine the direction of particle
physics for future. This study targets on VH(H→b¯b,V→leptons) process. Choosing a decay
mode of a pair of two bottom quarks with a branching fraction of 58 % is reasonable to obtain
enough statistics of VHproduction. Besides, focusing the VHproduction mode is reasonable to tag
theH→b¯bdecay events with high sensitivity. With these mutual proﬁts, targeting the VH(→b¯b)
process is eﬀective approach to measure the di ﬀerential cross-section of the VHproduction and
inclusive cross-section of the VH(→b¯b) process that is equivalent to the coupling strength of the
H→b¯bdecay. Since the dominant uncertainty of the previous VH(→b¯b) analysis comes from
systematic errors rather than its statistical uncertainty, improving analysis techniques to suppress
the systematic uncertainty is the major theme in this thesis.

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
vi
This thesis presents studies for improvement of precise measurement of the VH(→b¯b) process
using 140 fb−1of proton-proton collision data at
→
s=13 TeV collected in the A TLAS detector.
There are three major results from these studies:
•Inclusive cross-section of the VH(H→b¯b) process (the highest precision)
•Inclusive cross-section of both the WHandZHproduction (the ﬁrst observation)
•Diﬀerential cross-section of the VHproduction (the highest performance)
The observed signal strength of VH(→b¯b) process, which is deﬁned as a ratio of observed signal
yields to predicted signal yields with respect to the SM, is
µbb
VH=0.92+0.16
−0.14=0.92±0.10(stat.)+0.13
−0.11(syst.).
According to the measurement of signal strength, the observed inclusive VHcross-section times
V→leptons andH→b¯bbranching ratio is 170 .1+29.6
−25.9[fb] within the analysis phase space. These
are the most precise results of the VHproduction cross-section at
→
s=13 TeV ever as of 2025.
In the measurement of inclusive cross-section of both of the WHandZHproduction, observed
(expected) signal signiﬁcance for background-only hypothesis in the WHproduction is 5.3(5.5)
σ, and the observed (expected) signal signiﬁcance in the ZHproduction is 4.9(5.6) σ. Especially,
the observation over 5 σis achieved in the WH(→b¯b) process for the ﬁrst time. The di ﬀerential
cross-section measurement of the VHproduction as functions of Z/Wboson momentum and jet
multiplicity is performed for the ﬁrst time. The observed cross-sections in almost all phase spaces
are consistent with the SM predictions. In some high Z/Wboson momentum categories, the ob-
served cross-sections are lower than the SM predictions within two standard deviations. In the
futureVH(H→b¯b) analysis with more statistics, the analysis techniques improved in this thesis
can help to suppress the systematic uncertainty, which may results in the observation of signiﬁcant
deviation from the SM prediction.

Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
1.1.1 The Standard Model (SM) of the particle physics
The Standard Model (SM) of particle physics is a relativistic ﬁeld theory to describe motions
of particles and interactions between elementary particles as the exchange of the force carrying
particles. Elementary particles and their interactions in the SM are summarised in Figure 1.1.
Quarks and leptons are fermions and building blocks of matters. The interactions between fermions
are mediated by gauge bosons, which are also called as vector bosons. Each type of gauge bosons
is associated with a speciﬁc interaction *1: photons for the electromagnetic interaction, gluons for
the strong interaction, and W/Zbosons for the weak interaction. The electromagnetic interaction
of quantum electrodynamics (QED) a ﬀects all the particles except an electrically neutral particle
such as a neutrino. The strong interaction of quantum chromodynamics (QCD) only interacts
with quarks due to their colour change. For the weak interaction, the W boson couples to only
left-handed particles, and the Z boson couples to both left-handed particles and right-handed anti-
particles. The SM is constructed based on gauge symmetry groups of SU(3) ×SU(2)L×U(1)Yin
gauge theory, where the SU(3) symmetry represents the strong interaction, and SU(2) L×U(1)Y
symmetry represents the electroweak interactions. The Higgs boson is a scalar boson to provide
masses to massive elementary particles of the SM. The fermion masses are generated via Y ukawa
interactions in the Higgs mechanism after spontaneous symmetry breaking happened, which is
brieﬂy summarised in Section 1.1.2. More details of the Higgs boson are in Section 1.2.
*1A gravitational force is not included in the SM

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
2 1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
Fig. 1.1: Elementary particles in the Standard Model [ 1]. The quarks and leptons are fermion
particles with 1 /2 spin. All matters are made up of these fermions. Anti-particles of fermions
are also available as particles with opposite sign quantum numbers. The vector bosons are 1 spin
particles and carry interactions. The Higgs boson is a 0 spin particle and gives masses to massive
elementary particles.
1.1.2 Spontaneous symmetry breaking
In the unbroken gauge symmetry system, all the elementary particles are massless. By intro-
ducing the Higgs mechanism to break symmetry spontaneously, the Higgs ﬁeld gives mass to the
particles*2as follows.
Lagrangian of the SM can be written as
LSM=LGauge+LHiggs+LY ukawa. (1.1)
LGauge represents the gauge interactions of the elementary particles, and kinematic terms of
fermions and gauge bosons. LHiggsrepresents the Higgs term. LY ukawarepresents the Y ukawa
interactions between fermions and Higgs.
*2A photon and a gluon are known as massless particles

1.1.3 Extension of SM Higgs sector
The SM has been tested and provides successful descriptions for the results of particle physics
experiments for now. However, because the SM cannot explain some remaining problems such as
no candidate particle of the dark matter, hierarchy problem and so on, new physics beyond the SM

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 5
(BSM) is demanded. There are some new BSM physics that can extend the Higgs sector in the
SM, for example, Minimal Supersymmetric Standard Model (MSSM) [ 3], and a composite Higgs
model [4]. These extensions of the SM Higgs sector propose the deviations from the SM that
can be observed in the high precise measurement. Therefore, the Higgs boson’s properties have
high sensitivity in searching for BSM. The precision of current measurements is still not enough to
ﬁgure out this evidence for BSM, but when the signiﬁcant deviations from the SM can be observed,
some BSM theories can be chosen as candidates for new physics and more veriﬁcations for these
theories can be performed. With this evidence for BSM in the high energy frontier physics, it’s
expected to be able to decide the direction of elementary particle physics in the future. Therefore,
the precise measurements of the Higgs boson’s properties are very important to accomplish the
discovery of new physics.
Herein, as the important properties of the Higgs boson to test possible new BSM physics models,
the coupling between the Higgs boson and the other particle, and di ﬀerential cross-section of the
Higgs production processes in certain phase space are measured. The extensions of SM Higgs
sector can modify the coupling between the Higgs boson and the other particles as shown in Figure
1.3. For diﬀerential cross-section measurement, the deviations can be interpreted by E ﬀective Field
Theory (EFT) framework as discussed in Section 1.1.4. This thesis focuses on the Higgs boson
production associated with a Z/Wboson in the decay channel of bottom quarks ( VH(H→b¯b))
as described in Section 1.2.3, whose Feynman diagram is shown in Figure 1.4. Hence, this thesis
measures the inclusive cross-section of VH(H→b¯b) process that is equivalent to the coupling
strength of the H→b¯b, as explained in Section 1.3.1, and the diﬀerential cross-section of the VH
production, as explained in Section 1.3.2, to examine their deviations from the SM predictions.
The remarkable vertices in the VH(H→b¯b) process in these measurements are also shown in
Figure1.4.
1.1.4 Probing new physics with Effective Field Theory (EFT)
Extension of the SM can be indirectly tested by the precise comparison of the experimental
observables and the SM predictions. By introducing many tree-level operators that can modify
Higgs observables, the deviations from the SM could be compared to the extended SM Lagrangian
with these operators in higher dimensions. The higher dimension operators are approximations of
the actual theory at the higher energy. The identiﬁcation of the corresponding higher dimension
operators, which describe the deviations, could help us specify the actual new BSM physics theory
at higher energy scale, as an approximation of possible UltraViolet completion theories. This
theory to extend the SM with arbitral higher dimension operators is called E ﬀective Field Theory

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
6 1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
Fig. 1.3: The expected deviation from the SM prediction for the coupling constant between the
Higgs boson and other particles [ 5]. The left plot introduces a model of Supersymmetry(SUSY);
the MSSM model, and the Y ukawa coupling of a bottom quark is predicted to be increased by
∼3 %. The right plot introduces a composite Higgs model, and the Y ukawa coupling of a bottom
quark is predicted to be decreased by ∼4 %.
Fig. 1.4: Feynman diagram of VH(H→b¯b) process. The targeted physics signal process in this
thesis. The remarkable vertices for the measurements conducted in this thesis are shown with red

1.2 The Higgs boson phenomenology in the LHC
The Higgs boson is an elementary particle in the SM and the massive scalar boson to give mass
to bosons and fermions of the SM with the so called Englert-Brout-Higgs mechanism [ 13,14,15,
16,17,18], which is shown in Section 1.1.2. The Higgs boson was discovered by the A TLAS
[19] and CMS [20] Collaborations in 2012 [ 21,22] with a mass of approximately 125 GeV using
the proton-proton ( pp) collisions produced by the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) [ 23]. Since this
discovery, about 30 times more Higgs boson data have been recorded in the A TLAS experiment,
and more precise measurements to test BSM physics theories have been performed. This thesis
focuses on an analysis of VH(H→b¯b) process (Details are in Section 1.2.3). This section reviews
productions and decay modes of the Higgs boson, and shows interest in the VH(H→b¯b) process.

Feynman diagrams for these modes are shown in Figure 1.6. Since the phase space of the Higgs
production depends on the Higgs mass, the cross-section of the Higgs production changes as shown
in Figure1.8(a). Figure 1.8(b) shows the predicted cross-section, which is calculated with

1.2.2 The Higgs boson decays
The produced Higgs boson immediately decays into other particles. Since the phase space of
the Higgs decay depends on the Higgs mass, the branching ratio of the Higgs decay changes as
shown in Figure 1.9(a). Figure 1.9(b) shows the predicted branching ratio, which is calculated
with
→
s=13 TeV and the Higgs mass as 125 GeV , and the observed branching ratio measured
with Run2 dataset (Section 3.1) of the A TLAS experiment. The observed branching ratios are
consistent with the predicted branching ratios within their uncertainties, and more evaluation of
the observed result of H→b¯bdecay mode is discussed in Section 1.3.1. The dominant decay
mode isH→b¯b. This mode has a branching ratio of 58 .2%[26].

1.2.3 VH(V→lepton,H→b¯b)process
The precise measurement of VHproduction may probe the BSM physics as discussed in Section
1.1.3. Moreover, as targeting a leptonic decay mode of a vector boson *5which can be detected
in high momentum resolution, the measurement can provide a sensitive probe for momentum
dependent higher dimensional operators in the EFT as discussed in Section 1.1.4. Considering the
low statistics of the VHproduction measurement due to the cross-section of the VHproduction

and the branching ratio of the leptonic decaying vector boson, H→b¯bdecay mode with the

WH(W→µν,H→b¯b) process in the dataset recorded in the A TLAS experiment (Section 3.1)

theVH(H→b¯b) process has many background processes such as V+jets andt¯tprocesses. The
key of precise measurement of the VH(H→b¯b) process is, thus, to reduce and constrain these
backgrounds as much as possible.

tails in Appendix B. Looking at the results of the VHproduction in Figure 1.11, the analysis of
VH(H→b¯b) process is already established [ 28,12] and it has the lowest uncertainty thanks to the
largest statistics. Systematic uncertainty determines the precision of the VH(H→b¯b) analysis,
while the other VHanalyses need more statistics.
This analysis ﬁxes the mass of Higgs boson( mH) to 125 GeV, because this analysis is not very
sensitive to the mH, and because this analysis focuses on precise measurements of the properties
of the Higgs boson, which can be performed accurately enough by assuming the mH=125 GeV
as a benchmark.

1.3 Prior measurements for the VH(H→b¯b)process
This section presents two kinds of measurements in the previous VH(H→b¯b) analyses.
1.3.1 Higgs-bottom Y ukawa coupling measurement
The Y ukawa coupling of a bottom quark and the Higgs boson (Section 1.1.2) is known to be
sensitive to BSM physics. Some BSM theories predict deviations from the SM for the Higgs-
bottom Y ukawa coupling for ∼3−5 % as shown in Figure 1.3.
The measurement of a signal strength as deﬁned in Eq. ( 1.15) is performed to evaluate the
deviation of the Y ukawa coupling of a bottom quark from the SM prediction.

with an observed (expected) signiﬁcance of 6.4(6.3) standard deviations for the background-only
prediction. The uncertainty is not small enough to evaluate the deviation from the SM prediction.
The systematic uncertainty is the dominant uncertainty in the inclusive VH(H→b¯b) analysis,
and the major systematic uncertainties are modelling uncertainties and experimental uncertainties,
which can be reduced by the analysis techniques.
1.3.2 Fiducial differential cross-section measurement
As explained in Section 1.1.4, cross-section of VHproduction is sensitive to BSM physics
processes, especially in the phase space of high transverse momentum of a vector boson ( pV
T).
The advantage to measure cross-section rather than the signal strength is to reduce the contribu-
tion of signal theoretical uncertainty to the measurement. When the signal strength is measured,
several theoretical uncertainties from the simulated SM prediction (Section 9.2.1) are folded into
the measurement. Meanwhile, the di ﬀerential cross-section measurement only considers the un-
certainty of the fraction of event yields in an analysis phase space derived by MC simulation,
referred to as “acceptance” uncertainty.
TheVH(V→lep,H→b¯b) analysis adopts a Simpliﬁed Template Cross-section (STXS) frame-
work to measure the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section of VHproduction mode.
Simpliﬁed Template Cross-section (STXS) framework [26,29]:
This framework is developed and established in order to maximise the Higgs signal sensitiv-
ity of the measurements, while at the same time, to minimise the theory dependencies which
are directly folded into the measurements. Phase space of the di ﬀerential cross-section is
divided by simpliﬁed (i.e. ideal) “STXS bins”. The STXS bins are deﬁned with respect to
the following points.
• To minimise acceptance uncertainties; estimated at the boundary of the STXS bins.
• To maximise experimental sensitivity.
• To isolate possible BSM e ﬀects from other BSM e ﬀects.
Since the optimisation of the STXS bins di ﬀers between topologies of the Higgs production
modes, the measurement with the STXS framework is conducted separately for the pro-
duction modes. In the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement, the STXS bins are
applied to the phase space based on simulation-based truth particle information, referred to
as “truth level” *6(Section3.2.1), so that the results of di ﬀerent experiments in the LHC
*6Since objects are reconstructed from discrete detected data /sample in a particle physics analysis, generally, the event
categorisation into regions is conducted based on the reconstructed object information; referred to as “reconstructed
level”

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 15
can be combined.
In theVH(V→lep) production mode, the ﬁnest STXS bins are deﬁned with two kinematic
variables, as shown in Figure 1.12: Transverse momentum of a vector boson ( pV,t
T), and number of
jets originated from Initial State Radiation (ISR) in truth level. This binning deﬁnition is e ﬀective
in terms of separating the contribution of di ﬀerent higher dimension operators, or separating the
signal processes by partons (quarks and gluons) in their initial states. In this analysis, as shown
in Figure1.12, the cross-sections of WHandZHare measured separately. The cross-sections of
qq→ZHandgg→ZHprocesses are measured inclusively because the signal sensitivity with an
available dataset is currently insu ﬃcient to distinguish these two processes.
Fig. 1.12: Deﬁnition of the phase spaces of the STXS framework for VHproduction mode [ 26,29].
The phase space is divided by two variables; a vector boson transverse momentum and the number
of ISR jets. The measurement is performed separately based on the ZHprocess or the WHprocess.
The result of the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement in the previous analysis [ 12] is
shown in Figure 1.13. Due to time constraints, the binning is reduced, and the measurement with
the binning based on only the pV,t

the highpV,t

within their uncertainties. The SMEFT interpretation from this result is summarised in Appendix

1.4 ImprovedVH(H→b¯b)analysis
The analysis of the VH(H→b¯b) process is re-analysed with improved analysis techniques and
strategy as described in Section 1.4.1. The improved analysis allows us [A.] to improve the preci-
sion ofVH(H→b¯b) signal strength measurement, and [B.] to perform ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-
section measurement with ﬁner bins.
1.4.1 Analysis strategy
The main target process of this analysis is the VH(H→b¯b) signal process, but a coherent treat-
ment with the VH(H→c¯c) process is reasonable to perform due to the similarity of the H→b¯b
process and H→c¯cprocess*7in terms of the major backgrounds and the object reconstruc-
*7H→c¯cmode has a branching ratio of 2 .9%[26]

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 17
tion such as jets. Therefore, this analysis aims to perform the measurement of VH(H→b¯b) and
VH(H→c¯c) processes simultaneously. It should be noted that the e ﬀect of theVH(H→c¯c) pro-
cess is limited since its statistics is very low. This combination can provide signiﬁcant gains for
the constraint on the background processes for both the VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) processes.
Hereinafter, VH(H→b¯b) is also denoted as VH(b¯b),VH(H→c¯c) asVH(c¯c). As illustrated in
Figure1.14, the three individual previous analyses [ 28,30,31] are combined, and categorised into
three working points in this analysis: “resolved VH(b¯b) regime” and “boosted VH(b¯b) regime” tar-
geting theVH(H→b¯b) signal process, and “ VH(c¯c) regime” targeting the VH(H→c¯c) process.
The diﬀerent analysis regimes are separated by the dedicated reconstruction and identiﬁcation
technique of the bottom and charm quark jets (ﬂavour tagging), and separated by the transverse
momentum of the vector boson ( pV
T) at 400 GeV . With the ﬂavour tagging, the events in pV
T<400
GeV are categorised orthogonally into the resolved VH(b¯b) regime and VH(c¯c) regime, as dis-
cussed in Section 5.1.3.1. For the events in pV
T>400 GeV , the dedicated bottom quark jet tagging
is deployed in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, but the same ﬂavour tagging used in pV
T<400 GeV
is deployed in the VH(c¯c) regime, as discussed in Section 5.1.3.2, since dedicated charm quark
jet tagging in the boosted regime is not developed enough yet. The analysis techniques in the
resolvedVH(b¯b) regime and the VH(c¯c) regime are harmonized as much as possible, based on
the idea that the only di ﬀerence between these two regimes should be the ﬂavour tagging. The
VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) analysis uses the leptonic decay modes of the vector boson. There are three dif-
ferent analysis channels targeting the number of charged leptons decayed from the vector boson *8;
Z→ννas ”0-lepton” channel, W→ℓνas ”1-lepton” channel and Z→ℓℓas ”2-lepton” channel,
whereℓrefers to electrons or muons. This thesis mainly explains the analysis techniques relating
to theVH(H→b¯b) signal process, including the constraint of the background process from the
VH(c¯c) regime.
1.4.2 Main objectives of this thesis
•Measurement of the signal strength and improvement of the precision:
The signiﬁcance for the background-only hypothesis is also measured to evaluate signal
sensitivity. The properties of inclusive VH,H→b¯bprocess and the separated WH,H→b¯b
andZH,H→b¯bprocesses are measured. This measurement is motivated by the purpose
[A.].
•Measurement of ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section as functions of pV
Tand jet multiplicity

1.4.3 Analysis validation
The diboson processes of VZ(Z→b¯b)(V=W,ZandW→ℓν,Z→ℓℓ/νν), which are
the background processes in this analysis, have the similar topology as the Higgs signal pro-
cessesVH(H→b¯b), where only the di ﬀerence is the invariant mass of di-jet( b¯b):mZ=91 GeV ,
mH=125 GeV . Therefore, the diboson processes VZ(Z→b¯b) can be a substitute for the Higgs
signal processes in this analysis. In order to validate the whole analysis methods, especially the
estimations for the other background processes, the signal strength and signiﬁcance of the diboson
processesVZ(Z→b¯b) are measured before the measurements for the Higgs signal processes, by
supposing a hypothesis that the signal strengths of diboson processes are consistent with the SM
prediction.

1.5 My contributions in this thesis
I’m one of the leading and main analysers of the international analysis team for the
VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) analysis. I made leading progresses in the development study of the FSR
recovery (Chapter 7), which contributes to both of the analysis purpose [A.] and [B.], and almost
all modelling studies of the diboson background process (Chapter 10), which contributes to the
purpose [A.]. In addition, I contributed to the optimisation of the MV A input variables (Chapter
8) and the ﬁne-tuning study of the ﬁnal ﬁt (Chapter 11).
Besides the studies of Higgs boson properties in the VH(H→b¯b) analysis, I contributed to the
operation and the development of the A TLAS experiment. In the Run 3 operation (Section 2.1),
I was in charge of the experts for the TGC system and L1 Muon trigger system of the end-cap
region, and contributed to the stable A TLAS operation as described in Section 2.2.5. Moreover, I
achieved successful progress in the development study of a new electronics system of the A TLAS
experiment for HL-LHC, which is described in detail in Appendix A.
1.6 Thesis composition
This thesis summarises a study of the VH(H→b¯b) process, and concludes by discussing the
results of the measurements and discrepancies between experimental observations and the SM
predictions.
Chapter2describes the setup of the experiment in this analysis. Chapter 3describes the dataset
recorded in the experiment and the simulated samples used in this analysis. Chapter 4describes
the reconstruction, identiﬁcation and calibration of the objects: Electron, muon, tau, MET and jets.
Chapter5describes the event selection and event categorisation adopted in this analysis. Chapter 6
describes four kinds of jet energy corrections for the Higgs-candidate jets applied in this analysis.
Chapter7summarises studies of a Final State Radiation (FSR) jet emitted from the H→b¯b
decay for this analysis. Chapter 8describes the MV A used in this analysis. Chapter 9describes the
systematic uncertainties of this analysis including the experimental uncertainties and the modelling
uncertainties derived from the simulated samples. Chapter 10summarises modelling studies for
diboson background processes and the detailed modelling uncertainties of the diboson processes
applied in this analysis. Chapter 11describes the statistical treatment in the ﬁt between the data
and simulated samples in this analysis. Chapter 12presents the results and discussion, followed
by Chapter 13to conclude this thesis.

=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
20
Chapter 2
The LHC-ATLAS experiment
This chapter describes the setup of the experiment in this analysis. Section 2.1explains an acceler-
ator of protons, and Section 2.2explains a detector to record the data of proton-proton collisions.
2.1 The Large Hadron Collider (LHC)
The LHC [23] is a particle accelerator at CERN *1to study particle physics at high energy fron-
tier. The LHC ring lies in tunnel of 27 km in circumference.
The accelerator complex is shown in Figure 2.1. First of all, hydrogen atoms are ionised to
produce protons. These protons are injected into the linear accelerator LINAC2 and accelerated
up to 50 MeV . Then, proton bunches are formed and accelerated by several circular accelerators:
BOOSTER up to 1.4 GeV , Proton Synchrotron (PS) up to 26 GeV , and Super Proton Synchrotron
(SPS) up to 450 GeV . Finally, they are injected and accelerated in the LHC. There are four main
experiments in the LHC which are located at the collision points as shown in Figure 2.1: A Toroidal
LHC Apparatus (A TLAS) [ 19], Compact Muon Solenoid (CMS) [ 20], LHC beauty (LHCb), A
Large Ion Collider Experiment (ALICE). The A TLAS and CMS experiments mainly study proton-
proton (pp) collisions.
Figure2.2shows the schedule of LHC and the future plan: The LHC accelerator will be up-
graded to increase its instantaneous luminosity referred to as “High-Luminosity LHC (HL-LHC)”.
TheVH(H→b¯b) analysis presented in this thesis uses the dataset of full Run2 experiment (2015
- 2018).
*1EN:European Organization for Nuclear Research, FR:Organisation Europ ´eenne pour la Recherche Nucl ´eaire

2.2 The A TLAS detector
The A TLAS detector is a general purpose detector [ 19] to measure particles from ppcollisions
in the A TLAS experiment. The A TLAS detector covers almost 4 πsolid angle with symmetric

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-A TLAS experiment 23
cylindrical geometry around the collision point. The A TLAS detector consists of inner track-
ing detectors enclosed by a thin superconducting solenoid magnet, electromagnetic and hadronic
calorimeters, and muon spectrometers integrated with two kinds of large superconducting toroidal
magnets as shown in Figure 2.3. Every sub-detector is structured with detectors separately in the
barrel region and in the end-cap region. The region nearby the beam pipe is called as forward
region.
Fig. 2.3: Cut-away view of the A TLAS detector [ 38].
These sub-detector components are integrated in hierarchical structure as shown in Figure 2.4in
order to identify the type of particles from the IP . The inner tracking detectors detect charged parti-
cles, which are bent by the solenoid magnet, and reconstruct tracks of these particles, and measure
their momenta from the curvature. The details are described in Section 2.2.1. The electromag-
netic calorimeters detect mainly photons and electrons, and measure their energy and positions.
The hadronic calorimeters detect hadrons, and measure their energy and positions. The details of
both calorimeters are described in Section 2.2.2. The muon spectrometer detects charged particles
which penetrate all the internal detectors; in general, muons are detected. A muon is bent by the
solenoid and toroidal magnets. The muon spectrometer extrapolates a track of muon from the IP
and measures its momentum from the curvature. The details are described in Section 2.2.3. The
coverage and resolution of each sub-detector are summarised in Table 2.2. In addition, the trig-
ger and data acquisition system is summarised in Section 2.2.4, followed by the A TLAS control

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
24 2.2 The A TLAS detector
system in Section 2.2.5. All the setups described in this section are for the Run 2 experiment.
Fig. 2.4: Hierarchical structure of the A TLAS detector and particle visualisations in a projection
of x-y plane (Barrel side) [ 39].

The A TLAS experiment uses a right-handed coordinate system, whose origin is deﬁned at the
centre of the A TLAS detector. The beam direction deﬁnes the z-axis and the x-yplane is deﬁned
as transverse to the beam direction. The positive x-axis points to the centre of the LHC ring, and
the positive y-axis points vertically upward. The azimuthal angle φand the perpendicular length
from thez-axisRare deﬁned to measure around the z-axis of beam line. The pseudorapidity ηis
deﬁned as the high energy limit of the rapidity y=(1/2) ln[(E+pz)/(E−pz)], denoted by,

2.2.1 Inner tracking detector (ID)
The inner tracking detector (ID) consists of high-resolution semiconductor pixel and strip detec-
tors in the inner part and straw-tube tracking detectors, and is immersed in a 2 T solenoid magnetic
ﬁeld. The layout of these detectors is illustrated in Figure 2.5. This sub-detector system aims to
reconstruct tracks of charged particles.
Silicon Pixel detector (Pixel) and Insertable B-Layer (IBL) is the closest sub-detector system
to the IP . They consist of four cylindrical layers in the barrel region and three disks on each side of
the end-cap regions, where the IBL is implemented in the innermost layer and attached on the beam
pipe [40]. The pixel sensors have a size of 50 ×250µm2for the IBL and that of 50 ×400µm2for the
others. They are segmented in φ×zplane. The total number of readout channels is approximately
92 M.
SemiConductor Tracker (SCT) is a semiconductor strip detector and is located outside the
Pixels. The SCT is composed of four cylindrical layers in the barrel region and nine disks on each
side of the end-cap regions. The SCT consists of two 6.4 cm daisy-chained strip sensors with a
pitch of 80 µm. The total number of readout channels is approximately 6.3 M.
Transition Radiation Tracker (TRT) provides a large number of hits, which is typically 36 hits

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
26 2.2 The A TLAS detector
Fig. 2.5: Cut-away view of the inner tracking detector [ 38].
per track, with the 4 mm diameter straw tubes covered within |η|<2.0. The transition radiation
material is made of polypropylene. The TRT provides the hit positions in R×φ(z×φ) plane
in the barrel (end-cap) region with an accuracy of 130 µm per straw. The TRT consists of 73
straw planes in the barrel region and 80 straw planes on each side of the end-cap region. The total
number of readout channels is approximately 351 k. The detection of transition radiation photons
in a Xenon-based gas mixture of the straw tube enhances the electron identiﬁcation performance.
2.2.2 Calorimeter
The calorimeter is placed outside the ID and consists of an electromagnetic (EM) calorimeter
and a hadronic calorimeter. The EM calorimeter measures the energy of electrons and photons
using electromagnetic showers with ﬁne granularity, and the hadronic calorimeter measures the
energy of hadrons using hadronic showers with enough granularity to satisfy the physics require-
ments forEmiss
T measurement and jet reconstruction. The layout of all the calorimeters is illustrated
in Figure2.6.
Liquid Argon (LAr) ElectroMagnetic (EM) calorimeter is a sampling calorimeter with a

=== PDF PAGE 41 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-A TLAS experiment 27
Fig. 2.6: Cut-away view of the calorimeter [ 38].
lead absorber and liquid Argon (LAr) active material. This detector covers |η|<1.475 in the barrel
region, and 1 .375<|η|<3.2 in the end-cap region. The lead absorber induces an EM shower
when an electron or photon passes through the detector, and the energy of EM shower is measured
in the LAr detector. The number of ionised particles in the LAr detector is proportional to the total
energy of the EM shower. Total thickness is >22 radiation length (X 0)*3in the barrel region, and
>24X0in the end-cap region. This thickness limits the punch-through of the EM showers into the
hadronic calorimeter. In |η|<1.8, a presampler detector is installed to correct energy loss before
the calorimeter, and has a ﬁner granularity in the ηdirection to identify between a signal photon
and a di-photon pair decayed from π0. The total number of readout channels is approximately 110
k in the barrel region and 64 k in the end-cap region.
Tile hadronic barrel calorimeter (Tile) is the A TLAS hadronic calorimeter in the barrel region
covering|η|<1.7. This is placed outside the LAr EM calorimeter. This calorimeter is composed of
steel as the absorber and scintillating tiles as the active material and segmented with three layers.
The total thickness is approximately 9.7 interaction lengths λ*4atη=0. This thickness limits
*3A distance to reduce energy of an electron by 1 /e.
*4A distance to reduce the number of charged particles in a hadron shower by 1 /e.

=== PDF PAGE 42 ===
28 2.2 The A TLAS detector
the punch-through of the hadronic showers into the muon system. The total number of readout
channels is approximately 6 k for barrel detector and 4 k for extended barrel detector.
LAr Hadronic End-cap Calorimeter (HEC) is the A TLAS hadronic calorimeter in the end-
cap region covering 1 .5<|η|<3.2. This is placed outside the EM calorimeter sharing the same
LAr cryostats, and segmented into two independent wheels per end-cap side. This is a sampling
calorimeter with copper absorber plates for 25 mm and 50 mm, and with LAr active material in
8.5 mm gaps between the plates. The total thickness is approximately 9.7 λ[41]. The total number
of readout channels is approximately 5.6 k.
LAr EM/hadronic Forward Calorimeter (FCal) is placed on both sides of forward regions
covering 3.1<|η|<4.9. This consists of three modules per end-cap side: the innermost mod-
ule uses copper for electromagnetic measurement, the other two outer modules use tungsten for
measuring the energy of hadrons. The total thickness is approximately 10 λ. The total number of
readout channels is approximately 3.5 k.
2.2.3 Muon spectrometer
The muon detection system consists of trigger chambers and high-precision tracking chambers
incorporating with the large superconducting air-core toroidal magnets, which are illustrated in
Figure2.7. In |η|<1.4, a charged particle, mainly a muon, is bent in magnetic ﬁeld of 0.2
- 2.5 T imposed by the eight large barrel toroidal magnets in the muon spectrometer area. In
1.6<|η|<2.7, a muon is bent in magnetic ﬁeld of 0.2 - 3.5 T imposed by the eight small end-cap
toroidal magnets per end-cap side in the interval area between the inner muon spectrometer and
outer muon spectrometer. The combination of the barrel and end-cap toroidal magnets provides
magnetic reﬂection in 1 .4<|η|<1.6. This system measures the momentum of muons by the
magnetic reﬂection of muon tracks using the di ﬀerence from inﬁnite momentum tracks (straight
lines). The trigger chambers are used to trigger the interesting physics events which are described
in Section 2.2.4, thus, the coarse position granularity and ﬁne timing resolution are required for
their designs in order to detect a signal within proton bunch crossing space 25 ns [ 23]: Resistive
Plate Chambers (RPC) in the barrel region and Thin Gap Chambers (TGC) in the end-cap region.
The high-precision tracking chambers are used for precise muon trajectory reconstruction of the
triggered events: Monitored Drift Tube (MDT) in all the regions and Cathode Strip Chambers
(CSC) in the forward region.

=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-A TLAS experiment 29
Fig. 2.7: Cut-away view of the muon spectrometer [ 38].
Monitored Drift Tube (MDT) consists of six or eight layers of drift tubes covering |η|<2.7,
while the innermost layer covers |η|<2.0. The drift tube is 30 mm diameter tube ﬁlled with a gas
mixture of Argon and CO 2(93:7) at∼3 atmospheric pressure. The average position granularity
per tube is approximately 80 µm. The drift time is a maximum of 700 ns. The total number of
readout channels is approximately 354 k.
Cathode Strip Chambers (CSC) use multi-wire proportional chambers (MWPC) covering
2.0<|η|<2.7. The plane of CSC is ﬁlled with a gas mixture of Argon and CO 2(80:20). The
position granularity per plane is approximately 60 µm. The drift time is a maximum of 40 ns in a
timing resolution of ∼7 ns. The total number of readout channels is approximately 31 k.
Resistive Plate Chambers (RPC) consist of parallel electrode plates ﬁlled with gas mixture of
C2H2F4:Iso-C4H10:SF6(94.7:5:0.3) in a 2mm gas gap. The RPC system covers |η|<1.05 in the
barrel region. The readout pitch is 23 - 35 mm in the z-φposition. The position granularity is ∼10
mm in the zposition, and ∼10 mm in the φposition. The timing resolution is ∼1.5 ns. The total

=== PDF PAGE 44 ===
30 2.2 The A TLAS detector
number of readout channels is approximately 373 k.
Thin Gap Chambers (TGC) use MWPC ﬁlled with gas mixture of CO 2:n-pentan (55:45). The
TGC system covers 1 .05<|η|<2.7 (1.05<|η|<2.4 is used for trigger) in the end-cap region and
is placed at both the inner and outer sides of the end-cap toroidal magnet. The position granularity
is 2 - 6 mm in the Rposition, and 2 - 3 mrad in the φposition. The timing resolution is ∼4 ns. The
total number of readout channels is approximately 318 k.
2.2.4 T rigger and data acquisition
Theppbunch crossing rate is 40 MHz. The cross-section of targeted physics events, such as
top quarks, weak bosons, or Higgs bosons, is 6 - 8 times smaller than the total inelastic scattering
cross-section in the ppcollision. Therefore, a procedure system to select and record the interesting
physics events is required. This system is called as a trigger and data acquisition (TDAQ) system
[42]. The trigger system is composed of two phases: the First Level trigger (L1 trigger), and the
High Level Trigger (HLT). The overview of the A TLAS TDAQ system is illustrated in Figure 2.8.
L1 trigger is the ﬁrst stage of the A TLAS trigger system and is performed in a hardware based
electronics exploiting an FPGA or an ASIC for quick decision. The event rate is reduced from 40
MHz to 100 kHz in a ﬁxed latency of 2.5 µs. The L1 trigger makes a judgement with information
from the muon spectrometers (RPC, TGC) and from the calorimeters. In order to realise the quick
judgement, the L1 Muon trigger system calculates the muon pTby the position information in
coarse granularity. The L1 calorimeter trigger system uses a Cluster Processor (CP) for a lepton
and a photon, and uses a Jet /Energy-sum Processor (JEP) for a jet in parallel. At last, the Central
Trigger Processor (CTP) makes the ﬁnal L1 judgement and issues a L1 Accept (L1A) signal to all
sub-detectors.
High Level Trigger (HLT) is the next stage of the A TLAS trigger system exploiting software
resources. The event rate is reduced from 100 kHz to 1 kHz. The HLT can recover the objects,
which are abandoned in the L1 trigger, by using the information from the whole sub-detectors.
All information of the sub-detectors is ﬁrst bu ﬀered in derandomising memory in their Front-
End (FE) electronics in the A TLAS cavern. When the L1A signal is received, the accepted events
are sent to ReadOut System (ROS) in the counting room, which is placed about 100 m far away
from the cavern, by ReadOut Drivers (ROD) via optical links. After the HLT is performed, the data

=== PDF PAGE 45 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-A TLAS experiment 31
Fig. 2.8: Overview of the trigger and data acquisition system [ 42].
is stored in a permanent storage of CERN’s data centre at an average of 1.2 GBytes per second.
2.2.5 Control and operation
The infrastructure is established to operate the A TLAS detector system.
Timing Trigger Control (TTC) system distributes the L1A signal and LHC’s 40.079 MHz
global clock and reset signals to the whole sub-detectors in ﬁxed and synchronised clock phase. A
bunch crossing identiﬁcation (BCID) is performed on every event in the whole sub-detector system
using the LHC’s 40.079 MHz global clock.
Detector Control System (DCS) controls and monitors the detector parameters, for example,
Low-V oltage electric current, High-V oltage electric current, gas and cooling system, magnetic
ﬁeld, temperature, and humidity. The DCS also manipulates the communications between the
A TLAS sub-detectors and the other systems, such as the LHC accelerator, the CERN technical

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
32 2.2 The A TLAS detector
services, the A TLAS magnets, and the detector safety system.
Operation organisation for the ATLAS detector The A TLAS detector is mainly controlled
and monitored at A TLAS Control Room (ACR) 24 hours a day and every day through the operation
period by organising man-power with a shift schedule. When some problems occur in a sub-
detector system, the people in the ACR call to experts of the corresponding sub-detector system,
and solve the problems as soon as possible, so that the A TLAS detector prevents from losing the
collision events delivered by the LHC.

Data and simulated samples
The A TLAS data and the simulated samples are used for the estimation of background processes
and the prediction of event yields of the VH(H→b¯b) signal process. This chapter describes the
A TLAS data used in the VH(b¯b) analysis in Section 3.1, and the simulated samples in Section 3.2.
3.1 Data
Theppcollision data used in this thesis were collected by the A TLAS detector in 2015 - 2018 at
→
s=13 TeV . Cumulative integrated luminosity as a function of data taking period is presented in
Figure3.1. Total delivered luminosity from the LHC is 156 fb −1, in which 147 fb −1was recorded
by the A TLAS detector. The luminosity is mainly measured by LUminosity Cherenkov Integrating
Detector (LUCID-2) forward detector [ 43]. As physics analyses require the recorded data which
pass standard data quality requirements in the A TLAS experiment [ 44], the actual data used in the
A TLAS physics analyses have the integrated luminosity of 140 ±1.2 fb−1[36], which passed the
standard data quality selections. The integrated luminosity of the data for the physics analyses in
2015, 2016, 2017, and 2018 is 3.24, 33.40, 44.63, and 58.79 fb −1, respectively [ 36].
There are multiple soft ppcollisions in bunch crossings in addition to the targeting hard scatter-
ing. These extra interactions are called as “pile-up”. The mean number of interactions is di ﬀerent
in each bunch crossing ( ⟨µ⟩) according to the instantaneous luminosity *1. Figure3.2shows distri-
butions of⟨µ⟩.
*1The number of ⟨µ⟩was calculated by the mean value of µ=Lbunch×σinel./fr[44] in each bunch and in a certain
period, where Lbunchis luminosity per proton bunch, σinel.is the inelastic cross-section (80 mb for 13 TeV pp
collisions), and fris the LHC revolution frequency.

3.2 Simulated samples
The physics events in the ppcollisions detected in the A TLAS detector are simulated, and that
simulated events are used to describe the A TLAS data.
3.2.1 Simulation of physics events in the A TLAS experiment
The simulation of the physics events in the A TLAS experiment, generally, consists of the fol-
lowing two simulations: the simulation of physics processes in the ppcollision and the pile-up
exploiting Monte Carlo (MC) technique [ 46], and simulation of hits and readout in the A TLAS
detector. Every simulation and calculation is described in detail in this section. The deliverable,
i.e. simulated sample, is also called as “MC sample”.
Simulation of physics process
Since complex physics phenomena have occurred in the ppcollision, the simulation of the
physics processes in the ppcollision is performed with the following event generation steps. Fig-
ure3.3illustrates the event generation step of t¯thevent as an example. A proton is considered as a
composite particle with partons (quarks and gluons) in high energy collisions. In the collision of
protons, partons from the proton interact with partons from the other proton. Among the parton in-
teractions, a hard scattering interaction induces the targeting physics process. The hard scattering
process can be predicted by factorising the computation into two parts: the perturbatively calcu-
lable part, corresponding to the Matrix Element (ME) using Parton Distribution Function (PDF),
and the perturbatively non-calculable part, corresponding to the Parton Shower (PS) and hadro-
nisation (fragmentation). The separation of two parts is parameterised with a factorisation scale
(µF). The PS and hadronisation are estimated by phenomenological models with parameters tuned
to match observed data. Initial State Radiation (ISR) is radiated from the hard scattering partons,
and Final State Radiation (FSR) is radiated from the decay process of the hard scattering event.
There are interactions with other partons from the same colliding protons as the hard scattering
partons, referred to as “Underlying Event (UE)”, which is modelled using data. The computations
of complex event generation are performed by several MC generators.
Calculation of cross-section
The number of events can be derived from cross-section and integrated luminosity, which is
given as
N=σ
∫
Linst.dt, (3.1)

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===
36 3.2 Simulated samples
Fig. 3.3: Illustration of event generation step of t¯thevent in the ppcollision [47].h1,h2denote the
colliding hadrons (protons). Blue lines represent partons from the colliding hadrons. Initial State
Radiation (ISR) is also shown, which is associated with the hard scattering parton. A noticeable
red blob represents the hard scattering interaction to produce the t¯thprocess, and red lines and
blobs illustrate the decay of the top quarks and the Higgs boson. Final State Radiation (FSR) is
also shown in the hard scattering process, which is radiated from the decay process. A noticeable
purple blob denotes the interaction with other partons from the same colliding hadrons as the hard
scattering partons, which is referred to as “Underlying Event (UE)”. Small light green blobs and
dark green marks illustrate the hadronisation. Y ellow lines represent the radiation of photons.
whereLinst.represents instantaneous luminosity deﬁned in Eq. ( 2.1). The details of integrated
luminosity used in this thesis are described in Section 3.1. In the calculation of cross-section ( σ),
a physics process in the ppcollision as discussed above is considered.
Parton Distribution Function (PDF) describes the distribution of momentum carried by a parton
contributing to collisions; deﬁned as a probability fi(xi,µF), wherexiis a momentum fraction of
partoniover proton momentum PandµFis a factorization scale. The factorisation scale ( µF)
separates the perturbatively calculable part and non-calculable part [ 48]. The energy scale is pa-

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 37
rameterised by the µF. The PDF is determined by experimental data of deep-inelastic scattering
(DIS) and related hard scattering data [ 49]. Thus, the cross-section in the proton-proton collision
for a certain process pp→Xcan be written as
σpp→X=
∑
i,j
∫1
0
dxi
∫1
0
dxjfi(xi,µF)fj(xj,µF)σij→X(xiP,xjP,µR,µF), (3.2)
whereσijis the cross-section of hard scattering process with initial state of partons iandj.
The cross-section of σij→Xis calculated mainly by QCD; the precision of calculation is tiered
as Leading Order (LO), Next to Leading Order (NLO), and Next-to-Next Leading Order (NNLO)
in ascending order. Two kinds of QCD scale variations should be considered; factorisation scale
(µF) and renormalisation scale ( µR). The strong coupling constant in the ME is subject to quantum
corrections, such as corrections to the quark and gluon self-energies. The renormalisation scale
(µR) removes inﬁnities in the quantum correction. The value of µRvaries with energy scale. These
QCD scale variations would be one of the major modelling uncertainties in general.
As shown in Figure 3.4, the proton-proton cross-sections as a function of the centre of mass
energy (parameterised by µF,µRin Eq. (3.2)).
Fig. 3.4: Cross-section of the Higgs production modes as a function of the centre of mass energy
at the LHC [ 26]. The cross-section is calculated with the Higgs mass as 125 GeV . As the energy
increases, the cross-section also increases.
Simulation of pile-up

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
38 3.2 Simulated samples
All the simulated samples include the e ﬀect of pile-up from multiple interactions in the same
bunch crossing as well as the contributions from neighbouring crossings. This is achieved by
overlaying minimum bias events, simulated using P ythia8, using A3 tune and interfaced with
NNPDF 2.3 PDFs. In addition, all events of the simulated samples are reweighted to match these
MC samples to the data in terms of the ⟨µ⟩in Figure3.2[50,51].
Simulation of hits in the ATLAS detector
Afterwards, the simulation of hits in the A TLAS detector is performed. Both signal and back-
ground simulated processes are passed through the A TLAS detector simulation [ 52], and digitised
to emulate hits and readout on every sub-detector, based on Geant4[53]. After that, the objects
such as leptons, photons, and jets are reconstructed with the A TLAS o ﬄine reconstruction software
in the identical way to reconstruct the data hits. The “truth” information of simulated processes *2
is also stored in the MC samples so that it can be used in the physics analysis.
3.2.2 Overall set-up of simulated samples
Most of the signal and background processes are simulated using MC samples, except for the
multi-jet background in the 1-lepton channel, and the top-quark background process in the 2-lepton
channel of the resolved VH(H→b¯b) analysis. These exceptional background processes are esti-
mated using data-driven techniques. In order to verify the theoretical modelling of the simulated
samples, besides a nominal MC sample serves as a baseline of a physics process simulation, alter-
native MC samples derived by alternative calculation methods are also prepared for each process,
which is described in detail in Section 9.2.1. This chapter shows only the nominal samples of
all the processes. Table 3.1lists the matrix element generators, parton showers generators, and
parton distribution function (PDF) releases used for the nominal samples of simulated events. The
cross-sections corresponding to each process are also shown. The cross-sections are computed
from the best theoretical predictions, except for the diboson process of which the cross-section
is derived from the nominal MC generator. The simulated samples, except those generated with
Sherpa, exploit the E vtGen1.6.0 program [ 54] to describe properties of the bottom and charm
hadron decays.
The detailed conﬁgurations of simulated samples for the signal and background processes are
in Section3.2.3and Section 3.2.4respectively.

3.2.3 Signal process
Allqq-initiated production processes are simulated using the P owheggenerator with the MiNLO
(Multiscale Improved Next-to-Leading Order) procedure applied [ 55], interfaced to the P ythiaMC
generator (Pythia8.245 for the quark-induced processes, P ythia8.307 for the gluon-induced pro-
cesses) [56] for the modelling of parton shower (PS), underlying event (UE) and multiple parton
interactions (MPI), applying the AZNLO tune [ 57] with the NNPDF3.0NLO PDF [ 58] for the
matrix elements (ME), and then reweighted to the PDF4LHC15NLO PDF set. The gg-initiated
ZHcontribution relies on the P owhegprediction interfaced with P ythia8 at Leading Order (LO).
Additional electroweak (EW) corrections at NLO are applied to the qq→VHprocesses as a
function of the transverse momentum of the vector boson [ 59]. These corrections are applied as

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
40 3.2 Simulated samples
reweightings of the event yields, and they are computed with Hawk[60,61].
The signal cross-sections are obtained from the LHC Higgs Working Group prescriptions for
a Higgs boson mass of mH=125 GeV [26]. The inclusive cross-sections for WHandZHare
calculated at Next-to-Next-to-Leading Order (NNLO) in QCD [ 62] and Next-to-Leading Order
(NLO) in EW [ 59]. The computation of the one-loop amplitudes is performed with the GoSam
automated software [ 63].
The contribution of the signal process in analysis regions is described in Section 9.2.2, and the
signal prediction is explained in Section 11.1.
3.2.4 Background process
There are ﬁve kinds of background processes in this thesis: vector boson +jets (V+jets) pro-
duction, top-pair production, single top production, diboson production, and multi-jet production.
Their contributions in analysis regions and background estimations are described in Section 9.2.3.
3.2.4.1 V+jets
The production of a single vector (V =W or Z) boson in association with jets is one of the main
backgrounds for all the lepton channels considered in this analysis. The Feynman diagrams of the
V+jets process are illustrated in Figure 3.5. The V+jets processes are simulated with S herpa2.2.11
[64]. In Sherpa2.2.11, NLO-accurate Matrix Element (ME) for up to two jets and LO-accurate
ME for between three and ﬁve jets are calculated in the ﬁve-ﬂavour scheme using the Comix [ 65]
and OpenLoops[66,67,68] libraries. The b- andc-quarks are treated as massless at the ME
level and massive in the Parton Shower (PS). The default S herpaPS [69] and hadronisation based
on Catani–Seymour dipole factorisation and cluster-hadronisation model [ 70] is used. The built-
in Hessian NNPDF3.0NNLO PDF set [ 58] is used. The default Underlying Event (UE) tune of
Sherpais used. The samples are generated using a dedicated set of tuned parameters developed by
the Sherpaauthors. The NLO matrix elements for a given jet multiplicity are matched to the PS
using a variant of the MC@NLO algorithm [ 71]. Diﬀerent jet multiplicities are then merged into
an inclusive sample using an improved CKKW matching procedure [ 72,73], which is extended to
NLO accuracy using the MEPS@NLO prescription [ 74]. The merging cut parameter Qcut, which
is a criterion to allow jets initiated from the ME partons when Q>Qcut, is set to 20 GeV . In
addition, ﬂavour ﬁltering is used in the sample generation to ensure su ﬃcient statistics for events
withb-,c-jets. The cross-section is calculated at NNLO QCD accuracy [ 75].
In addition, virtual loop NLO electroweak (EW) corrections are available for S herpa2.2.11,
with three di ﬀerent NLO QCD and NLO EW combination schemes [ 76,77]. The additive

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 41
scheme shows the smallest deviation from NLO QCD-only prediction and is thus chosen as the
Sherpa2.2.11 EW-corrected new nominal. The multiplicative and exponentiated schemes have
larger deviations and are used as sources of modelling uncertainties.
Fig. 3.5: Feynman diagram of V +jets processes. (a) shows the W +jets process and (b) shows the
Z+jets process.
3.2.4.2 T op-pair
The nominal MC sample for the t¯tprocess is generated with the P owheggenerator using an NLO
calculation for the ME [ 78,79] and interfaced to P ythia8.230 [80] with NNPDF3.0NLO PDFs
and the A14 set [ 81] of parameters for the PS, hadronisation, and UE description. In addition,
ﬁltering at the generator level is applied during production to enhance statistics. The top-pair
cross-sections are computed assuming a top quark mass of 172.5 GeV [ 1] at NLO+NLL(the next-
to-leading logarithmic) accuracy. The Feynman diagrams of the top-pair process are illustrated in
Figure3.6.
Fig. 3.6: Feynman diagram of top-pair process.
In the 2-lepton channel, a data-driven method is used to estimate the total top-quark background
(t¯t+single-top), since the top-quark dominant (top control) region with enough statistics can be

=== PDF PAGE 56 ===
42 3.2 Simulated samples
found when eandµlepton pair events are selected and the other event selections in the signal
sensitive region in the 2-lepton (Section 5.1) are applied. More details about the 2-lepton top
control region are available in Section 5.2.
3.2.4.3 Single-top
Single-top processes are generated for the di ﬀerent channels (s-channel, t-channel, Wt-channel)
using the P owhegMC generator for the hard scattering prediction with NNPDF3.0NLO PDFs,
interfaced with P ythia8.230 using the A14 tune for the simulation of PS, UE and MPI. The Feyn-
man diagrams of the single-top processes are illustrated in Figure 3.7. The cross-sections for s
andtchannel production are calculated with the MCFM program [ 82] at NNLO in QCD using a
top-quark mass of 172.5 GeV [ 1]. The ﬁducial Wtchannel production cross-section is calculated
with approximate NNLO accuracy from NNLL soft-gluon resummation [ 83,84]. In the case of
Wtproduction, some overlap with t¯tproduction occurs. At NLO in QCD, these processes have
overlapping diagrams, and it is no longer possible to keep them formally apart. The diagram sub-
traction (DS) scheme [ 85] is used to handle the overlap with t¯tpair production by removing doubly
resonant diagrams from the Wtsample, i.e. where both top quarks are on-shell. A comparison with
the alternative diagram removal (DR) scheme is used to assess systematic uncertainties. The DS
scheme showed better agreement of MC with the data in the boosted VH(H→b¯b) regime and is
thus used as the nominal sample.
Fig. 3.7: Feynman diagram of single-top processes. (a) shows the s-channel process, (b) shows the
t-channel process, and (c) shows the Wt-channel process.
3.2.4.4 Diboson
The diboson background consists of three production processes: WW, WZ, and ZZ events. The
nominal simulation of these processes relies on the S herpa2.2.11 MC generator for the qq-initiated
and on Sherpa2.2.2 for the gg-initiated processes. In both cases, the NNPDF3.0NNLO PDF set
is used for both the ME calculation and the PS. The default UE tune of S herpais used. For the

=== PDF PAGE 57 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 43
virtual loop NLO EW corrections, the additive scheme is adopted as a new nominal sample. The
generator used for the nominal samples provides the diboson cross-sections at the NLO accuracy
in QCD. The Feynman diagrams of the diboson processes are illustrated in Figure 3.8. The details
of the diboson modelling study are summarised in Chapter 10.
Fig. 3.8: Feynman diagram of diboson process. Only the qq-initiated processes are shown.
3.2.4.5 Multi-jet
Multi-jet events can contribute to the background due to muons or electrons from heavy-ﬂavour
hadron decays, or jets faking leptons. A small fraction passes the lepton isolation requirements, but
the production cross-section is large *3. Thus, the contribution of the multi-jet should be considered.
Using MC simulations to achieve good modelling of this background is very di ﬃcult, mostly
because the fraction of events that pass the selection criteria is very limited, and the available
simulations do not ensure enough statistics in this phase space. Therefore, data-driven template
methods are used to extract the multi-jet contributions as discussed in Section 9.2.3. The multi-jet
templates are only considered in 1-lepton in the resolved VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) regimes,
since their contributions are found to be negligible in the other regions.
*3It’s almost eight orders of magnitude larger than the Higgs boson cross-section.

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===
44
Chapter 4
Objects reconstruction and identiﬁ-
cation
This analysis uses the reconstructed objects: charged tracks, primary vertex, electrons, muons,
missing transverse momentum and jets. This chapter describes the reconstruction and identiﬁca-
tion of these objects in Section 4.1-4.3and4.5, and the identiﬁcation of b-,c-jets in Section 4.4.
Overlap removals between di ﬀerent objects are described in Section 4.6.
4.1 T rack and primary vertex
A charged particle passes through and deposits hits in the inner tracking detectors. A cluster
is reconstructed with the bunch of hits in the pixel, and a tracklet is made by the hits in the SCT.
The cluster and the tracklet are ﬁtted and assembled to a charged track. After that, the charged
track is extrapolated to the hits in the TRT in another ﬁt. In the procedure of ﬁtting the track, bad
reconstructed tracks are rejected [ 86]. The tracks reconstructed with inner tracking detectors are
referred to as ID tracks.
Interaction vertices are reconstructed with at least two of these tracks [ 87], called as a “primary
vertex” (PV). A PV with the highest sum of transverse momentum of its tracks is selected as the
PV corresponding to the hard scattering event, among ∼60 PVs in the same ppcollision event.
Electrons, muons, and jets should be originated from the selected PV to suppress the background
objects from pile-up collisions. Relative position between a track and the selected PV is deﬁned
as follows:
•d0: minimum distance of a track to the selected PV in the x-y plane
•z0: minimum distance of a track to the selected PV along the z-axis

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identiﬁcation 45
The value of d0is evaluated by the standard deviation of the d0, referred to as ” d0/σd0”. Thez0is
usually evaluated by |z0sinθ|in order to make this variable independent of the polar angle.
4.2 Leptons
Electrons are reconstructed and selected based on requirements for the properties of ID tracks
and the energy deposit matched to the corresponding track in the EM calorimeters. The selection
criteria for electrons are deﬁned in Tables 4.1, which diﬀer depending on the requirements of the
analysis lepton channels. After the reconstruction, prompt electrons from hard scattering vertex
are identiﬁed and isolated from electrons from semi-leptonic decays of hadronic sources or jet-
faking electrons (“non-prompt electrons”). The candidate electrons are identiﬁed and isolated
with likelihood discrimination, of which this analysis adopts “Loose quality” or “Tight quality”
identiﬁcation (ID) working points, and “Loose quality” or a tighter quality of “HighPtCaloOnly” *1
isolation working points [ 88]. After the identiﬁcation and isolation, the e ﬃciency of candidate
electrons is scaled to match the data in the calibration with the Z→eeandJ/ψ→eeevents
[88]. The scale factors are calculated from the ratio of the e ﬃciency measured between the MC
simulation and the data. Di ﬀerent selection levels are required depending on the situation in the
0-, 1-, 2-lepton channels. In VH-Loose level, the criteria are deﬁned to keep as many electrons
from the signal process as possible. The “Loose” isolation is applied to reduce jet-faking electrons,
and that working point keep 99 % e ﬃciency for prompt electrons from hard scattering vertex [ 88].
In ZH-Signal level for the 2-lepton channel, tighter criteria for the lepton pTis applied. In WH-
Signal level for the 1-lepton channel, a much tighter selection is applied to suppress the multi-jet
background. The tighter calorimeter-based isolation is applied with 95 % signal e ﬃciency [88].
Table 4.1: Electron selection requirements

ZH-Signal >27 GeV |η|<2.47 Loose quality <5 <0.5 mm Loose quality
WH-Signal >27 GeV |η|<2.47 Tight quality <5 <0.5 mm HighPtCaloOnly
Muons are reconstructed and selected with the information from the inner tracking detectors
and the muon spectrometers. The selection criteria for muons are deﬁned in Table 4.2, which
diﬀer depending on the requirements of the analysis lepton channels. After the reconstruction,
*1This electron isolation uses only the variables of EM calorimeters

46 4.3 Jets
prompt muons from hard scattering vertex are identiﬁed and isolated from muons from semi-
leptonic decays of hadronic sources or jet-faking muons (“non-prompt muons”). The candidate
muons are identiﬁed and isolated with likelihood discrimination, of which this analysis adopts
“Loose quality” or “Medium quality” identiﬁcation (ID) working points, and “Loose quality” or
a tighter quality of “HighPtTrackOnly” *2isolation working points [ 89]. After the identiﬁcation
and isolation, the e ﬃciency of candidate muons is scaled to match the data in the calibration with
theZ→µµandJ/ψ→µµevents [90,89]. The scale factors are calculated from the ratio
of the eﬃciency measured between the MC simulation and the data. Di ﬀerent selection levels
are required depending on the situation in the 0-, 1-, 2-lepton channels. In VH-Loose level, the
criteria are deﬁned to keep as many muons from the signal process as possible, and to reject pile-
up events and cosmic muons. The “Loose quality” of ID working point selects a default muon,
and a “CalTagged” muon. The CalTagged muon is reconstructed from an ID track with an energy
deposit in the calorimeter as a minimum ionising particle in |η|<0.1 where is a gap of the muon
spectrometers [ 89]. The “Loose quality” of the muon isolation reduces non-prompt muons, then
the isolation realises 99 % e ﬃciency for the signal muons [ 89]. In ZH-Signal level, the same
tighter criterion is added as the electrons above. In WH-Signal level for the 1-lepton channel, a
much tighter selection is applied to suppress the multi-jet background. The CalTagged muon is not
included in the Medium quality muon ID working point. A tighter track based isolation is applied
with 95 % signal e ﬃciency [89].

4.3 Jets
A hadron containing quarks and gluons is observable, while a quark or gluon itself is unable to
be observed as a single particle because of the conﬁnement e ﬀect of QCD. In the fragmentation
process, a bunch of hadrons can be observed, and it inherits the kinematic information of a quark
or gluon. Then, the bunch of hadrons is reconstructed as a cluster in the calorimeter and as tracks
*2This muon isolation uses only the variables of the inner tracking detectors

=== PDF PAGE 61 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identiﬁcation 47
in the ID detectors. This object is called “jet”.
Three diﬀerent jets are used in this analysis: small- Rjet for the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c)
regimes, large- Rjet and V ariable-R(VR) track jet for the boosted VH(b¯b) regime.
Small-Rjet
Small-Rjets are reconstructed from a particle-ﬂow (PFlow) algorithm [ 91], which combines
energy deposits in the calorimeter and tracks in the inner tracking detector. The PFlow objects are
clustered as a jet exploiting anti- ktalgorithm [92] with a distance parameter of R=0.4. The anti- kt
algorithm forms clusters of soft (low momentum) constituents ﬁrst to detach the soft constituents
from hard constituents, which helps to assess soft jets such as Initial State Radiation (ISR) jets.
Once the jets are reconstructed, the jets need to be adjusted according to several e ﬀects: detector
eﬀects such as the di ﬀerence between the EM and hadronic calorimeters, the energy leakage of the
jet in the calorimeter, and reconstruction e ﬀects such as energy deposited outside the jet cone, and
the jets from pileup events. Jets are adjusted by applying a jet energy scale (JES) [ 93,94]. The
derivation and calibration of JES are conducted in the following steps:
1.PV correction: The jet axis is corrected to point toward the selected PV in the corre-
sponding event, while it originally points to the detector centre. The four-vector of jet is
recalculated. The jet energy is not changed.
2.Pile-up correction: The energy deposits by the pile-up contribution are subtracted from
the measured energy deposits in the calorimeters. The corresponding energy to be sub-
tracted is calculated by the transverse energy density, which is measured in the pile-up
events beforehand.
3.MC-based calibration: The JES is also derived from the jet energy response in MC-
based calibration. The jet energy response Ris computed by comparing the reconstructed
jet energy Ereco.and the “truth” jet energy Etruthin MC simulation of di-jet events, given
asR=Ereco./Etruth, as functions of jet energy and jet η. Here, the “truth” jet is formed
with “truth” particles in the MC simulation by the PFlow algorithm, and the “truth” jets are
geometrically matched to the corresponding reconstructed jet within ∆R=0.3.
4.Global Sequential Calibration (GSC): The jet energy response varies depending on a
jet-initiating particle, e.g. the ﬂavour of quark or gluon. To distinguish the calibration of
the jet energy response between them, additional kinematic variables that can characterise
these jets are used, such as the fraction of the measured jet pTfrom the tracks associated
with the jet.
5.“In-situ” calibration: The ﬁnal calibration is applied to the data to account for the

=== PDF PAGE 62 ===
48 4.3 Jets
possible diﬀerences between the reconstructed jets in the data and the “truth” jets in the
MC samples, referred to as “in-situ” calibration. These di ﬀerences are caused by imperfect
simulations of the detector and the physics processes. The pTratio is measured between jets
and well-calibrated reference objects, e.g. Zbosons, photons, and low pTjets, in both the
data and MC samples, to extract the correction as functions of jet pTandη. This calibration
consists of three steps. Firstly, the energy of jets in the forward region (0 .8<|η|<4.5)
is corrected based on the well-measured jets in the central region ( |η|<1.4) using di-jet
events, referred to as “ ηinter-calibration”. Secondly, by balancing the pTof jets and the
reference objects, the measurements of Z+jets (Z→ℓℓ) andγ+jets events provide the
calibration in |η|<0.8, in 20<pjet
T <400 GeV and in 40 <pjet
T <950 GeV , respectively.
Finally, a single high- pTjet is calibrated by using a pTbalance with the referenced low- pT
jet in multi-jet events with high- pTjets up to 2.4 TeV .
Moreover, following the JES calibration, the jet energy resolution (JER) σpT/pTis calibrated by
the “in-situ” technique with the data and di-jet simulated samples [ 95]. In addition to the standard
jet calibrations, several jet energy corrections are applied to improve the invariant mass of two
b-,c-jets, exploiting the topological features in the H→b¯b/c¯cdecay processes, as described in
Chapter6.
Jets are classiﬁed as “signal” or “forward” jets with the selection requirements in Table 4.3.
As the tracking information is necessary for ﬂavour tagging, as described in Section 4.4, only the
signal jets are used in the Higgs boson reconstruction. Both the signal and forward jets are used in
the counting of jet multiplicity for event categorisation in Section 5.2.
In order to suppress jets from pileup events, the information of the selected hardest primary
vertex, jetpT, and track pTis used with a multivariate likelihood, which is called Jet V ertex Tagger
(JVT) [96]. The JVT is applied for jets with |η|<2.5 andpT<60 GeV . The JVT achieves ∼90 %
eﬃciency with ∼1 % fake rate of pileup events. Because of the lack of tracking information, for
the forward jet with pT<120 GeV , a forward Jet V ertex Tagger (fJVT) [ 97] is applied.
Jets have a possibility to come from calorimeter noise or from beam-induced background. These
jets can be suppressed by cut-based selection in Jet Cleaning [ 98] method: “Tight” quality realises
rejection of non-collision background with an e ﬃciency of selecting jets from proton-proton col-
lisions above 95 % (99.5 %) for pT>20 (100) GeV .
Unless there is an explicit statement about the term “jet”, a “jet” refers to a Small- Rjet.
Large-Rjet

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identiﬁcation 49
Large-Rjets are formed with clusters detected in the calorimeter exploiting the anti- ktalgorithm
with a radius parameter of R=1.0.
The large-Rjets pass through several simulation-based calibration algorithms [ 99]. First, the
Jet Energy Scale (JES) is corrected to the particle-level scale using matched truth-jet collections.
Afterwards, any o ﬀset inηis corrected in the same way. Both corrections are applied as a function
of the reconstructed jet energy and η. Since the jet mass is especially sensitive to wide-angled
soft radiation rather than the total jet energy, the Jet Mass Scale (JMS) is explicitly calibrated
in a last step using simulated di-jet events [ 99]. For further validation and estimation on the
uncertainties associated with the calibration procedure, JES and JMS are also calibrated with data
by the “in-situ” technique. This “in-situ” calibration results in better agreement between the data
and simulated samples. Jet Energy Resolution (JER) and Jet Mass Resolution (JMR) are also
calibrated by the “in-situ” technique in di-jet events.
The Large-Rjets are used in the reconstruction of the Higgs boson in the boosted VH(H→b¯b)
regime.
Variable-R(VR) track jet
Tracks from the inner tracking detector with pT>0.5 GeV and |η|<2.5 form the inputs to
VR track jet clusters. Then, the VR track jets are formed by clustering tracks with the anti- kt
algorithm. In an iterative reconstruction algorithm, a variable Ras a function of jet pTis deﬁned
asR(pT)=ρ/pT, whereρis a parameter to decide the e ﬀective jet size according to the track
VR jetpT[100].R(pT) has an upper limit ( Rmax) and lower limit ( Rmin). The VR track jets are
reconstructed with parameters {ρ=30GeV;Rmax=0.4;Rmin=0.02}, which have been optimized
forb-tagging eﬃciency in the boosted VH(H→b¯b) regime [101]. The selection requirement of
the VR track jet is shown in Table 4.4.
The VR track jets are used in MV A ﬁnal discrimination, ﬂavour tagging of jets, and the event
categorisation to set a top enriched control region in the boosted VH(H→b¯b) regime. Since the
momentum of VR track jet is not used in Higgs boson reconstruction, no additional uncertainty of
their momentum nor energy is considered in this analysis. The uncertainties of ﬂavour tagging are
expected to cover most e ﬀects of the uncertainties from the track jet reconstruction.
4.4 Flavour tagging of jets
There are the borcquark initiated jets ( b-,c-jets) from the Higgs boson in the signal processes,
meanwhile there are also jets initiated from the other quarks or gluons. The quark ﬂavour identiﬁ-

=== PDF PAGE 64 ===
50 4.4 Flavour tagging of jets

cation of jets plays an important role in collecting the signal events.
4.4.1 Identiﬁcation of several jets
Theb- andc-jets have a unique feature to have a secondary vertex (SV) and an impact parameter
(d0) to show the distance of the hard scattering primary vertex (PV) and the extrapolated point
from the SV . The transverse distance of the hard scattering PV and SV varies ﬂavour-by-ﬂavour
according to their lifetime: a b-quark has a relatively long lifetime for 1.5 ps (ﬂying 5 mm in the
ID), ac-quark (forming D-meson) has a short lifetime for 0.2 - 1.0 ps. Especially, since ∼90 % of
b-quark decays into c-quark due to the CKM matrix |Vcb|2≫|Vub|2,b-jet contains a c-hadron at a
few mm far from the SV , which results in that the b-jet has multiple vertices. Figure 4.1illustrates
these unique features.
The identiﬁcation of b- orc-jets is performed based on the associated tracks reconstructed in
the ID and primary vertex reconstruction. The ﬁnal ﬂavour discriminant is based on the DL1 algo-
rithms, a deep feed-forward neural network, using low-level tagger information including impact-
parameter based algorithms (IP2D /IP3D), secondary vertex SV1, multi-vertex decay-chain ﬁtter
(JetFitter), recurrent neural network track-based tagger (RNNIP) as well as the jet kinematic in-

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identiﬁcation 51
Fig. 4.1: Several unique features of the b-,c-jets. An impact parameter ( d0) shows the distance of
the hard scattering PV and the extrapolated point from the SV .
formation as the input variables, referred to as ”DL1r” tagger [ 102,103]. In the previous analysis
[12,31], the ﬁnal ﬂavour discriminant is performed with multi-variant analysis with the same input
variables, referred to as “MV2c10”. Figure 4.2shows the c-jet and light-ﬂavour jet rejection as
a function of the b-tagging eﬃciency. Comparing the background rejection performance at 70 %
b-tagging eﬃciency between the previous MV2c10 tagger and the current DL1r tagger, especially
thec-jet rejection improved ∼200 %.
In the simulation samples, the jet truth ﬂavour is labelled using a match to weakly decaying b/c-
hadron with pT≥5 GeV within a cone of size ∆R=0.3. Once a single b-hadron is assigned to a
jet, it is excluded from the list for further associations. Thus, overlap tagging on one reconstructed
jet won’t happen. If the jet cannot be labelled as neither bnorc, it will be labelled as a light jet.
Theb-,c-jets are referred to as “heavy ﬂavour” jets, and the jets originated from other quarks
such as strange quark are referred to as “light ﬂavour” jets.
4.4.2 T agging scheme
Because thec-tagging performance is not good enough to apply in the boosted regime ( pV
T>400
GeV), application schemes di ﬀer between the resolved (resolved VH(b¯b),VH(c¯c)) regimes, and the
boosted (VH(b¯b)) regimes.
