56 4.4 Flavour tagging of jets
4.4.4 Maximising the statistics after ﬂavour tagging selection
In a certain ﬂavour tagging working point, the corresponding ﬂavour tagged events are directly
selected, while the other ﬂavour tagged events are not used. Because of this strong suppression of
the other ﬂavour tagged event, the statistical uncertainty of the applicable events becomes large,
which is problematic in the training of the ﬁnal discriminant in Chapter 8and of the neural network
based shape uncertainty estimation in Section 9.2.1, and in the analysis phase spaces with low
statistics such as c-tagged, non-tagged bins, or the boosted VH(b¯b) regime.
In order to increase the statistics of available MC samples, a dedicated method is introduced,
referred to as “Truth tagging”. In the method, the tagging requirement is not applied to the MC
samples. Instead of the requirement, events are weighted by the probabilities ( ϵtag·ϵnon−tag) of every
jet as given in ( 4.3). The tagging e ﬃciencies (ϵ) in the probabilities are derived by a Graphical
Neural Network (GNN) training approach [ 106] with the input training variables, denoted as x
(e.g. 4-momentum tracks information of jets, secondary vertices of jets). The tagging e ﬃciencies
are also parameterised as a function of a set of variables θ(e.g.pTandηof the jets).
w=
C∑
i
ϵtag(Ti,x,θ)·ϵnon−tag(¯Ti,x,θ) (4.3)
where,
ϵtag(Ti,x,θ)=
∏
j′Ti
ϵj(x|θ) (4.4)
ϵnon−tag(¯Ti,x,θ)=
∏
j′¯Ti
(1−ϵj(x|θ)) (4.5)
Here,jdenotes one of the reconstructed jets in the event. The set of tagged jets can be deﬁned as
Ti, while the set of non-tagged jets can be deﬁned as ¯Ti.Cstands for all kinds of jet ﬂavours. The
training models are prepared for each signal or background process separately to catch the topo-
logical features of the process. For validation, the distributions of MV A input variables and ﬁnal
discriminant score is used. The distributions applying the tagging requirement and that applying
the Truth tagging method are almost consistent within their statistical uncertainties.
In the measurement of the signal strength and di ﬀerential cross-section, the Truth tagging is
applied to the V +jets,s-,t-channel of single top quark samples in inclusive regime, and to the t¯t
and Wt single top quark samples in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime. The Truth tagging is not applied
to signal samples and diboson samples, whose signal strengths are measured, so that the Truth
tagging doesn’t give some bias on the ﬁnal results.

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identiﬁcation 57
4.5 Missing T ransverse Momentum (MET)
A missing transverse momentum (MET, Emiss
T ) plays a key role in the 0-lepton channel and the
1-lepton channel in this analysis, since a Z boson decays to two neutrinos in 0-lepton and a W
boson decays to one lepton and one neutrino.
The MET is reconstructed as the negative vector sum of the transverse momenta of the recon-
structed objects: electrons, muons, τhads, jets, and a “soft-term” [ 107]. The “soft-term” is derived
as the vector sum of the pTof well-qualiﬁed ID tracks which are not associated with a lepton nor
jet. Since the well-qualiﬁed ID tracks are associated with the PV , the “soft-term” is less sensitive
to the eﬀects of pileup events.
Since the signal process of the 0-lepton channel has only two neutrinos decayed from a Z boson
in its ﬁnal state, the transverse momentum of a Z boson is equivalent to Emiss
T . In the 1-lepton
channel, a W boson of the signal process is reconstructed with a pTof lepton and a MET. In
addition, the longitudinal momentum of a neutrino can be obtained by constraining the lepton and
neutrino system with the W boson mass.
4.6 Overlap between different objects in the same event
Because the object reconstructions are performed at the same time, the same response in the
detector may be treated in two di ﬀerent objects. The VH-loose electrons, VH-loose muons, τ
lepton, small-Rjets, and large- Rjets are considered. The overlap between the di ﬀerent objects is
removed as follows:
•tau-electron: If∆R(τ,e)<0.2, theτlepton is removed.
•tau-muon: If∆R(τ,µ)<0.2, theτlepton is removed. If the τlepton has pT>50 GeV and
the muon is a CaloTagged muon, then the τlepton is not removed.
•electron-muon: If a default muon shares an ID track with an electron, the electron is
removed. If a CaloTagged muon shares an ID track with an electron, the muon is removed.
•muon-small-Rjet:If a jet and a muon are close (Satisfying a condition {A.∞(B.∈(C.∞
D.))}), then the jet is removed.
A.∆R(jet,µ)<0.2
B.the jet has less than three associated tracks with pT>500 MeV
C.thepTratio of the muon and jet is larger than 0.5
D.the ratio of the muon pTto the sum of pTof tracks with pT>500 MeV associated with
the jet is larger than 0.7
For any surviving jets, if ∆R(jet,µ)<min(0.4,0.04+10 GeV/pµ
T), the muon is only con-

Chapter 5
Event selection and categorisation
This chapter describes event selection requirements in Section 5.1, and event categorisations in
Section5.2.
5.1 Event selection
This analysis uses the events collected by the trigger as described in Section 5.1.1. After that,
this analysis selects events characterised by a vector boson and a Higgs boson. The criteria to
enhance events having a vector boson are described in Section 5.1.2with their di ﬀerent event
topologies. The conditions to identify the Higgs candidate with pairs of either b- orc- jets are
described in Section 5.1.3. Table 5.2and Table 5.3in the end of this section summarise all the
event selection requirements in di ﬀerent regimes and lepton channels.
5.1.1 T rigger selection
According to the data taken period and information of the HLT and L1 system as explained
in Section 2.2.4, the several kinds of triggers are prepared. The trigger used in this analysis is
described in this section and summarised in Table 5.1.
As discussed in Section 1.2.3, this analysis uses a vector boson signature decaying into leptons
to get high signal sensitivity for the VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) processes. Since triggering the events with
leptons from a vector boson is the e ﬀective way to obtain the targeted physics events of this anal-
ysis, triggers by the missing transverse momentum ( Emiss
T ), electrons, and muons are used. The
same trigger selections can be adopted to collect both the targeted VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) events. In the
events with muons from a vector boson ( µsub-channel) in the 1-, 2-lepton channel, the events col-
lected by the µtrigger and the Emiss
T trigger are overlapped. The Emiss
T trigger uses the observables

GeV . This is because the single muon trigger e ﬃciency is higher in the 2-lepton channel than in
the 1-lepton channel.
For aeµcontrol region, which is described in Section 5.2.2, the single electron triggers are
required ﬁrst. Afterwards, the single muon triggers are required to the selected events. More
details on the triggers used in the Run2 experiment are summarised in Table E.1in Appendix E.

Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 61
5.1.2 Vector boson selection
5.1.2.1 0-lepton channel
Since the ZH,Z→νν,H→b¯b/c¯cprocess is the target of this channel, the transverse mo-
mentum of two neutrinos is equivalent to Emiss
T , There is a signal process where ZHis in the
production, with Zdecaying into neutrinos and the Higgs decaying into b¯b(c¯c), resulting in a ﬁnal
state containing zero VH-loose leptons, as deﬁned in Section 4.2.
TheEmiss
T is required to be greater than 150 GeV , with respect to the Emiss
T trigger thresholds (see
Section5.1.1), where the trigger e ﬃciency achieves about 90%. Trigger scale factors of 0.9 to 1.0
are applied in the low Emiss
T region to correct for the residual turn-on e ﬀects.
Furthermore, a scalar sum of the pT(ST) is required to be greater than 120 GeV for the 2-jet
region, 150 GeV for the 3 or more jet region. The regions classiﬁed based on the number of jets
are deﬁned in Section 5.2.2. This criterion is designed to remove phase space that is associated
with uncertainties due to a non-trivial dependence of the trigger e ﬃciency on the jet activity and
is also inherited from the previous analysis [ 28,31].
5.1.2.2 1-lepton channel
Since the one lepton ﬁnal state of the W→ℓνprocess is considered, events with exactly one
WH-signal lepton and no additional VH-loose lepton, as deﬁned in Section 4.2, are selected. The
pV
Tis reconstructed as the vector sum of the Emiss
T and the lepton transverse momentum in pV
T>75
GeV .
5.1.2.3 2-lepton channel
Sinceeeorµµﬁnal states in the Z→ℓℓprocess are considered, the Zboson can be reconstructed
as a system of two VH-loose leptons, as deﬁned in Section 4.2. The two leptons are required to
have the same ﬂavour, and at least one of them needs to satisfy the ZH-signal lepton requirements.
The leading electron needs to satisfy pT>25 GeV, and the leading muon needs to satisfy pT>
27 GeV with respect to the trigger thresholds. For di-muon events, the two muons are further
required to be of opposite charge; the requirement is not applied to di-electron events due to a
higher rate of charge mis-identiﬁcation. Furthermore, the invariant mass of the di-lepton system
must be consistent with the Z-boson mass: 81 <m(ee, µµ)<101 GeV in the resolved VH(b¯b)
andVH(c¯c) regimes, 66 <m(ee, µµ)<116 GeV in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime. This requirement
suppresses backgrounds having a non-resonant lepton-pair, such as t¯tand multi-jet processes.

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
62 5.1 Event selection
5.1.3 Higgs-candidate selection
The Higgs boson is reconstructed with two reconstructed jets that pass jet ﬂavour tagging in
Section4.4. The two jet system for the Higgs boson reconstruction is called as “Higgs-candidate”;
the rest of the jets are called as “non-Higgs-candidate” jets. According to the ﬂavour tagging
scheme, diﬀerent selection requirements are applied to the resolved ( VH(b¯b),VH(c¯c)) regimes and
the boosted regime.
5.1.3.1 Resolved regime
Events with multiple jets ( Njet≥2) are accepted to reconstruct the H→b¯bprocess with two
small-Rjets (pT>20 GeV).
The Higgs-candidate jets are identiﬁed with two signal jets deﬁned in Table 4.3, and tagged
by the PCFT scheme. Based on the PCFT bins (Section 4.4.2.1), the resolved VH(b¯b) regime
andVH(c¯c) regime are orthogonally deﬁned, as shown in Figure 5.1. Events with exact two b-
tagged jets are categorised into the resolved VH(b¯b) regime (BB-tag region), while events with at
least onec-tight tagged jets are categorised into the VH(c¯c) regime (CTC, CTN-tag regions). The
two heavy ﬂavour tagged *1signal jets are referred to as the Higgs-candidate jets. Exceptionally,
events with only one c-loose tagged jet, and events with one b-tagged jet and one c-tight tagged
jet are categorised into the background dominant control regions, which are discussed in Section
5.2.2. TheVH(H→b¯b) events distribute in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime, while the VH(H→c¯c)
events distribute in the VH(c¯c) regime. Since this thesis focuses on the property measurements
ofVH(H→b¯b) signal process, the VH(H→b¯b) signal prediction in the BB-tag region and the
background estimation in all the tagged regions are considered in this thesis as shown in Figure 5.1.
Here, the details of the background estimation, which provide large impacts on the VH(H→b¯b)
signal prediction, are described in Section 9.2.3.
The rest of jets including the forward jets are treated as the non-Higgs-candidate jets, typically
from the initial state radiation (ISR) processes.
The two Higgs-candidate jets are sorted by their pT, where the leading jet is denoted as j1or
b1and the sub-leading jet is denoted as j2orb2. The non-Higgs-candidate jets are also sorted by
theirpT.
The leading Higgs-candidate jet is required to have pT>45 GeV , while the other is required to
havepT>20 GeV . Due to the known issue of the mis-modelling of gluon splitting in V +jets at
low Higgs mass region, the invariant mass of the two Higgs-candidate jets (denoted as mbb,mcc) is
required to satisfy mbb,cc>50 GeV .
*1In the CTN region, two signal jets are chosen; c-tight tagged jet and pV
Tleading non-tagged jet.

5.1.3.2 Boosted regime
In the boosted regime, the dedicated b-jet tagging (PCBT) is deployed in the boosted VH(b¯b)
regime, but the same ﬂavour tagging used in pV
T<400 GeV (PCFT) is deployed in the VH(c¯c)
regime in pV
T>400 GeV since dedicated charm quark jet tagging in the boosted regime is not
developed enough yet. This thesis focuses on the VH(H→b¯b) signal prediction in the boosted
VH(b¯b) regime.
The VR track jets are b-tagged or untagged with the 85 % WP in the PCBT scheme. The large-
Rjet with the highest pTis used for the Higgs-candidate reconstruction. The three VR track-jets
with the highest pTare required, and the large- Rjet is required to have exactly two b-tagged VR
track jets inside the large- Rjet’s cone. The reconstructed mass of the Higgs-candidate large- Rjet
(denoted as mj) needs to have mj>50 GeV .
Because diﬀerent ﬂavour tagging frameworks are deployed in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime
(PCBT) and the VH(c¯c) regime in pV
T>400 GeV (PCFT), there are overlap events between the
boostedVH(b¯b) regime and VH(c¯c) regime. The overlap of VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) is
negligible as described in Appendix D.

=== PDF PAGE 78 ===
64 5.2 Event categorisation
5.1.4 Additional background suppression
Multi-jet suppression in 0-lepton:
Multi-jet background due to mismeasured jets in the calorimeters is suppressed by the following
selection requirements:
•For resolved VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c),|∆Φ(j1,j2)|<140◦,
•|∆Φ(Emiss
T ,Hcand)|>120◦,
•|∆Φ(Emiss
T ,closest jets)|; In resolved VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) regime,>20◦in 2-jet
region,>30◦in 3-jet region; In boosted VH(H→b¯b) regime,>30◦,
wherej1andj2are the Higgs-candidate jets in resolved VH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) regime,
and∆Φ(Emiss
T ,closest jets) is the azimuthal angle between the Emiss
T vector and the closest jet. In the
resolvedVH(H→b¯b) andVH(H→c¯c) regime, three leading signal jets are considered, while in
the boostedVH(H→b¯b) regime, only small- Rjets withpT>70 GeV outside the Higgs-candidate
large-Rjet are considered.
The requirement is applied because the kinematic parameter tends to concentrate on small values
of|∆Φ(Emiss
T ,closest jets)|in the case of multi-jet events. With this selection, the remaining fraction
of multi-jet contamination is as small as O(1) % of the total background, and the multi-jet process
is negligible in the 0-lepton channel.
Multi-jet suppression in 1-lepton:
To suppress multi-jet backgrounds, Emiss
T is required to be larger than 30 GeV in the electron
sub-channel of resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes, Emiss
T >50 GeV for the boosted VH(b¯b)
regime. The mW
T, as deﬁned in Eq. ( 8.1), is required to be larger than 20 GeV in 75 <pW
T<150
GeV . As the Emiss
T trigger is also used in the 1-lepton’s µsub-channel, the same ST*2cut in the
0-lepton channel is applied.

measurement, which is introduced in Section 1.3.2. Thus, the analysis region is divided by the

5.2.2 Resolved regimes
In the resolved VH(b¯b) regime, two pV
Tregions are deﬁned as 150 <pV
T <250 GeV and
250<pV
T<400 GeV in the 0-lepton channel, and three pV
Tregions are deﬁned as 75 <pV
T<150
GeV , 150<pV
T<250 GeV , and 250 <pV
T<400 GeV in the 1-,2-lepton channels. The analysis
region is also divided by the number of inclusive (signal +forward) jets, where the non-Higgs-
candidate jets with pT>30 GeV is counted, referred to as “ Njetregion”. Since all the jets are
available in pT>20 GeV in Section 4.3, to avoid confusion between the counting of all jets and
theNjetregion, a term “ N-jet event” is introduced for an event with Njets. For example, when there

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===
Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 67
is an event with two Higgs-candidate ( b-)jets and one non-Higgs-candidate jet in 20 <pT<30
GeV , this event is called as “3-jet event”, while this event is categorised into “2-jet region”. In the
0-lepton channel, three Njetregions are deﬁned as 2-jet, 3-jet, and 4-jet regions. In the 1-lepton
channel, two Njetregions are deﬁned as 2-jet and 3-jet regions. In the 2-lepton channel, three Njet
regions are deﬁned as 2-jet, 3-jet, and Njet≥4 (denoted as ” >4-jet”) regions.
Signal Region (SR) and ∆RControl Region (CR):
The signal regions require the events to be categorised in the BB-, C TC-, and CTN-regions of
Figure5.1. The targeted signal events ( VH(H→b¯b) process) in this thesis are predicted in the
signal region of the BB-tag region. As shown in Figure 5.2, since the signal events are concen-
trating in the middle of the 2-D histograms as functions of the opening angle ( ∆R) between the
Higgs-candidate jets ( ∆R(j1,j2)) andpV
T, the signal pure region and background dominant regions
are deﬁned as follows according to these observables. Because the Higgs-candidate jets are de-
cayed from a resonant Higgs boson in the signal events, the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets is reconstructed with a peak of the Higgs mass ( ∼125 GeV), and the ∆R(j1,j2) has a peak as
a function of pV
T. Therefore, the combination of these observables approximates the invariant mass
of two Higgs-candidate jets, and these properties can help to distinguish the signal events from
the background events. Figure I.2shows the distribution of invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets for all signal and background events, and Figure 5.3shows the distribution of ∆R(j1,j2) for
all signal and background events. Historically, since the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets is used as a ﬁnal discriminant variable in the ﬁt, and since the SR-CR cut with ∆R(j1,j2) and
pV
Tis eﬀective to describe the background contributions as a function of pV
T, this SR and ∆RCR
deﬁnitions are exploited. Besides the Higgs boson, a resonant diboson decay ( Z→b¯b) also has
the same property with its invariant mass ( Z=∼91 GeV).
A High ∆RControl Region (High- ∆RCR) is designed with the cut function in Table 5.4to
constrain the V+jets and top quark background events. The cut functions are deﬁned to allow 95
%(85 %) of the signal events in the 2-jet( >3-jet) region stay below this cut function. In order
to discard the mis-modelling issue by MC simulation in High- ∆RCRs and to keep the High- ∆R
CR kinematically closer to the signal region, an upper cut at ∆R=πis applied. This cut discards
∼40 % of events in High- ∆RCRs and has a negligible impact on the signal region. The High-
∆RCRs are enriched with the top quark background events in the 1-lepton channel, with Z +jets
background events in the 2-lepton channel, and both events in the 0-lepton channel.
A Low∆RControl Region (Low- ∆RCR) is designed with the cut function in Table 5.4, to model
theW+jets process with b¯bjets in the 1-lepton channel in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime. The cut

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
68 5.2 Event categorisation
functions are deﬁned to allow 90 % of the diboson events stay in the signal region above this cut
function, for the validation analysis that uses the diboson VZ(Z→b¯b) events as the signal events,
which is explained in Section 1.4.3.
The signal region (SR) is deﬁned between these two cut functions, or below the High- ∆RCR
cut function, to predict the VH signal events ( VH(H→b¯b)) or to predict the VZ signal events
(VZ(Z→b¯b)). The High- ∆RCR cut functions and Low- ∆RCR cut functions are superimposed
in signal distributions in Figure 5.2.

V+light ﬂavour Control Region (CR):
TheV+light ﬂavour Control Region ( V+lf CR) is the inclusive C TN-tag region. The V+lf CR
consists of events with one of the Higgs candidate jets being a loose c-tagged jet, and one being
a non-tagged jet. This CR is set in all three lepton channels. In the 0-lepton channel, both of
W+light andZ+light ﬂavour jet events exist. In the 1-lepton channel, this CR is composed of ∼60
% ofW+light ﬂavour jet events. In the 2-lepton channel, it is composed of more than 70 % of
Z+light ﬂavour jet events.

In the 2-lepton channel, a high purity t¯tcontrol region is deﬁned by selecting SR events with
diﬀerent ﬂavoured leptons ( eµorµe). This treatment eliminates most of the backgrounds and the
signal events, which are all expected to contain the same ﬂavour lepton pairs from a Zboson decay.
TheeµCR in resolved VH(b¯b) 2-lepton channel is used for deriving the top quark background
template of BDT ﬁnal discriminant distributions for the corresponding SRs.
BCT-tag Top Control Region (CR) in 0-,1-lepton channels:
The top background components with b+other mixed ﬂavour labelled jets at the truth level, de-
noted as Top(bc) and Top(bl), enter into the SRs in both the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===
70 5.2 Event categorisation
due to ﬂavour mis-tagging, which is not negligible. As discussed in Section 5.1.3.1, the Top CR
region can be deﬁned by selecting events with at least one tight c-tag and at least one b-tag (BCT-
tag region). Since SRs in the 0-, 1-lepton channels use tight c-veto andb-veto in resolved VH(b¯b)
andVH(c¯c), respectively, the Top CR is completely orthogonal to SRs in the resolved VH(b¯b) and
VH(c¯c) regimes. In this top CR, the Higgs-candidate is constructed from the pTleadingb-tagged
jet and the pTleading tight c-tagged jet. As shown in Figure 5.4(a), when the b-jet and c-jet
decayed from the same top quark are used in the Higgs-candidate reconstruction, this process has
the same the kinematic feature such as mj1,j2and∆Ras the signal process, which directly a ﬀects
the signal sensitivity in the SRs.

5.2.4 Improved event categorisation for the STXS measurement

reconstructed analysis, cross-section of each truth STXS bin is measured with the signal simulated
samples that preserve the truth information of their applicable STXS bin as discussed in Section

of three track jets is a touchstone of the TopCR requirement.

Fig. 5.5: The overall analysis regions. In regions indicated in italicizedfont, the event yield in

=== PDF PAGE 87 ===
Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 73
11.2. According to this truth bin information, the events in a truth STXS bin are migrated from
several reconstructed regions. This migration is caused by the object reconstruction and event cat-
egorisation eﬀect such as the resolution, identiﬁcation e ﬃciency, or counting of the lepton and jets.
As a result of this event migration, the statistical uncertainties of the di ﬀerential cross-section in
STXS bins increase, since the statistics of signal events categorised in a certain STXS bin would be
smaller in a corresponding analysis region. The changes of the statistical uncertainty can be eval-
uated with the di ﬀerential cross-sections normalised with the SM predictions in di ﬀerent STXS
bins as shown in Figure 7.10. The event migration can be evaluated by a correlation between the
reconstructed regions and the truth STXS bins as shown in Figure 7.7or a correlation between
each truth STXS bins as shown in Figure 7.12. Here, while the less events migrate between di ﬀer-
ent two bins/regions, the correlation of these two bins /regions decreases, which can be said as “the
correlation is improved”.
In order to improve the correlation, mainly two kinds of treatments are performed. Firstly, the
pTrequirement for counting the number of non-Higgs-candidate reconstructed jets is set to pT>30
GeV to align the truth ISR jet deﬁnition in the STXS framework. Secondly, a Final State Radiation
(FSR) jet is identiﬁed and removed from the counting of non-Higgs-candidate jets to align the truth
ISR jet deﬁnition. This FSR jet treatment is described in detail in Chapter 7.

=== PDF PAGE 88 ===
74
Chapter 6
Energy correction of jets originating
from Higgs decays
The overview of jet energy correction is provided in Section 6.1, followed by descriptions of four
correction methods in the following subsections. Section 6.6describes the performance of the four
corrections on the Higgs mass resolution.
6.1 Overview of dedicated jet energy correction
The dedicated jet energy corrections for the Higgs-candidate jets are applied considering the
topological information of the VH(H→b¯b) signal process *1to improve the Higgs mass resolu-
tion and signal sensitivity on top of the standard jet energy correction. There are four dedicated
corrections: Muon-in-jet correction, pTdependent correction (PtReco), Kinematic Fit (KF), and
FSR recovery. These corrections are performed after the object reconstruction, event selection,
and event categorisation.
6.2 Muon-in-jet correction
Muon-in-jet correction calibrates the energy of the Higgs-candidate jets that decay semi-
leptonically into muons in ∼20 % probability, as the energy of muon is not fully ab-
sorbed by the calorimeter. In the resolved regime, if a muon is identiﬁed with ∆R(j,µ)<
min(0.4,0.04+10 GeV/pµ
T), where jdenotes a Higgs-candidate jet, the muon 4-momentum is
added to the Higgs-candidate jet. O(1) GeV energy loss of a muon in the calorimeters calculated
*1The energy corrections in the VH(c¯c) regime are conﬁgured considering the VH(H→c¯c) process.

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===
Chapter 6. Energy correction of jets originating from Higgs decays 75
according to the path length is subtracted, and therefore there is no double counting of the muon
energy deposit in the calorimeters expected. In the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, a muon inside
the Higgs-candidate large- Rjet cone is identiﬁed, then the muon 4-momentum is added to the
Higgs-candidate large- Rjet.
The muon-in-jet correction is applied to all the events. Systematic uncertainty on the momentum
correction is considered to be negligible in this correction.
6.3 pTdependent correction
ApTdependent correction, denoted as “PtReco”, aims to correct the missing energy due to
neutrinos produced in the b-jets with semi-leptonic decays. The PtReco is applied in the resolved
VH(b¯b) regime in all the lepton channels. The correction factor is derived from the ratio between
muon-in-jet corrected b-tagged jets, and generator-based truth jets which include truth muons and
neutrinos. The b-jets with muons and without muons are considered separately. There is a ∼10 %
diﬀerence on the correction factor between b-jets with and without muons. The correction factor
is derived in the nominal model and applied to all systematic variations. Systematic uncertainty on
the correction factor is considered to be negligible in this correction.
6.4 Kinematic Fit (KF)
Kinematic Fit (KF) is applied in the 2-lepton channel with at most one non-Higgs-candidate jet
(pT>20 GeV) after the muon-in-jet correction. The four-momentum of a Zboson decaying into
an electron or a muon pair is measured with high precision, allowing a constraint on momentum
balance in the transverse plane to be applied, thereby improving jet energy resolution. The resolu-
tion of lepton is typically 1 % level, resolution of jet is typically 10 % level, and no physical Emiss
T
is expected in the 2-lepton channel.
In the KF, a negative log likelihood is minimised to obtain the ﬁt value

where the index idenotes all the objects except for the Higgs-candidate jets including leptons, jets,
and MET soft term, index jdenotes all ﬂavour-tagged small- Rjets, and index kdenotes all objects.
The ﬁrst term is the resolution term for the ﬁnal state objects such as electrons, muons, light
ﬂavour jets and MET. The energy responses of these objects are treated as Gaussian distributions.
The MET resolution term is considered to comprehend the ISR e ﬀect inggZH.σiis the energy or

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===
76 6.5 FSR recovery
momentum resolution of each object.
To better model the energy response for the Higgs-candidate jets, instead of Gaussian distributions,
a transfer function of the probability density function of the ratio between reconstructed jet pTand
generator-based truth jet pTare used in the likelihood, which is presented as the second term. The
transfer function is measured in 8 pTregions, 2 ηregions and with or without muon decay inde-
pendently measured from the signal simulated sample. The transfer functions are updated in the
resolvedVH(b¯b) regime, and are ﬁrst derived in the VH(H→c¯c) regime. The transfer functions
are only available for small- Rjets, and the Gaussian distribution is treated as the energy response
for large-Rjets.
The third and fourth terms are the balance constraint on the xandydirection, respectively. The
Gaussian constraint of xorydirection is obtained from the ZHsignal simulated sample, where
σbalis expected to be 12 GeV for the 2 jets region and 15 GeV for the 3 jets region.
The ﬁfth term is the Z boson mass constraint which is assumed to follow a Breit-Wigner distribu-
tion, where mZandΓZare the Z boson mass and width, respectively. The mℓℓis calculated with
the ﬁtted lepton’s momentum.
No additional uncertainty is applied in the KF correction.
6.5 FSR recovery
Non-Higgs-candidate jets may come from a Final State Radiation (FSR) of the Higgs-candidate
jets. To identify jets from the FSR, a continuous cut on the sum of ∆Rbetween a non-Higgs-
candidate jet and each of two Higgs-candidate jets ( ∆Rj,j1
Higgs
+∆Rj,j2
Higgs
) as a function of pV
T, is
deﬁned as discussed in Section 7.2. A non-Higgs candidate jet below the cut function is considered
as the FSR jets, and is merged to the Higgs candidate system. This correction is referred to as “FSR
recovery”. The FSR recovery is applied in the 2-lepton channel for the resolved regimes for the
ﬁrst time to improve the signal mj1,j2resolution by ∼4 %, and the MV A based signal sensitivity by
∼1 %. The FSR recovery is not applied to the 0-,1-lepton channel, since the t¯tbackground events
get the peak of mj1,j2around the Higgs mass ( ∼125 GeV) when the FSR recovery is applied, and
it degrades the signal sensitivity. Additional systematic uncertainty for the FSR recovery is not
applied since they are comprehended in other systematic uncertainties.
More details on the FSR recovery are described in Chapter 7.

79
Chapter 7
FSR recovery
This chapter summarises the studies of the FSR recovery. The overall strategy of the FSR re-
covery is described in Section 7.1, the cut-based optimal identiﬁcation of FSR jets is explained
in Section 7.2, and the performance studies are summarised in Section 7.3in terms of the mass
resolution, signal sensitivity, the statistical uncertainties of the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section
measurement based on the STXS framework. The application of the FSR recovery into the main
VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) analysis is described in Section 7.4.
7.1 Strategy of FSR recovery
In case an event contains jets that do not originate from a Higgs boson, several possible sources
for these jets can be considered as follows.
•Gluon radiation from either or both b-jets decayed from Higgs boson, known as Final State
Radiation (FSR)
•Gluon or quark radiation from initial state partons in the Higgs boson production, known as
Initial State Radiation (ISR)
•Other radiation from Pile-up
Figure7.1illustrates the VH(H→b¯b) signal events containing an ISR jet and an FSR jet. At
the stage of event reconstruction, the types of jets are treated without classiﬁcation, called as
“additional jets”, which is equivalent with the term of “non-Higgs-candidate jets” used in the other
chapter.
In the resolved regimes ( pV
T<400 GeV), Final State Radiation (FSR) causes a loss of the
reconstructed invariant mass of the Higgs, since additional jets are typically not included in the

=== PDF PAGE 94 ===
80 7.1 Strategy of FSR recovery
HB1
B2FSR JetV
ISR Jet
Fig. 7.1: A VH(H→b¯b) process with an FSR jet and an ISR jet.
reconstruction of the Higgs system. Figure 7.2shows a histogram of invariant mass with two b-jets
and a leading additional jet ( mbb j), where a leading additional jet is labelled as FSR /ISR/pile-up
jet using truth information of the signal simulation sample. When the FSR jet is correctly identiﬁed
as a decay product of the Higgs boson, the invariant mass is accurately calculated, resulting in a
peak at the Higgs boson mass of around 125 GeV as shown in the FSR labelled histogram of Figure
7.2, along with an improvement in mass resolution.
In addition, as discussed in Section 5.2.4, the event migration across the reconstructed regions
degrades the statistical uncertainties of cross-section measurement in di ﬀerent STXS bins. The
STXS bins are classiﬁed based on the number of truth ISR jets in pV
T
,truth
<400 GeV as deﬁned
in Section 1.3.2, meanwhile the analysis regions are simply classiﬁed based on the number of
reconstructed jets including additional jets in pV
T<400 GeV . If the FSR jets can be removed from
the ISR jet counting, the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement for the number of ISR jets
would be improved.
In summary, the correct FSR jet identiﬁcation derives the following improvement:
•Correction of the four momentum of the Higgs system, and improvement of the resolution
of the mass reconstruction.
•Improvement of the correlation between the truth and reconstructed categorisation in the
STXS binning.
In theVH(H→b¯b/c¯c) analysis, an identiﬁcation of the FSR jets is performed toward all the
additional jets for pT>20 GeV , exploiting a cut function as described in Section 7.2. The iden-
tiﬁed FSR jet and the corresponding closest Higgs-candidate jet are summed in vectors, and the
corresponding Higgs-candidate jet is updated. After merging the jets, the events with the identiﬁed
FSR jets in pT>30 GeV are re-categorised to the smaller number of jet region. This treatment is
referred to as “FSR recovery”.

The order of corrections to apply the FSR recovery between the other three jet corrections
(Chapter6) is studied. As the result, no signiﬁcant performance change is found whenever the
FSR recovery is applied, which is described in detail in Appendix G.2. For the simplicity of im-
plementation and the theoretical reason from the KF, the FSR recovery is applied at the last of the
jet energy corrections.

7.2 FSR identiﬁcation
The FSR recovery should be applied to the jets that have FSR-like characteristics based on their
topological features. The criteria for identifying FSR jets are optimised using all additional jets:
the signal jets in pT>20 GeV and the forward jets in pT>30 GeV (See Table 4.3). As the QCD
colour exchange between the two b-jets tends to generate the FSR [ 109], most signiﬁcant topolog-
ical diﬀerences for diﬀerent types of additional jets are shown in Figure 7.3. These plots show the
2D∆η-∆φrelative distribution between additional jet and each b-jet using the VH(H→b¯b) signal
simulated samples, where leading b-jets (b1) is located in a ﬁxed position. The b1 is always ﬁxed
at (0,0) position, and then the plane is rotated to keep the sub-leading b-jets (b2) at π/4 direction.
A remarkable feature observed in these plots is that FSR jets tend to concentrate between the two
b-jets, and this characteristic can be utilised to distinguish them from the other types of additional
jets.
Considering this feature and good resolution of pV
Tas proxy of pH
T, a cut function with variables
of∆Rb1,j+∆Rb2,jandpV
Tis introduced to distinguish the FSR jets and other types of additional jets.

Tof the signal nominal MC
sample, where distributions of the events with FSR jet (red), ISR jet (blue), and pile-up jet (green)
are labelled with truth information stored in the MC sample. The cut function is intended to be
drawn at the boundary between the FSR jet region and the other types of additional jet regions. The
optimisation of the FSR cut function is conducted using all events containing three jets (Figure 7.4
(a)) in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime. The signal and background nominal
MC samples are used for the optimisation of the cut function. The signiﬁcance of the mbbsignal

7.3 Performance of FSR recovery
7.3.1 Higgs mass resolution
Here, the impact of FSR recovery on Higgs mass resolution is discussed. Figure 7.5shows the
improvement of mass resolution owing the FSR recovery. These plots show the Higgs mass distri-
bution (mbb) of the signal nominal MC sample ( ZH(Z→ℓℓ,H→b¯b) process). The corrections
of invariant mass of Higgs candidate system (Muon-in-jet and PtReco, described in Chapter 6) are
already applied. A Bukin Novosibirska function [ 108] is exploited to ﬁt each mass distribution and
to estimate the mass peak (mean[GeV]) and mass resolution ( σ[GeV]). Figure 7.5(a) shows the
distribution of events with only FSR identiﬁed jets in the 3-jet events before the event migration.
A blue line shows invariant mass of two b-jets (mbb), and a red line shows invariant mass of two

b-jets and an FSR identiﬁed jet ( mbb j(FS R)). In the Figure 7.5(a), the mass peak gets closer to ∼125
GeV after the FSR recovery (red). Figure 7.5(b) (c) shows the impact of the FSR recovery in 2-jet
events and in 3-jet events, respectively. After the event migration, the mass distributions in both
the 2-jet and 3-jet events get a closer peak to ∼125 GeV and the mass resolutions are improved.

7.3.2 Purity
The purity of truth FSR jet identiﬁcation is deﬁned by Eq. ( 7.3) and is evaluated based on the
truth information from the signal MC sample. According to the event yields of truth FSR events,
the overall purity of all pV
Tregions is 82%, while the purity for individual pV
Tinterval regions of
75<pV
T<150, 150,pV
T<250, and 250 <pV
T<400 GeV are 80%, 86%, 80%, respectively.

86 7.3 Performance of FSR recovery
Therefore, the FSR identiﬁcation can collect the correct FSR with high purity.
Purity=
Events with FSR labelled jets
Events with FSR identiﬁed jet
. (7.3)
Thanks to this accurate FSR identiﬁcation, the events in the ∼125 GeV peak of mbb jdistribution
are collected and migrated to the less Njetregion as shown in Figure 7.9(d).
7.3.3 Signal event migration
When there are FSR identiﬁed jets in the additional jets in pT>30 GeV , a signal event with
these jets is re-categorised into the smaller number of jets ( Njet) region. This signal event migration
is illustrated with Figure 7.6, and the impact of this migration is shown in Figure 7.7.
Figure7.6shows the signal event migration across the analysis Njetregions, where the signal
events are labelled with the number of truth ISR jets as deﬁned by the STXS bins. By applying the
FSR recovery, the signal events move to the 2-jet region. The problematic events are the events
with FSR jets ( Ntruth
IS R jets=0) but categorised in ≥3-jet region as shown in Figure 7.6(a). These events
are migrated to the 2-jet region more than the events with ISR jets ( Ntruth
IS R jets ≥1) that are migrated
due to mis-identiﬁcation of ISR jets by the FSR recovery. This result proves that the FSR recovery
can identify the correct FSR jets. The total event yields are changed between Figure 7.6(a) and
(b). This is because the FSR recovery collects the FSR-identiﬁed jets from up to the 4-jet event,
while the analysis uses only the 2-, 3-jet region in the 1-lepton channel. The FSR recovery brings
4-jet events with FSR-identiﬁed jets into the analysis regions in the 1-lepton channel. The similar
plots in the di ﬀerent lepton channels are shown in Figure G.11in Appendix G.3.
Figure7.7shows the correlation matrix between the truth STXS bin and the reconstructed anal-
ysis region in the 2-lepton channel. In Figures 7.7(a) and (b), fraction of event yields in the
diﬀerent analysis regions is calculated relative to inclusive event yields in the same lepton channel
in the same STXS bin. Figures 7.7(a) and (b) show the case before and after the event migration
is conducted by the FSR recovery, respectively. Figure 7.7(c) shows the di ﬀerences of the rela-
tive fraction values between these two cases. The correlation between the STXS truth bin and the
reconstructed analysis region improves. For example, for the component in the STXS bin with 0
ISR jet in the 2-jet region, the relative fraction increases. The changes of the relative fraction in all
the diﬀerent lepton channels have the same trends as shown in Figure G.12in Appendix G.3.

7.3.4 Impact on signal sensitivity
This analysis exploits BDT VHas a ﬁnal discriminant comparing the Higgs mass ( mbb) and other
kinematic observables, and its deﬁnition is explained in detail in Chapter 8,
The BDTVHlearns the trends of several kinds of kinematic variables as the BDT input variables.
The FSR recovery a ﬀects the input variables used for the BDT training, where mbbhas the most
signiﬁcant impact. Therefore, the re-training of BDT VHis demanded after the FSR recovery.
To evaluate the performance of signal sensitivity, a BDT VH-based signiﬁcance is deﬁned by the
following equation
S=
)
Σi[2(si+bi)ln(1+
si
bi
)]. (7.4)
Table7.2shows the comparison of BDT VH-based signiﬁcance before and after the FSR recovery in
the diﬀerent regions and di ﬀerent lepton channels. The BDT VHafter the FSR recovery is trained
with the same conﬁguration. The BDT VHsigniﬁcance is improved by ∼0.5 % in the 2-lepton
channel, but is decreased by ∼0.3 %,∼1.9 % in the 0-, 1-lepton channels, respectively. The
signiﬁcance in the 2-jet region tends to be increased except in the 1-lepton channel, while the
signiﬁcance in the multiple jet region tends to be decreased. This is because the yields of signal
events increase in the 2-jet region, while decreases in the multiple ( >3) jet regions as shown in

7.3.5 Impact on precision of ﬁducial differential cross-section measurement
Here, the eﬀect of FSR recovery on the measurement of ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section based
on the STXS framework is discussed. The phase space is divided into 15 STXS bins (15 Param-
eter of Interests (15POIs)) using a combination of pV
T
,truth
and the number of the ISR jets at the
truth level. The Asimov dataset is used to assess uncertainties in the ﬁt as described in Section
11.3. Figure 7.10shows the results of the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement of 15
POIs before and after applying the FSR recovery. These measurements consider all the statistical
and systematic uncertainties estimated for the ﬁnal results *2. Figure 7.10(a) is the result before
the FSR recovery, and Figure 7.10(b) is the result after the FSR recovery in all lepton channels.
The eﬀect in the 0-, 2-lepton channels is shown in the ZHSTXS bins, while the e ﬀect in the
1-lepton channel is shown in the WHSTXS bins. These plots show the expectation of signal
strength measurement with errors in the di ﬀerent STXS bins (POIs). In all STXS bins, the statis-
tical uncertainty is dominant in the total uncertainty of the cross-section measurement except for
the ‘WH,75<pV
T
,truth
<150’ STXS bin.
Table7.3summarises the relative changes of the total, statistical, and systematic uncertainties
between the results before and after the FSR recovery in the di ﬀerent STXS bins in the Figure
7.10. In the STXS bins split by the number of truth ISR jets, the statistical uncertainties decrease
by a maximum of 8 %. The correlation of truth STXS bins and reconstructed analysis region is
improved as discussed in Section 7.3.3. As discussed in Section 11.2, the POI is assigned to each
STXS bin in the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement. When more events of the same
STXS bin are collected in the same analysis region as shown in Figure 7.11, the corresponding
POI is determined with more statistics. Therefore, the improvement of the correlation results in
the improvement of the statistical uncertainty in the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement
as explained in Section 5.2.4.
In the STXS bins of pV
T
,truth
>400 GeV , the di ﬀerence is not observed since the FSR recovery
is not applied in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime. In all the STXS bins except the ‘ WH,75<pV
T
,truth
<
150’ STXS bin, the changes of systematic uncertainties don’t largely a ﬀect the total uncertainties,
since the statistical uncertainties are dominant. As the results, the total uncertainty improves by a
maximum of ∼9 % by applying the FSR recovery in all lepton channels.
Figure7.12shows the correlation matrix between the STXS bins (POIs) in the measurements
before (a) and after (b) the FSR recovery. In these correlation matrix plots, the statistical uncer-
tainty is only considered. The correlation between the STXS bins is calculated as discussed in
*2The acceptance uncertainty (Section 9.2.1) in the 2-lepton channel is estimated after the event migration of the FSR
recovery, but those in the 0-, 1-lepton channel are estimated before the event migration.

7.4 Optimal application to the ﬁnal results of this analysis
The FSR recovery was found to reduce the statistical uncertainty of the ﬁducial di ﬀerential
cross-section measurement by up to ∼8 % across all lepton channels. Concerning the signiﬁcance,
it improves by ∼0.5 % in the 2-lepton channel, but leads to a decline in the 0- and 1-lepton
channels. In the 2-lepton channel, the FSR recovery can improve the precision of the ﬁducial
diﬀerential cross-section measurement without degrading the signal sensitivity by migrating the
FSR-like events. Therefore, in the ﬁnal results of the VH(b¯b,c¯c) analysis, the FSR recovery is
applied into the 2-lepton channel.

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
97
Chapter 8
Multivariate discriminants
This chapter describes a strategy of event discrimination by MV A in Section 8.1, input variables
of MV A in Section 8.2, MV A training setup in Section 8.3, MV A evaluation in Section 8.4.
8.1 Strategy of discrimination in SR and Low- ∆RCR
Having an invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate jets at the head of the list, there are several
kinematic variables to verify some di ﬀerences between the signal and background processes. In
order to increase the signal purity in the SR and the Low- ∆RCR, this analysis uses MultiV ariate
Analysis (MV A). By training a set of well-chosen input variables, the multivariate likelihood learns
the diﬀerence between signal and background events and produce a one dimensional discriminant.
This analysis uses the Boosted Decision Tree (BDT) method provided by the TMV A package
of ROOT [ 110]. The BDT produces a score distributing in the range of [-1,1], referred to as
”BDT (output) score”, where the low score represents background-like events and the high score
represents signal-like events.
This analysis utilises three di ﬀerent types of BDT models: 1⃝“BDTVH”,2⃝“BDTCRLow”, and3⃝
“BDTVZ”. The training conﬁgurations of these three BDT models are the same, except the training
samples as described in Section 8.3.
1⃝To maximise the signal sensitivity and to measure the Higgs properties with high precision,
BDTVHis used to construct a ﬁnal discriminant variable to distinguish the VHsignal events
from the other background events in the SRs.
2⃝To have better constraint on the W+jets in the SRs of the resolved VH(b¯b) regime in the
1-lepton channel, the BDT CRLowis used to discriminate the W+jets events from t¯tevents in
the Low-∆RCR as discussed in Section 9.2.3.

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
98 8.2 Input variables
3⃝BDTVZis trained to discriminate diboson VZ(Z→b¯b/Z→c¯c) events from other back-
ground events. As discussed in Section 1.4.3, since these diboson processes share nearly
the same topology with the VH(H→b¯b/H→c¯c) signal processes, BDT VZis used
as the replacement of the BDT VHto measure the signal strength and signiﬁcance of the
VZ(Z→b¯b/Z→c¯c) processes before the VHproperty measurements for the validation of
this analysis.
8.2 Input variables
Table8.1summarises all the BDT input variables. The three BDT models (BDT VH, BDTCRLow,
BDTVZ) use the same set of input variables.
Variables in the resolved regimes: *1Here, all the input variables used in the resolved regimes
are explained.
•pV
T: transverse energy of the vector boson. In the 0-lepton channel, it is equivalent to the
missing transverse energy ( Emiss
T ); in the 1-lepton channel, it is the vector sum of Emiss
T and
the lepton pT; in the 2-lepton channel, it is the vector sum of the 2 charged lepton pT.
•pj1
Tandpj2
T: transverse momenta of the Higgs candidate jets. j1refers to the jet with higher
pT.
•mj1j2: invariant mass of the Higgs candidate system.
•∆R(j1,j2): the angular distance between the two Higgs-candidate jets *2.
•mj1j2j3: the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate jets and the remaining jet with the highest
pT. When there are only 2 jets in an event, mj1j2j3=mj1j2.
•∆φ(⃗V,⃗Hcand): azimuthal distance between the reconstructed vector boson and Higgs boson
candidates.
•binDL1r(j1), binDL1r(j2): the variable shows the PCFT-bin to which each Higgs-candidate jet
belongs; the untagged, the loose (70 % WP) and the tight (60 % WP) b-tagged, and the loose
and the tight c-tagged bins. In the MV A, the bin numbers for two of the Higgs-candidate
jets are used.

0-lepton channel speciﬁc variables :
*1The usage of input variables is harmonized between the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes.
*2The angular distance ∆R(i,j) between two particles iandj(or objects such as jets) is deﬁned as ∆R(i,j)=√

Variables in the boosted regimes: Here, all the input variables used in the boosted regime are
explained. The boosted VH(b¯b) regime shares the MV A input variables with the resolved VH(b¯b)

=== PDF PAGE 116 ===
102 8.2 Input variables
regime due to the topological similarity. For example, the invariant mass of two Higgs candidate
jets (mbb) can be transferred as the mass of Higgs candidate large- Rjet (mj). Several additional
variables are added e.g. Colour information described below that improve the signiﬁcance.
•pV
T: shares the same deﬁnition as for the resolved regimes.
•pj1,trk
T ,pj2,trk
T andpj3,trk
T : transverse momenta of the VR track jets inside the Higgs candidate
large-R jet. j1andj2refer to the b-tagged VR track jets, and j3refers to the leading non-
Higgs-candidate jet.
•mJ: the invariant mass of the Higgs candidate large- Rjet.
•∆R(j1,trk,j2,trk): the angular distance between the two b-tagged VR track jets.
•N(track-jets in J): the number of VR track jets that are ghost-associated to the leading
large-Rjet.
•N(add. small R-jets): the number of additional small- Rjets that are not matched to the
leading large-Rjet, which requires ∆R(small-Rjet, large-Rjet)>1.0.
•∆φ(⃗V,⃗Hcand): the same deﬁnition as for the resolved regimes.
•Colour: a variable designed to exploit the di ﬀerence in colour-ﬂow between gluon splittings
and decay from QCD singlet states, which is described below.
•binDL1r(j1,trk), binDL1r(j2,trk): corresponds to the PCBT bins to which the b-tagged VR track
jet belongs: the 85 %, the 77 %, the 70 % and the 60 % b-tagging eﬃciency bins.
0-lepton channel speciﬁc variables :
•Emiss
T : missing transverse energy, same as pV

8.3 T raining setup
The signal and background events that pass the event selection requirements in Section 5.1and
the jet energy corrections in Chapter 6are used for the BDT training. Furthermore, the truth
tagging (Section 4.4.4) is applied to produce the training samples to enhance the training statistics.
The nominal MC samples are used in the BDT training.
In the BDTVH, a signal sample is the VH(H→b¯b) events in the VH(b¯b) regimes*3, and back-

The hyperparameters are optimised per training region to maximise the BDT-based signiﬁcance
calculated by Eq. ( 8.6), but avoid overtraining. The gradient boost method is used in the resolved
VH(b¯b) regime. It is known to be good at capturing e ﬀects outside the bulk of the distributions,
and to give improvement to the BDT performance. AdaBoost is an optimal method for low sig-

8.4 MVA evaluation
By using the MV A, signal and background events can be successfully distinguished in all three
cases: BDTVH, BDTCRLow, BDTVZ. The BDT output distributions (post-ﬁt) are shown in: Section
12.1.2for the BDTVH, Figure9.5for the BDTCRLow, and Appendix I.4for the BDTVZ.
The separation power in the BDT output distribution is evaluated with Eq. ( 8.6) in binned
distributions, referred to as ”Asimov Signiﬁcance”.
S=
)
Σi[2(si+bi)ln(1+
si
bi
)], (8.6)
wheresiandbiare the number of signal and background events contained in the ithbin of a binned
BDT score histogram. The signiﬁcance represents the signal excess over the background-only
hypothesis, where the unit is the standard deviations ( σ). The Asimov signiﬁcance is used as an
important criterion to adopt new attempts in the BDT training.
The signiﬁcance can also be calculated with the mbbbinned distribution, which has the best
separation power of the signal and background processes in the kinematic variables used in this
analysis. The comparison of the separation power between the BDT output distribution and the
mbbdistribution is summarised with the signiﬁcance values in Table 8.3. By introducing the BDT
score as the ﬁnal discriminant variable, the Asimov signiﬁcance improves ∼60 % for the BDT VH
and∼20 % for the BDT VZfrom thembbdistribution. Therefore, the MV A method improves the
separation power compared to the usage of the mbbdistribution as the ﬁnal discriminant variable.
As an overtraining cross-check, the ROC curves and the Asimov signiﬁcance of the training and
testing samples are compared to each other, and the Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) test is performed
with BDT output distributions to make sure the BDT trained model has similar separation power
in both samples, as shown in Figure 8.3.

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
107
Chapter 9
Systematic uncertainties
This chapter describes experimental uncertainties in Section 9.1, and the modelling uncertainties
from the MC simulations in Section 9.2. The breakdown of the systematic uncertainties and their
impact on the signal strength measurement for the inclusive VH(H→b¯b) process is shown in
Table12.4.
9.1 Experimental uncertainties
Experimental uncertainty is estimated during the calibration procedure of the object reconstruc-
tions and ﬂavour tagging as discussed in Chapter 4, from the detector performance study, and from
the trigger eﬃciency study. In this thesis, the uncertainties of ﬂavour tagging performance, the jet
energy scale (JES) calibration, and the jet energy resolution (JER) calibration have large impacts
on the property measurements of the VH(H→b¯b) process among the experimental uncertainties.
9.1.1 Jet reconstruction
The calibrations of reconstructed jets are described in Section 4.3.
For the small- Rjet, the uncertainties are estimated for the calibration of the JES [ 93], JER
[93], and Jet V ertex Tagger (JVT) [ 113]. Figure 9.1shows the uncertainties of JES, JER for the
small-Rjet. The uncertainty size of JES varies 1-5 %, and that of JER varies 0.5-1.5 %. The
dominant uncertainties in the JES (Figure 9.1(a)) are the systematic uncertainty of the in-situ
calibration such as modelling uncertainty of MC samples, and the variation comes from the jet
ﬂavour response between quark-initiated jet and gluon-initiated jet. The dominant uncertainty in
the JER (Figure 9.1(b)) is the systematic uncertainty of the in-situ calibration.
For the large-Rjet, the uncertainty of JES, JER, Jet Mass Scale (JMS), and Jet Mass Resolution

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
108 9.1 Experimental uncertainties
(JMR) are considered in this thesis, where they are derived for the in-situ calibration [ 99]. The
uncertainty size of JES varies 1-2 %, and that of JMS varies 2-10 %, while the precision of ratio
of the JER/JMR in data and simulation varies 10-15 % [ 99]. The contribution of the uncertainty
of large-Rjet is smaller than that of the small- Rjet in the measurement of signal strength of the
inclusiveVH(H→b¯b) process.
For the VR track jet, the uncertainty is not considered in this analysis, since the momentum of
track is not used in the Higgs candidate reconstruction and the uncertainty of ﬂavour tagging are
expected to cover the most e ﬀects of the uncertainties from the VR track jet reconstruction.
Fig. 9.1: Uncertainties of JES and JER for small- Rjet (anti-kt,R=0.4). The jet is reconstructed
with PFlow algorithm and calibrated with the JES. (a) shows the fractional JES uncertainty as a
function of jet pTatη=0. (b) shows the absolute uncertainty of JER as a function of jet pTat
η=0.2. The total uncertainty (blue ﬁlled shape) is derived as the quadrature sum of all sources
listed in the legend.
9.1.2 Flavour tagging
The calibration of scale factor in the ﬂavour tagging is described in Section 4.4. The ﬂavour
tagging eﬃciency is calibrated for b-jet,c-jet, and light jet, which are labelled in truth level. The
uncertainties of the scale factors, as shown in Figure 4.4, are considered separately for the di ﬀer-
ent jet ﬂavour, and are treated as uncorrelated between the small- Rjets and VR track jets. The
uncertainty is decomposed into several orthogonal systematic sources: 45 for b-jet, 20 for c-jet,
and 36 for light jet. The major uncertainties come from the di ﬀerence of the parton-shower MC
simulations and the statistics of the data used in the calibration.

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 109
9.1.3 Lepton reconstruction
The uncertainties estimated during the calibration of the candidate electrons [ 88] and the candi-
date muons [89], which assess all the uncertainties of reconstruction, identiﬁcation, and isolation,
are considered, in addition to the uncertainties from their trigger e ﬃciencies.
9.1.4 Emiss
T reconstruction
The uncertainties of the calibration of reconstructed leptons and jets are propagated into the
uncertainty of the reconstructed Emiss
T . The uncertainties of the energy scale and resolution of the
soft term in the Emiss
T reconstruction are also considered [ 107].
9.1.5 Other sources of experimental uncertainty
There are other experimental uncertainties that are considered in this thesis. For the electron and
muon trigger, uncertainties of their trigger scale factor (SF) are considered. For the mettrigger,
the uncertainty for trigger e ﬃciency and SF between MC samples and the data is evaluated from
theW+jets,Z+jets, andt¯tevents, which is inherited from the previous analysis [ 28]. Luminosity
is measured 140 ±1.2fb−1(0.83 %) [36] by LUCID-2 Cherenkov detector, which is located in the
forward region outside the A TLAS detector. The distribution of the mean number of interactions
per crossing (pile-up ⟨µ⟩) in MC samples is corrected with factor 1 .0/1.09 to match the distribution
in the data as discussed in Section 3.2.1. The uncertainty of the correction factor is estimated with
measured inelastic cross-sections [ 114] by varying the factor between 1 .0/1.0 and 1.0/1.18.
9.2 Modelling uncertainties
Modelling (theoretical) uncertainty is derived from the comparison between di ﬀerent theoret-
ical models in the MC simulations. In this thesis, the uncertainties of the VHsignal process,
V+jets background processes, and diboson background process have large impacts on the property
measurements of the VH(H→b¯b) process among the modelling uncertainties.
9.2.1 General strategy to derive modelling uncertainty
The uncertainties for the modelling are studied for both the background estimation and sig-
nal prediction. For the background estimation, both of the data and MC samples in background
dominant regions (CRs) are used to estimate and constrain the background process in the signal
sensitive regions (SRs) in the VH(b¯b) regimes. The deﬁnitions of CRs and SRs are described in
Section5.2, and the estimations and modelling studies of the di ﬀerent background processes are

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
110 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
discussed in Section 9.2.3. For the signal prediction, signal MC samples and the estimated back-
ground samples are utilized to explain the data in the SR in the VH(b¯b) regimes and signal MC
samples are used to extract the VH(H→b¯b) signal event yields from the data. The modelling
study of the VH(H→b¯b) signal process is discussed in Section 9.2.2. The signal prediction and
background estimation are performed in proﬁle likelihood ﬁt, whose methodology is discussed in
Chapter11.
InpV
T<400 GeV , the modelling uncertainties of the VH(H→b¯b) process and the VH(H→c¯c)
process is estimated coherently, with sharing the same modelling uncertainties of their background
processes. Since the boosted VH(b¯b) regime adopts di ﬀerent analysis technique from the resolved
VH(b¯b) regime and the VH(c¯c) regime, the modelling in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime is considered
separately from these two regimes. The modelling of events in pV
T>400 GeV in the VH(c¯c)
regime is studied by being included in pV
T>250 GeV region in the VH(c¯c) regime.
Apart from the nominal model that is explained in Section 3.2.2, alternative models are pre-
pared to ensure the accuracy of the physics process simulation. The modelling uncertainties are
derived by comparing the nominal model from the corresponding alternative models. The alter-
native models come from the alternative sets of parameters in the nominal MC generator, referred
to as “internal scale variation”, and from the alternative MC generators. The MC samples and the
internal scale variations for the di ﬀerent signal and background processes are summarised in Table
9.1. The alternative models are determined to disentangle di ﬀerent sources as much as possible.
The alternative MC samples are used to assess the impacts of Matrix Element (ME) or Parton
Shower (PS) computation variations. The internal scale variations are generally used to assess the
impacts of the renormalisation ( µR) scale, the factorisation ( µF) scale, Parton Distribution Function
(PDF), and strong coupling constant ( αs) variations. Additional internal scale variations such as
NLO electroweak (EW) corrections are also considered depending on the physics processes.
The modelling uncertainties are derived separately for every physics process, and they are fac-
torised into three kinds of e ﬀects: normalisation, relative acceptance, and shapes of distributions
of ﬁt variables deﬁned in Section 11.2.
Normalisation
Overall event yield in every MC sample of a certain physics process is normalised to the same
cross-section times branching ratio predicted by the SM. When the cross-section and branching
ratio are predicted from theoretical studies or other experiments, the event yield of a certain process
is normalised to that cross-section, and its uncertainty is applied as the normalisation uncertainty
for that process. This treatment is conducted for the VHsignal process and top quark background

=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 111
Table 9.1: Summary of nominal and alternative theoretical models for di ﬀerent physics processes.
The nominal MC generators, internal scale variations of the nominal generators, and alternative

processes. For the other background processes, the cross-section and branching ratio simulated
by the nominal MC generators is used for the normalisation. The overall acceptance uncertainty,
which is derived from the variations of event yield in the inclusive analysis phase space due to
the diﬀerent theoretical calculation models, is applied as the normalisation uncertainty. The value
of overall acceptance uncertainty is the sum in quadrature of uncertainties from all the available
alternative models, given by Eq. ( 9.2).
In the ﬁt, a normalisation factor is introduced to modify the event yield of the background pro-
cesses. The normalisation factors of the major background ( V+jets and top quark processes) are
left to ﬂoat in the ﬁt to match the data, referred to as “ﬂoating normalisation”, while the normalisa-
tion factors of minor background processes are ﬁxed at the MC predictions in the ﬁt. To estimate
a certain background contribution in the Signal Regions (SRs), where several physics processes
exist in the same region, the ﬂoating normalisation is set across the SRs and the dedicated Control
Regions (CRs) for that background process. The ﬂoating normalisation is evaluated in the CRs
with high purity of that background process, and propagated to the SRs. The same extrapolation
by the ﬂoating normalisation is performed across the lepton channels: from the 1- or 2-lepton
channel to the 0-lepton channel. The ﬂoating normalisation is split by the pV
Tregions and the num-
ber of jet regions as much as the data has enough statistics, since there are trends of the ﬂoating
normalisation between these regions as discussed in Section 12.1.2. The statistical uncertainty of
ﬂoating normalisation is considered in the ﬁt instead of the modelling uncertainty. The detailed
usage of the ﬂoating normalisation for every background process is described in the background
modelling parts below.

=== PDF PAGE 126 ===
112 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
Relative acceptance
There is a possible migration of the events from one region to another region caused by the vari-
ations of theoretical models. Especially, in the case of extrapolation across several regions, this
migration causes huge impact on the uncertainty of modiﬁcation of event yields in the extrapolated
region. To evaluate this migration, relative acceptance between the two regions under the relation
of extrapolation is derived. The simplest way to implement the relative acceptance uncertainty
in the ﬁt is to calculate the ratio of acceptance of the extrapolated region over the extrapolating
region, and to apply the value of its uncertainty to the extrapolating region. To assess the di ﬀer-
ence between the nominal and alternative models, the value of relative acceptance uncertainty is
calculated as a double-ratio given by Eq. ( 9.1).
Acceptance[Region B(alternative MC)]
Acceptance[Region B(nominal MC)]
/
Acceptance[Region A(alternative MC)]
Acceptance[Region A(nominal MC)]
, (9.1)
where region A (Region A) is the region with the highest purity of a certain background process,
and region B (Region B) is the extrapolated region. The direction of extrapolation (A →B) depends
on the contribution of each background process: when there is little di ﬀerence in the purity of a
physics process between both regions, the region with more event yield is assigned to the region
A. In case of extrapolation across three regions A, B, and C, in order of purity, the value is ﬁrst
computed from the highest purity region to the two less purity regions i.e. A →[B+C], and then
computed between these two less purity regions i.e. B →C.
The value of relative acceptance uncertainty applied in the ﬁt is the sum in quadrature of uncer-
tainties from all the available alternative models, given by Eq. ( 9.2). In this sum of uncertainties,
the uncertainty from the same kind of scale variation, e.g. QCD scale with six variations, is deﬁned
as the maximum shift in the envelope of these variations.
Total acceptance =
√
Σi(Acceptance unc vari
)2, (9.2)
where the “Acceptance unc vari” is an acceptance uncertainty due to the variation from the nominal
model to an alternative model vari.
Shapes of ﬁt variables
Shape uncertainty quantiﬁes the e ﬀects on the probability density function (p.d.f.) of variables
used in the ﬁt by comparing the available theoretical models. There are two ways to evaluate
shape uncertainty: comparing two models with a reweighting technique, or comparing two models
directly.

=== PDF PAGE 127 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 113
When comparing nominal and alternative MC samples which have a large di ﬀerence on their
statistics, event weights are applied to the shape of nominal MC sample to reproduce the shape
of alternative MC sample, which is referred to as “reweight”, and then the shape uncertainty is
estimated by comparing between the reweighted shape and the default shape of nominal MC sam-
ple. The details on the reweighting technique are described in Section 10.4.1. The event weight
is estimated by Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) framework [ 115]. More details on
the CARL are described in Section 10.4.3.
When comparing nominal and alternative MC samples which have small di ﬀerence on their
statistics, and when comparing nominal scale and internal systematic scales in the same nominal
MC sample, the shape uncertainty is estimated by comparing two models directly.
The shapes of alternative models in each analysis region are normalised to that of the nominal
model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and relative acceptance uncertainties.
9.2.2 Signal modelling
The signal prediction and the modelling study of the VH(H→b¯b) signal process are performed
based on the MC samples. The VH(H→b¯b) signal process contributes in the SRs in the resolved
and boosted VH(b¯b) regimes. The fractions of event yield of the signal MC samples in the di ﬀerent
lepton channels are shown in Figure 9.2, where the regions are deﬁned in Section 5.2. TheWH
signal events dominate in the 1-lepton channel, and also they contribute to the 0-lepton channel
due to mis-reconstruction of a lepton. The ZHsignal events with Z→ℓℓdecay dominate in the
2-lepton channel, and ZHsignal events with Z→ννdecay dominate in the 0-lepton channel,
while the ZHsignal events in the 1-lepton channel are negligible. The gg-initiatedZH,H→b¯b
signal processes are considered in 0-lepton and 2-lepton channels. These gg-initiated processes
make a larger contribution in multiple jet regions.
Major systematic sources for the modelling of signal MC samples are outlined as follows:
•QCD scale This systematic source assesses the variation of the range of perturbative QCD
computation and non-perturbative computation by changing the renormalisation scale ( µR)
and the factorisation scale ( µF) in the nominal MC sample of P owheg+Pythia8, which
are implemented as internal weights. The QCD scales vary with seven points:
[µR/µnominal

The scale [1,1] is the nominal scale, hence, there are six variations.

The normalisation, relative acceptance uncertainties are derived, which are optimised to the
ﬁducial diﬀerential cross-section measurement in the STXS framework, and shape uncertainty is
also considered in the ﬁt. The summary of the VHsignal uncertainty is shown in Table 9.2.
Normalisation uncertainty The normalisation uncertainty in the inclusive phase space is
derived from the uncertainties of cross-section and branching ratio. The inclusive cross-section
uncertainties for WH,q¯q→ZH,gg→ZHproduction modes due to QCD scale variations are
0.7%, 0.6%, and 25%, respectively [ 26]. The branching ratio uncertainty for the H→b¯bdecay is
1.61% [26].
STXS uncertainty The ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section of the VHsignal process is mea-
sured in the STXS framework as discussed in Section 1.3.2. The phase space of measurement
“STXS bin” is deﬁned in the framework, which is split by the transverse momentum of a vector
boson in truth level ( pV
T
,truth
) and the number of ISR jets at the truth level. Due to the systematic
variations in the signal MC models, the event migration across the STXS bins has happened. Gen-
erally, in this analysis, the relative acceptance uncertainty due to the event migration is evaluated
by the double-ratio of Eq. ( 9.1). However, this method allows to apply the relative acceptance
uncertainty into only the extrapolated region but not into the extrapolating region, i.e. this method
under-estimates the acceptance uncertainty in the extrapolating region, although the modiﬁcation
of cross-section in all STXS bins should be performed by the relative acceptance uncertainty. In
the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement, the uncertainty of event yields in each STXS
bin, referred to as “STXS uncertainty” [ 117], is considered by alternative method to take account
of the cross-section modiﬁcation for each STXS bin. This methodology can also be applied to the
relative acceptance uncertainty of the background processes for the event migration between pV
T
regions or between the number of jet regions in the reconstructed level, since ideally the relative
acceptance uncertainty should be applied to all these regions. For the uncertainty estimation with
high granularity, the diboson production adopts the same methodology for the relative acceptance
uncertainty of pV
Tand the number of jet regions in terms of QCD scale variations as discussed in

Section10.3.
The QCD scale variation is the largest uncertainty among the systematic sources. To evaluate the
STXS uncertainty by the QCD scale variation, Stewart-Tackmann (ST) method [ 118] is exploited.
In the ST method, the decrease of event yield in one region and the increase of event yield in
another region in the event migration are both considered in the calculation of uncertainty values,
where the 6 QCD scale variations are enveloped. The e ﬀects on the migration between pV
T
,truth
[75, 150, 250, 400, 600] boundaries are referred to as ∆75,∆150,∆250,∆400,∆600, and the migration
from 0 to 1 ISR jets and 1 to at least 2 ISR jets are referred to as ∆1and∆2. The overall cross-
section uncertainty ∆yis equivalent to the normalisation uncertainty above. Figure 9.3shows the
example of the derivation of STXS uncertainty from QCD scales by the ST method in the split of
pV
T
,truth

event yields, the migration from lower to higher STXS bins is only considered. For example, in
the case of migration across a boundary of pV

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 117
tainty. Since the impact of event migration due to these systematic sources is relatively small in
the ﬁt, the STXS uncertainty is simply computed by the relative changes of the cross-section in
each STXS bin separately, apart from the ST method.
In the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement in the STXS framework, the STXS uncer-
tainties are divided into two kinds: the relative acceptance uncertainty across the STXS bins, and
the normalisation uncertainty to modify the cross-section of each STXS bin. In the cross-section
measurement, the measured cross-section is not normalised by the SM predicted cross-section, so
the normalisation uncertainty of the VHsignal models is not included into the uncertainty of mea-
sured cross-section. Therefore, the normalisation STXS uncertainty is not included in the proﬁle
likelihood ﬁt as discussed in Section 11.1.1, and only considered in the SM predictions with which
the measured cross-sections are compared as shown in Table 12.3, while the relative acceptance
STXS uncertainty is considered in the measured cross-section. Since the gg-initiatedZHsignal
process are simulated at Leading Order (LO), and they provide large contribution in the multiple
jet regions as shown in Figure 9.2, the uncertainties of the SM predictions in the ≥1 truth ISR jet
STXS bin are larger than those of the other STXS bins.
Other uncertainty In the resolved VH(b¯b) regime, the High /Low-∆RCRs are deﬁned based
on the∆R(bb) variable, where is not deﬁned in the STXS bins. Hence, relative acceptance uncer-
tainties between SRs and ∆RCRs are estimated at the reconstructed level. Here, the QCD scale
variations are only considered in this SR-CR acceptance due to their large impacts on the ﬁt.
The shape uncertainties of QCD scale variations, and of di ﬀerence between P ythia8 and
Herwig7 are considered. The shapes of alternative models in each STXS bin are normalised
to that of the nominal model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and STXS un-
certainties.
9.2.3 Background modellings

MC samples, while top-quark production in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime
and multi-jet production in the 1-lepton channel are modelled with the data-driven technique. Fig-
ure9.4shows the fraction of di ﬀerent background events in the di ﬀerent SRs and CRs in each
lepton channel of the resolved or boosted regimes, where the regions are deﬁned in Section 5.2. In
the Figure 9.4(a) (b) (c) for the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes (pV
T<400 GeV), many of
the CRs are used to estimate the background process for the VH(H→b¯b) signal prediction, where
theVH(H→b¯b) signal events distribute mainly in the BB-tagged SR of the resolved VH(b¯b)

VH(b¯b) regimes. W+jets andZ+jets are considered separately. The contribution in the 2-lepton
channel is almost dominantly composed of the Z+jets events, while that in the 1-lepton channel
consists of almost the W+jets events. The contributions of both Z/W+jets events are present in the
0-lepton channel, where the Z+jets events are more dominant, and the W+jets events are entered
due to the failure of lepton reconstruction by mis-identiﬁcation or outside of detector acceptance.
Their MC events are categorised based on the ﬂavours of two Higgs-candidate jets at the truth
level, referred to as “ﬂavour composition”: “ V+hf” (heavy ﬂavours) for V+bbandV+cc,“V+mf”
(mixed ﬂavours) for V+bc,V+bl, andV+cl, and “V+lf” forV+jets with both light-ﬂavour
jets. TheV+bbevents contribute 85% to 95% of the V+jets background in the resolved VH(b¯b)
regime, while the fraction decreases to ∼50% in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime due to the loose
ﬂavour tagging requirement *1. Therefore, in the SRs of resolved and boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the
V+hfis the dominant V+jets background, while the V+mfandV+lfprovide small but not
negligible contributions. The V+hfevents are dominant in the High- ∆RCR (0-,2-lepton channel)
and the Low- ∆RCR (1-lepton channel) of the resolved VH(b¯b) regime. The V+mfevents are
dominant in the High- ∆RCRs of the C TCL-tag and CTN-tag regions in the VH(c¯c) regime. The
*1∼15% for the V+ccevents.

V+lfevents are dominant in the V+lightCR in theVH(c¯c) regime. The fractions of three ﬂavour
compositions are shown in Figure 9.4.
The normalisation factors of the V+jets events are free to ﬂoat in the ﬁt to the data, as explained
in Section9.2.1. In the overall analysis regions, the ﬂoat normalisation factors move independently
in diﬀerent jet multiplicity and pV
Tinterval regions, but the same factors are set across the lepton
channels. With this setup, the W+jets components in SRs and CRs in the 0-lepton channel are
constrained and extrapolated from the corresponding components in the 1-lepton channel, and
theZ+jets components in SRs and CRs in the 0-lepton channel are constrained and extrapolated
from the corresponding components in the 2-lepton channel. In the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c)
regimes, they are also split by three ﬂavour compositions, but the same factors are set across these
two regimes. With this setup, The V+hfcomponent is constrained by the High /Low-∆RCRs in
the resolved VH(b¯b) regime (BB-tag region), the V+mfcomponent is constrained by the High- ∆R
CRs of the CTN-tag regions in the VH(c¯c) regime, and the V+lfcomponent is constrained by the
V+light CRs of the C LN-tag regions in the VH(c¯c) regime, where the dedicated CRs are deﬁned in

MC sample (S herpa2.2.11) [77], which is explained in the systematic sources below. In the Low-

component are signiﬁcantly improved by adopting the MV A (“BDT CRLow”) as deﬁned in Section
8.1. Figure 9.5shows the BDT CRLow post-ﬁt distributions in Low- ∆RCR, which discriminates
theW+jets events from other events such as top quark backgrounds. Compared to a ﬁt with
only a single bin in the Low- ∆RCR, the statistical uncertainty of the W+hfﬂoat normalisation
decreases by a factor of two (by ∼30%) in the 75 <pV
T<150 GeV (150 <pV
T<250 GeV) region.
Considering the constraint in the 0-lepton channel from other lepton channels, in the High- ∆RCR
(BB-tag, CTN-tag) and V+light CR in the 0-lepton channel, only the event yield in each analysis
region is used in the ﬁt. In the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the ﬂoat normalisation factors are set
inclusively in the V+hfandV+mfevents, while dedicated normalisation uncertainty is derived
and applied for the V+lfevents due to its small contribution.
