Top quark process modelling in the 0,1-lepton channels
Top quark process is one of the major backgrounds in the SRs of the resolved and boosted
VH(b¯b) regimes*2in the 0-,1-lepton channels. As at least one Wboson in the top quark pro-
cess decays leptonically, the events enter into the 0- and 1-lepton channel, where the events in
the 0-lepton channel are present due to the failure of lepton reconstruction by mis-identiﬁcation
or outside of detector acceptance. Its contribution in this analysis increases as the number of jet
region increases, and decreases as pV
Tincreases. The top quark background consists of t¯tpro-
duction and single top ( t-channel,s-channel, and Wt-channel) productions. The major top quark
backgrounds are stemmed from t¯tproduction and single top Wt-channel production, which are
treated inclusively to minimise the impact of mutual interference between these two productions
given that they have the very similar ﬁnal state topology in this analysis phase space. The relative
fraction of the Wt-channel production varies from 5% to 15 % as pV
Tincreases in the resolved
and boosted VH(b¯b) regime. The single top t-channel production can be observed mainly in the
1-lepton channel in the resolved and boosted VH(b¯b) regimes, where its relative fraction of the top
quark background is ∼10 % in the low pV
TSRs. The single top s-channel production is negligible
in all the analysis regions. The modelling of t¯t+single topWt-channel and single top t/s-channels
are considered separately.
Thet¯tand single top Wt-channel backgrounds are categorised according to the ﬂavour com-
position of two Higgs-candidate jets: “Top( bb)” for the top quark process containing two b-jets,
“Top(bq)” for the top quark process containing a b-jet and either a c- or light ﬂavour jet from
theWboson decay, and “Top( qq)” for the top quark process containing only c- or light ﬂavour
jets from the Wboson decay. In the resolved VH(b¯b) regime, the Top( bb) component is a lead-
ing background and the Top( bq) component is a sub-leading background among all the top quark
backgrounds in the SRs. Here, the dedicated CRs for each of Top( bb) and Top(bq) components
play an important role for modelling both components in the same SRs, where the dedicated CRs
are deﬁned in Section 5.2.1. The Top(bb) component provides a dominant or large contribution in
the High-∆RCRs of both the lepton channels, since it has a feature of a large angle of ∆Rbetween
theb-tagged Higgs-candidate jets. The Top( bq) component provides the dominant contribution in
the Top CRs in the BC T-tag region, where its relative fraction in top quark backgrounds is up to
70%. In the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the Top( bb) and Top(bq) components are sub-leading back-
grounds in the SRs, and dominant backgrounds in the Top CRs. The Top( bq) component in the
boostedVH(b¯b) regime is a major top quark background, where its relative fraction in top quark
backgrounds is up to 85% in both the SRs and Top CRs, since the large- Rjet naturally captures
*2The top quark process in the VH(c¯c) regime (except BC T-tag region) is suppressed thanks to a veto on b-tagged jets.

=== PDF PAGE 138 ===
124 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
the full hadronic decay of a top quark. The Top( qq) component is negligible in the resolved and
boostedVH(b¯b) regime*3.
In the resolved V Hbbregime, the Top( bb) component and the combined Top( bq) and Top(qq)
component, referred to as “Top( bq/qq)”, are treated separately because the di ﬀerent dedicated
CRs are used for each. The fractions of the Top( bb), Top(bq/qq), and single top t/s-channel
components are shown in Figure 9.4(a)(b). In the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, all the Top( bb),
Top(bq), Top(qq) components are combined, referred to as “Top(boosted)”. The fractions of the
Top(boosted) and single top t/s-channel components are shown in Figure 9.4(d).
The nominal MC sample of the top quark background is P owheg+Pythia8. In the modelling
study and the estimation of systematic uncertainty of the top quark background, the systematic
sources come from two alternative MC samples, M adGraph5aMC@NLO +Pythia8 for ME
generator variation, P owheg+Herwig7 for PS generator variation, and the internal parameter
variation for the ISR and FSR in the alternative MC sample (M adGraph5aMC@NLO+Pythia8).
t¯tand single top Wt-channel modelling in the 0,1-lepton channels
The normalisation factors of the t¯tand single top Wt-channel events are free to ﬂoat in the ﬁt to
the data, as explained in Section 9.2.1. The ﬂoat normalisation factors move independently in
diﬀerent jet multiplicity and pV
Tinterval regions. They are also split by Top( bb) and Top(bq/qq)
components, but the same factors are set across both the 0-,1-lepton channels and across the re-
solvedVH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes. The Top( bb) component in the SR in the resolved VH(b¯b)
regime is constrained from the High- ∆RCR in the same regime, while the Top( bq/qq) component
in the SR in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime is constrained by the corresponding component in the
BCT-tag Top CR. In the case of the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the same ﬂoat normalisation factors
are set for the inclusive Top(boosted) component. The Top(boosted) component in the SR in the
boostedVH(b¯b) regime is constrained from the Top CR in the same regime. All the top quark
components in the 0-lepton channel are constrained by the 1-lepton channel. In the High- ∆RCR
of the 0-lepton channel, only the event yield in each analysis region is used in the ﬁt to constrain
the Top(bb) component in SRs, because the component in the 0-lepton channel is constrained from
the 1-lepton channel. On the other hand, in the High- ∆RCR of the 1-lepton channel, the pV
Tshape
distributions are used in the ﬁt to prioritise the V+jets modelling as discussed above. Since the
Top(bq) and Top(boosted) component tends to be reconstructed with the bquark and hadronic
Wboson decayed from the same top quark as explained in Figure 5.4, the invariant mass of two

The uncertainties are derived based on the general strategy explained in Section 9.2.1. For the
relative acceptance uncertainties, the acceptance di ﬀerences in the following inter-categories are
considered: the t¯t/Wt-channel, the Top( bb)/Top(bq/qq), the lepton channels, and the SR /CRs. The
shape uncertainties of all systematic sources are considered. For the alternative MC samples, the
CARL reweighting method is exploited to estimate their shape uncertainties.
Single top t-/s-channel modelling in the 0,1-lepton channels
The uncertainties are derived based on the general strategy explained in Section 9.2.1. The nor-
malisation uncertainties of each t-/s-channel are estimated from their MC models. The relative
*4The Large-Rjet naturally captures the all hadronic decay of a top quark.

=== PDF PAGE 140 ===
126 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
acceptance and shape uncertainties of t-channel is estimated. For the relative acceptance uncer-
tainties, the acceptance di ﬀerences in the following inter-categories are considered: the lepton
channels, the SR /CRs,pV
Tinterval regions, and jet multiplicity regions. The shape uncertainties
of all systematic sources are considered. For the alternative MC samples, the CARL reweighting
method is exploited to estimate their shape uncertainties.
Top quark process modelling in the 2-lepton channel
Top quark events, mainly di-leptonic t¯tevents, contribute in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved
VH(b¯b) regimes. The relative fractions of top quark backgrounds are shown in Figure 9.4(c),
labelled as “Top(2L)”. For the modelling of top quark process, the data-driven approach is used to
estimate the top quark background in the SR, and the Top eµCR as deﬁned in Section 5.2.2is ex-
ploited. Due to the very high purity of top quark background in the Top eµCRs (the fraction varies
99.5 % - 99.9 %), the distributions of data in the Top eµCRs can be used for the distribution of top
quark background in the SR with scale factors estimated by the MC simulation. The normalisation
uncertainty comes from these scale factors. In the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the t¯tcontribution is
negligible thanks to the cut on pV
T≥400 GeV and the Z boson mass selection requirement.
Dibson modelling
The diboson production consists of ﬁve processes: qq-initiatedWZ,ZZ,WW, andgg-initiated
ggWW,ggZZ. Because of the b-tagging event selection, the major diboson processes are WZ(W→
leptons,Z→b¯b), andZZ(Z→leptons,Z→b¯b) in the resolved and boosted VH(b¯b). These
processes contribute primarily into the SRs as shown in Figure 9.4, since it has the same topol-
ogy as the VHsignal production. They are exploited as the signal events in the VZsignal
strength measurement to validate the estimation of other background processes before the VH
signal strength measurements, as discussed in Section 1.4.3. In the resolved VH(b¯b) regime,
WZ(Z→b¯b),ZZ(Z→b¯b) processes contribute to ∼90% of the whole diboson production events,
while the ratio of these processes decreases to ∼60% in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime due to the
loose tagging requirement. The WZ(Z→b¯b) process distributes in the 0- and 1-lepton channels,
and theZZ(Z→b¯b) process distributes in the 0- and 2-lepton channels. The WZ(Z→b¯b) events
in the 1-lepton channel and the ZZ(Z→b¯b) events in the 2-lepton channel can be observed domi-
nantly, so these events extrapolate the corresponding diboson events in the 0-lepton channel. Since
the diboson production is a minor background and it doesn’t have any dedicated CRs as opposed
to the major background processes above, the normalisation factor of the diboson process in the
VHsignal ﬁt is ﬁxed to the SM prediction. The studies of the diboson modelling are discussed in

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 127
Chapter10.
Multi-jet modelling
The multi-jet background in the 1-lepton channel cannot be accurately estimated through MC
simulation due to practical limitations in generating su ﬃcient events for the analysis phase space.
Therefore, a data-driven approach has been adopted to estimate the impact of this background. This
approach extracts the MV A ﬁnal discriminant shape of the multi-jet background in the SR from
an orthogonal Control Region enriched with multi-jet events, referred to as “mJCR”. The mJCR is
deﬁned with the same event selection requirements as the SR except for the lepton isolation cut.
Since the isolation cut for the lepton suppresses the multi-jet events, the mJCR can be obtained by
requiring the inversion of the isolation cut. Because there are also the contributions of top ( t¯t,Wt)
and V+jets processes in the mJCR, the nominal multi-jet shape is formed by subtracting the MC
samples of these processes from the data. The uncertainty comes from the shape variations in the
several isolation cases or in the several lepton trigger cases, and the normalisation variations of the
MC samples.

Chapter 10
Improvement of systematic study for
diboson modelling
This chapter summarises the systematic study for diboson modelling. Section 10.1shows the
motivation of the diboson modelling study and strategy for the diboson modelling. Section 10.2
outlines the alternative models for the diboson process, and Section 10.3summarises the deviation
of normalisation, relative acceptance, and shape uncertainty. Particularly, a systematic study for
the shape uncertainty is improved by introducing a high performance estimation method; this is
discussed in Section 10.4.
10.1 Diboson modelling motivation and strategy
In the previous analysis, the modelling uncertainty of diboson process resulted in the second-
largest impact on the measurement of signal strength for the VH(H→b¯b) signal process among
the modelling uncertainties of the background processes [ 12,28], as shown in Table K.1. Besides,
in the validation analysis to measure signal strength of the VZ(Z→b¯b) process, the modelling un-
certainty of diboson process gives the largest impact on the measurement among all the modelling
uncertainties. In this thesis, the modelling study of the diboson production is motivated by the
following two points.
A.Update all the modelling uncertainties The phase space of this analysis is slightly
changed from the previous analyses [ 12,28], e.g. 75 <pV
T<150 GeV interval region
in the 1-lepton channel, and 4-jet region in the 0-lepton channel are added, besides >3-jet
region in the 2-lepton channel is split into 3-jet and >4-jet regions. Moreover, the nom-
inal MC sample is upgraded from S herpa2.2.1 to Sherpa2.2.11 by tuning the generator

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===
130 10.1 Diboson modelling motivation and strategy
parameters to adjust the bottom quark production and hadronisation properties to the world
average values [ 77,121], and by increasing the number of sample events that results in the
decrease of statistical uncertainty of MC sample by ∼50%. Therefore, all the modelling
uncertainties of the diboson process need to be re-estimated for this analysis.
B.Study the modelling uncertainty with high performance method In the previous analy-
ses, the modelling uncertainties of the diboson process are estimated by a bare minimal es-
timation method, which results in under-estimation or over-estimation of the uncertainties.
Especially, this problem is serious *1in the estimation of shape uncertainty from the alterna-
tive MC samples, as discussed in Section 10.4. Because the diboson modelling uncertainty
gives a large impact on the signal strength measurements of both the VH(H→b¯b) and
VZ(Z→b¯b) processes, the modelling uncertainty should be estimated properly by higher
performance estimation method than the previous method.
The diboson process consists of ﬁve production modes: qq-initiated WW,WZ,ZZ, andgg-
initiatedggWW,ggZZ. In this analysis, the following semi-leptonic decay processes contribute
to the property measurement of the VH(H→b¯b) process: ZZ(Z→leptons,Z→hadrons),
WZ(W→leptons,Z→hadrons),ZW(Z→leptons,W→hadrons), and WW(W→
leptons,W→hadrons). Flavour composition of hadrons decayed from a Z/Wboson is deﬁned
by the MC samples at the truth level. The ﬂavour composition of hadronic Zdecay consists of
Z→b¯b(Zbb),Z→c¯c(Zcc),Z→light-ﬂavour included jets ( Zbkg), while the hadronic Wdecay
(Whad) doesn’t contain b-jets (W→cl,W→ll, whereldenotes light-ﬂavour jets here). Among
these semi-leptonic diboson processes, WZbbandZZbbare the major processes in the resolved
and boosted VH(b¯b) regimes, and they concentrate in the SRs due to the topological similarity
with theVH(H→b¯b) signal process. The VZcc,VZbkg,VWhadprovide large contribution in the
VH(c¯c) regime, and small contribution in both the VH(b¯b) regimes. For the gg-initiated processes,
only theggZZbbprocess contribute less than 10% in the event fraction of diboson processes in the
resolvedVH(b¯b) regime, while the other gg-initiated processes are negligible. The detailed event
fractions of the di ﬀerent diboson processes in each region /regime are summarised in Appendix
H.1.
Because the diboson production is a minor background and it doesn’t have any dedicated CRs
as opposed to the other background processes, such as V+jets and top quark productions, the
normalisation factor of the diboson process in the VHsignal ﬁt is ﬁxed to the SM prediction in the
VH(H→b¯b) property measurements.
*1The estimation of relative acceptance uncertainty for pV
T, jet multiplicity regions has also this problem, as discussed
in Section10.3.2.

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 131
For the validation of this analysis, the WZbband (qq/gg)ZZbbprocesses are exploited as the
signal events in the VZ(Z→b¯b) signal strength measurement, which is discussed in Section 1.4.3.
Meanwhile, the other diboson processes are treated as background processes as ever. Hence, the
BDTVZdistributions of WZbbandZZbbprocesses have a peak around ∼1 BDT score, while
those of the other processes have a peak around ∼−1 BDT score. The distributions of BDT VZ
for diﬀerent diboson processes are shown in Appendix H.2. The same modelling uncertainties
are applied in both the signal strength measurement of VH(H→b¯b) process and VZ(Z→b¯b)
process.
10.2 Alternative models
The systematic sources for the modelling of qq-initiated diboson processes are outlined as fol-
lows:
•QCD scale This systematic source assesses the variations of the perturbative QCD com-
putation by changing the renormalisation scale ( µR) and the factorisation scale ( µF) in the
nominal MC sample of S herpa2.2.11, which are implemented as internal weights. The
QCD scales have six variations as shown in Eq. ( 9.3)
•Parton Distribution Function (PDF) +αs The uncertainties of Parton Distribution Func-
tion (PDF) and the strong coupling constant αsis evaluated using the PDF4LHC15 30 set
and following the “PDF4LHC recommendations for LHC Run II” [ 116]. A two-sided PDF
variation and a two-sided αsvariation are available as internal weights of the nominal MC
sample (Sherpa2.2.11), which is prepared by the “LHAPDF” tool [ 122].
•NLO electroweak (EW) correction The default Matrix Element (ME) computation of
Sherpa2.2.11 is conducted at next-to-leading (NLO) accuracy in QCD up to two extra par-
tons. An additional correction at NLO accuracy in electroweak (EW) is applied in the ME
computation for an accurate description of vector bosons [ 76,77], which takes an impor-
tant role in the BSM search by probing high- pV
Tphase space [ 123]. By applying the EW
correction, cross-section modiﬁcation in the high- pV
Tregion is conducted. The NLO EW
correction is calculated by O PENLOOPS[66] and implemented in S herpa2.2.11 in addi-
tion to the NLO QCD terms according to three schemes: “additive scheme”, “multiplicative
scheme”, and “exponentiated scheme”. Since the corrected pV
Tshape by the additive scheme
is the closest to the default NLO QCD shape, the additive scheme is adopted as the nominal
model, while the other two schemes are treated as systematic variations of the EW correc-
tion relative to the additive scheme. The cross-section computed in S herpa2.2.11 at NLO

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===
132 10.2 Alternative models
QCD accuracy is exploited for normalising the diboson events, but the NLO EW correction
is adopted in the nominal model, which results in the decrease of its cross-section. By ap-
plying the EW correction, the cross-section decreases ∼3% and the event yields decrease
byZZ:∼7.4%,WZ:∼3.8%, andWW:∼4.6%. Since this decrease is small enough against
the normalisation uncertainty below, the modiﬁcation of diboson event yields under the
normalisation uncertainty in the ﬁt would consider the e ﬀects of the EW correction. The
detailed implementations and e ﬀects on the ﬁnal results are discussed in Appendix H.3.1.
•ME+PS/UE/hadronisation (P owheg+Pythia8)To assess the computation variations
of ME, Parton Shower (PS), Underlying Event (UE), and hadronisation, the alternative MC
generator of P owhegME generator, interfaced to the P ythia8 PS/UE generator, is used
as the replacement of the nominal MC sample (S herpa2.2.11). The CT10 NLO set [ 124]
is used for the PDF of the hard scattering process, while the CTEQ6L1 PDF set [ 125] is
used for the PS /UE conﬁgured by the AZNLO tune. The E vtGenv1.2.0 program is used
for properties of the hadronisation of bottom and charm quarks. The P owheg+Pythia8
events are normalised to the cross-sections computed by this alternative MC generator at
NLO QCD accuracy.
•Fragmentation function of S herpa(Sherpa2.2.1) Besides adjusting the simulation pa-
rameters of the bottom quark decay process in the change of S herpaversion, its fragmen-
tation function is also changed [ 77]. This change results in a shift of the peak of invariant
mass of two b-jets toward higher mass in the VZbbprocesses between S herpa2.2.1 and
Sherpa2.2.11. This shift is also observed in the truth level. However, the modelling of frag-
mentation function is not well-calibrated with the data. Hence, in this analysis, S herpa2.2.1
is used as the fragmentation systematic variation in the S herpagenerator. The S herpa2.2.1
events are normalised to the cross-sections computed by this alternative MC generator at
NLO QCD accuracy. The detailed di ﬀerences of Sherpaversion and its e ﬀects on this thesis
are discussed in Appendix H.3.2.
Since the cross-section of gg-initiated processes are small comparing to the qq-initiated pro-
cesses, the systematic sources of the gg-initiated diboson processes have a small impact on this
analysis. Hence, the systematic uncertainty derived from diboson analyses in the A TLAS experi-
ment [126,127] is applied to the gg-initiated diboson processes. Because the relative acceptance
uncertainty and shape uncertainty must be derived in the dedicated phase space of this analysis,
only the normalisation uncertainty of gg-initiated diboson process is considered in this analysis.

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 133
10.3 Derivation of modelling uncertainty
According to the modelling strategy as discussed in Section 9.2.1, the normalisation, relative
acceptance, and shape uncertainties for the diboson process are derived.
10.3.1 Diboson normalisation uncertainty
For the diboson process, normalisation uncertainty is dominated by total acceptance uncer-
tainty in the inclusive analysis phase space. Normalisation uncertainty is derived separately for
ZZ,WZ,WW, andggVVproductions. For ZZandWZproductions, the total acceptance uncer-
tainties of the VZbbprocess in the resolved and boosted VH(b¯b) regimes are applied to all ZZ,
WZproductions, and the values in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime are shared in the VH(c¯c) regime
because the VZ(b¯b) process is dominant in the VH(b¯b) regime. For WWproduction, the total
acceptance uncertainties derived in the VH(c¯c) regime are used in all the regimes, because it’s
dominated in the VH(c¯c) regime. For ggVVproduction, the normalisation uncertainty that is used
in other A TLAS diboson analyses [ 126,127] is used. All the normalisation uncertainties are com-
puted by enveloping every kind of systematic sources with a quadrature-sum calculation by Eq.
(9.2). The derived values of normalisation uncertainties are shown in Appendix H.4.1.
10.3.2 Diboson relative acceptance uncertainty
Because the diboson modelling is estimated separately for ZZbb/ZZcc,WZbb/WZcc,ZZbkg,
WZbkg,ZWhad, andWWhadprocesses, the relative acceptance uncertainties across the analysis
categories are derived separately based on these di ﬀerent diboson processes. The uncertainties
ofVZbbprocess are derived in the VH(b¯b) regimes, since they are dominant among the diboson
processes in the SRs of the VH(b¯b) regimes. Since Z→b¯bandZ→c¯cdecays in the diboson pro-
cesses have the same topology and their di ﬀerences only come from the jet ﬂavour tagging, VZbb
andVZccprocesses in all three regimes share the same uncertainties derived in the corresponding
VH(b¯b) regimes for the VZbbprocess*2. For the other diboson processes in the resolved VH(b¯b)
andVH(c¯c) regimes, their uncertainties are derived in the VH(c¯c) regime because these processes
are rather dominant there, and their uncertainties are also applied in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime.
For the other diboson processes in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime, the uncertainties are derived and
applied in the same regime.
The relative uncertainties across the following analysis categories are considered. Generally, the
*2The same trends for the uncertainty values of VZccderived in the VH(c¯c) regime is observed. This decision is
motivated by harmonisation between the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes.

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
134 10.3 Derivation of modelling uncertainty
value of relative acceptance uncertainties of each systematic variation is calculated by Eq. ( 9.1),
except for the uncertainties between pV
Tregions and between the jet-multiplicity regions due to
the QCD scale variations. The relative acceptance uncertainties used for the ﬁt are derived by
enveloping every kind of systematic sources with quadrature-sum calculation by Eq. ( 9.2).
•Lepton channels Because the WZbb/WZcc,WZbkg, andWWhadevents are dominant in
the 1-lepton channels and the ZZbb/ZZcc,ZZbkg, andZWhadevents are dominant in the 2-
lepton channels, these events extrapolate the corresponding diboson events in the 0-lepton
channel. Hence, the relative acceptance uncertainties from the 1- to 0-lepton channel, or
from the 2- to 0-lepton channel for each diboson process are considered.
•SR and∆RCR The diboson process is distributed across the SRs and ∆RCRs. The SRs
in the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes have more diboson events than the High- ∆R
CRs and Low- ∆RCRs, so the extrapolation uncertainties from SR to ∆RCR are applied.
In the 1-lepton channel, the Low- ∆RCR is extrapolated from SR, and the High- ∆RCR is
extrapolated from an inclusive region of SR and Low- ∆RCR. The values are calculated in
the diﬀerent diboson processes and the di ﬀerent lepton channels.
•Jet multiplicity ( Njet) regions This uncertainty is extrapolated from a low number of
jets (Njet) region and applied to a high Njetregion. Only the Njetuncertainties in the re-
solvedVH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes are considered. The values are calculated in di ﬀerent
processes and di ﬀerent lepton channels. The QCD scale variation is not considered in the
uncertainties of the WZbb/ccandZZbb/ccprocesses, since they are estimated separately
as discussed below.
•pV
Tinterval regions The 150 <pV
T<250 GeV interval region is always selected as
the high purity region and extrapolating to other pV
Tregions in the resolved VH(b¯b) and
VH(c¯c) regimes. The pV
T>600 GeV interval region is extrapolated from the 400 <pV
T<
600 GeV interval region in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime. The values are calculated in the
diﬀerent diboson processes and the di ﬀerent lepton channels. The QCD scale variation is
not considered in the uncertainties of the WZbb/ccandZZbb/ccprocesses, since they are
estimated separately as discussed below.
•pV
T⊗Njetregions with ST method for QCD scale variation The relative acceptance un-
certainties between pV
T⊗Njetregion for the WZbb/ccandZZbb/ccprocesses due to QCD
scale variations are considered by the same methodology as the STXS uncertainty, which is
discussed in Section 9.2.2, since the QCD scale variations may cause large yield migration
across these regions. These uncertainties are computed with “Steward-Tackmann” (ST)
method. This is the ﬁrst time to exploit the ST method in the background process in this

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 135
analysis. This ST method [ 118] helps to evaluate the approximate yield migration uncer-
tainty, which is usually under-estimated with the default method Eq. ( 9.1), and it proves
its full potential when enveloping the several variations from the same source to evaluate
each migration uncertainty; Enveloping 6 variations of QCD scale in this case. The values
forpV
Tmigration uncertainties are computed for di ﬀerent diboson processes in the resolved
VH(b¯b) regime. The values for Njetmigration uncertainties are computed in each pV
Tinter-
val region for di ﬀerent diboson processes in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime.
The detailed calculation and the derived values of relative acceptance uncertainties are shown in
AppendixH.4.1.
10.3.3 Diboson shape uncertainty
The shape uncertainties of the ﬁt variables, which are explained in Section 11.2, for diﬀerent
systematic sources, as listed below, are estimated separately. The PDF +αsvariation is negligible.
The shapes of alternative models in each analysis region are normalised to that of the nominal
model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and relative acceptance uncertainties.
The shape plots of the ﬁnal discriminant variables (BDT VHand BDTVZ) for the diﬀerent systematic
sources are shown in Appendix H.4.2.
•QCD scale variation This variation is evaluated by internal weights of the nominal MC
sample Sherpa2.2.11. The variation of renormalisation scale ( µR) and factorisation scale
(µF) by a factor 2 to the nominal scales, which is evaluated in the ME only due to shape
ﬂuctuation, are considered for the shape uncertainty. It is applied to all the SRs and ∆R
CRs, and decorrelated by the boundary of pV
T=400 GeV .
•NLO Electroweak (EW) correction variation This variation is evaluated by internal
weights of the nominal MC sample S herpa2.2.11. The multiplicative scheme of the EW
correction is considered as shape uncertainty relative to the additive scheme that is adopted
as the nominal EW correction, because it’s the largest one of the two available alternative
combination schemes. It is applied to all the SRs and ∆RCRs, and decorrelated by the
boundary of pV
T=400 GeV .
•ME, PS/UE/hadronisation variation This variation is evaluated by the comparison of
Powheg+Pythia8 and Sherpa2.2.11. Since the shape of P owheg+Pythia8 is not
corrected at NLO EW, it is compared with that of S herpa2.2.11 without the additional
NLO EW correction. To estimate the shape uncertainty of the ﬁt variables by suppressing
the shape ﬂuctuations of P owheg+Pythia8, the shape of P owheg+Pythia8 is reproduced

by reweighting the shape of S herpa2.2.11 exploiting the CARL method as described in

10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty
between MC generators
As explained in Section 9.2.1and Section 10.3.3, the shape variations of ﬁt variables between
nominal and alternative MC samples are estimated by reweighting technique. In this section, to
examine the estimation method of the shape uncertainty for an alternative MC sample, the ZZ
diboson production mode in the 2-lepton channel of the resolved VH(b¯b) regime is focused, and
the variation of S herpa2.2.11 and Powheg+Pythia8 is used.
10.4.1 Improvement of reweighting technique
For the modelling study of the physics process, the MC sample is used for sampling the probabil-
ity density functions (p.d.f.) f(x) of the analysis variable, where x={x1,x2, ...}are the kinematic
variables (e.g. mbb,pV
T). Here, the p.d.f.s vary between the nominal MC sample f(x|y=0) and
the alternative MC sample f(x|y=1), and this is considered as the shape uncertainty. By learning
*3In theVH(c¯c) regime, to assess the shape uncertainty of Zcc,Zbkg,Whadtoo,ZZin the 0-,2-lepton channels, WZ
in the three lepton channels, and WWin the 0-,1-lepton channels are decorrelated.

=== PDF PAGE 151 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 137
these kinematic variables as BDT inputs, the BDT ﬁnal discriminant scores (BDT VH, BDTVZ) are
deﬁned as discussed in Chapter 8. As described in Section 11.2, not only the kinematic variables,
the BDT ﬁnal discriminant scores (BDT VH, BDTVZ) are also used as the ﬁt variables, which are
considered as the shape uncertainty in the modelling. For example, Figure 10.1(a) shows the
shape uncertainty of BDT VHhistogram between the nominal MC generator of S herpa2.2.11 and
the alternative MC generator of P owheg+Pythia8, after normalising both histograms with their
event yields in a certain region. The bottom panel of Figure 10.1(a) is a ratio of the normalised
alternative MC model relative to the normalised nominal MC model, referred to as “shape-shift”,
and shape-shift is applied as the nuisance parameter of shape uncertainty in the ﬁt, which is deﬁned
in Section11.1.1.
Due to limited computational resources, it’s impractical to prepare the MC samples of equal
size (unweighted statistics) for all the MC models in a physics analysis. Thus, the size of alter-
native MC sample is generally less than half of the size of nominal MC sample, e.g. the size of
Sherpa2.2.11 is∼16 times larger than that of P owheg+Pythia8. This results in the limitation
of statistical precision of the shape variation in each bin of a histogram in the ﬁt, which causes
the ﬁt stability issue. Moreover, this results in the large statistical ﬂuctuation of shape as shown
in Figure10.1(a), which causes over-estimation of the modelling uncertainty in the property mea-
surements. Especially, the large statistical ﬂuctuation of BDT VH ∼1 shape of the background
process provides large impact on the VH(H→b¯b) signal strength measurements.
To tackle the issues above, it’s necessary to have a strategy to approximate the shape of alter-
native model by suppressing its statistical ﬂuctuation. As the e ﬀective strategy, additional event
weightw(x) is estimated event by event, and applied to the nominal MC sample to reproduce the
shape of the alternative MC sample so that the shape variation would be evaluated with the same
statistics, which is referred to as “reweight”, given by
f(x|y=0)→w(x)·f(x|y=0)∼f(x|y=1). (10.1)
Figure10.1(b) illustrates the strategy of the reweighting technique. This reweighting technique
is a well-established method in particle physics that is used in the estimation of shape uncertainty
[115].
To get the p.d.f.s of the reweighted nominal MC model (blue line) closer to that of the alternative
MC sample (red line), the ideal weight w(x) should be estimated as the ratio
w(x)=
f(x|y=1)
f(x|y=0)
, (10.2)
wherew(x) andf(x) are multi-dimensional probability distribution functions. When applying the
event weight w(x) to the analysis variables of nominal MC sample f(x|y=0), the weight w(x)

=== PDF PAGE 152 ===
138 10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty between MC generators
must be converted to one-dimensional p.d.f. Here, the w(x) cannot be derived by the ratio of the
BDT output (one-dimensional p.d.f.) of nominal and alternative MC samples since eventually
the statistical ﬂuctuation is included in the ratio, so the w(x) should be estimated by some input
kinematic variables x. Then the w(x) corresponding to the events in bins with low statistics bins
(e.g. BDTVH ∼1) can be parameterised by the input variables x in bins with enough statistics.
However, since there are numerous dimensions for the BDT input variables xand the xhave
correlation with each other, the derivation of the ratio Eq. ( 10.2) is non-trivial in the estimation of
w(x) with the limited size of samples in the one-dimensional p.d.f. format.
In the previous analysis, because of time constraints, the event weights w(x) of the diboson
events are parameterised by only one mbbkinematic variable, whose methodology is discussed in
Section10.4.2.
To reweight the shape of BDT ﬁnal discriminant score more accurately, it’s demanded to con-
sider the shape variations of all the BDT input variables and to parameterise the event weights w(x)
with all the BDT input variables, called as “multi-dimensional parameterisation”. The accurate es-
timation and implementation of the event weight w(x) is diﬃcult with the limited statistics of MC
samples. Here, since the BDT input variables have multi-dimensional correlation, a likelihood
function (multi-dimensional probability density function) is the most e ﬀective tool that can learn
all the variations of input variables and their correlations and that can provide one-dimensional
output, In this analysis, the event weights w(x) are derived by multi-dimensional parameterisation
using a deep neural network (NN) technique *4in CARL framework [ 115] as discussed in Sec-
tion10.4.3. This new reweighting method strongly satisﬁes the motivation [B.] of the diboson
modelling study explained in Section 10.1.
10.4.2 mbb-based reweighting
The simplest way to derive the event weight w(x) is to calculate the ratio of Eq. ( 10.2) by the
p.d.f. of the most e ﬀective kinematic variable for considering the shape variations of analysis
variables including the BDT ﬁnal discriminant scores. Because the mbbis the most discriminant
kinematic variable in the BDT input variables, the e ﬀect ofmbbshape variation is most propagated
to the shape variation of the BDT ﬁnal discriminant scores. Therefore, in this reweighting method,
thembbis chosen to parameterise the event weight w(xmbb) in one dimension to approximate the
w(x):w(xmbb)→w(x).
*4BDT-based reweighting method was available as the multi-dimensional parameterisation in the previous analyses.
The CARL is preferably implemented in the analysis framework, and has more accuracy (e.g. for learning pV
T
shape).

10.4.3 Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) reweighting
Mechanism and training model
The Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) [ 115] is a framework to estimate the weight
w(x) based on the deep neural network (NN). The NN is reconstructed from a Multi-Layer Per-
ceptron (MLP), and is trained with kinematic variables including all the BDT input variables xto

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 141
distinguish between the nominal events ( y=0) and alternative events ( y=1) as a binary classiﬁ-
cation model. The output of the trained NN D(x) for an event with all the BDT input variables xis
given as one-dimensional continuous score y, where the output score of the MLP is calculated by
the Softmax function to derive the score between 0 to 1. As the number of events Nin the training
increases, the output score D(x) approximates to the probability that the event with xis classiﬁed
as the alternative MC model P(y=1|x), given by Eq. ( 10.5).
D(x)
N−→∞
−− − − −→P(y=1|x). (10.5)
Hence, the output D(x) can be used as an estimator for the probability P(y=1|x), which is
the advantage of using the CARL method in the estimation of the weight w(x) under the multi-
dimensional parameterisation.
The p.d.f. f(x|y=0) of the nominal MC events and f(x|y=1) of the alternative MC events
can be expressed with the probability as a function of x:P(x),
f(x|y=0)=P(x|y=0), (10.6)
f(x|y=1)=P(x|y=1). (10.7)
The ratio from Eq. ( 10.2) can then be rewritten as
w(x)=
f(x|y=0)
f(x|y=1)
=
P(x|y=0)
P(x|y=1)
. (10.8)
Using the Bayes theorem, Eq. ( 10.8) can be turned into
w(x)=
P(y=0|x)
P(y=1|x)
P(y=1)
P(y=0)
. (10.9)
Since both the nominal and alternative MC events are normalised to the same value, the terms
P(y=1) andP(y=0) are canceled out. Using unitarity, the ratio can be expressed as
w(x)=
1−P(y=1|x)
P(y=1|x)
. (10.10)
An estimator w∗(x) forw(x) can be then computed directlyfrom the trained NN output as
w∗(x)=
1−D(x)
D(x)
, (10.11)
sincew∗(x)−→w(x) because of Eq. ( 10.5). This estimator w∗(x) for the ratio is used in the event
reweighting as described in Section 10.4.1.
Training conﬁguration
The training in the CARL framework exploits “carl-torch” [ 128], which is the NN package
based on PyTorch. The setup in the NN is listed as follows.

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===
142 10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty between MC generators
•MLP: 3 layers with the number of neurons equivalent to the number of input kinematic
variables
•Activation function in the MLP: LeRU function in hidden modes, Softmax function in out-
put
•Loss function: categorical cross-entropy
•The number of training iterations (epoch): 300 times
The epoch number is optimised to maximise the reweighting performance such as closure dis-
cussed in Section 10.4.4.
The training in the CARL is done for each diboson production separately in each lepton channel
and in each regime, while the training is done inclusively in pV
T, the number of jet *5, and SR/∆RCR
regions. The input variables in the CARL training are the BDT input variables and auxiliary
variables (the number of jets, truth ﬂavour composition). The sample used in the CARL training is
produced by using the truth tagging method as described in Section 4.4.4to increase the number
of eventsNfor better traing performance, while the diboson sample in the ﬁt is produced by using
the direct tagging method to get rid of bias from the truth tagging. 75% of sample events are used
for the training, while 25% of events are used as the test sample for the validation.
Training validation
The training performance is conﬁrmed with the ROC curve. The trained NN is validated with
the transition of loss function as a function of the number of epochs, and with the closure between
the shape of reweighted nominal MC events and that of alternative MC events as discussed in
Section10.4.4.
10.4.4 Performance comparison between CARL and mbb-based reweighting
Evaluating and quantifying the closure
The goal of reweighting technique is to make the p.d.f. of the reweighted nominal MC sample
as close as possible to the p.d.f. of the alternative MC sample. The closure between the reweighted
nominal histogram and the alternative histogram is the most important evaluation metric of the
reweighting performance. The closure is assessed visually by residuals of each bin in the his-
togram, and is also quantiﬁed by the χ2test.
The residual represents the deviation of the entry of reweighted nominal histogram from the
*5If the reweighting performance is not qualiﬁed for the ﬁt, e.g. the closure of reweighted nominal and alternative
samples is bad, the pV
T, or the number of jet region is split in the training.

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 143
entry of alternative histogram in each bin, which is deﬁned as
residuali=
w·Nomi−Alti
√
σ(w·Nomi)2+σ(Alti)2
, (10.12)
whereNomis a histogram of the nominal MC sample, Altis a histogram of the alternative MC
sample,irepresents the ith bin in the histogram, wis the event weight estimated in the reweighting
technique, and σ(X) is a standard deviation (statistical error) of entry from a histogram X. As the
criteria of good closure, the residual in all bins should be within ±3σ, since the deviation over 3 σ
would be treated as a di ﬀerent shape from the original shape of alternative MC sample in the ﬁt,
while the deviation within 1 σis the best closure.
Theχ2value for two histograms is deﬁned as:
χ2=

whereniandaiare entries in the i-th bin of histograms of nominal and alternative MC samples,

iai. Theχ2value follows approximately the χ2
(r−1)distribution.
For two identical histograms, χ2is zero. For two di ﬀerent histograms but having almost the same
distribution, χ2
(r−1)/(r−1) is expected to be around 1.0, where ( r−1) is sometimes referred to as
the number of degrees of freedom (n.d.f.). The χ2/n.d.f.value is reported in the closure plots as
below. The χ2/n.d.f.value closer to ≈1.0 shows the better closure.
Closure of BDT input variables
To verify whether the derived event weight considers the shape variations of the BDT input
variables, the p.d.f. of all the BDT input variables are reweighted. Besides the mbb, the invariant
mass of two leptons mℓℓis one of the BDT input variables that have a large variation between the
nominal (Sherpa2.2.11) and alternative (P owheg+Pythia8) MC sample, as shown in the shape-
shift plot of Figure 10.3. Therefore, for here, the shape variation of mℓℓshould be considered to
estimate the event weight w(x).
Figure10.4and10.5show the closure of mbbandmℓℓ, respectively, in comparison between the
case of applying mbb-based weight w(xmbb) and applying CARL weight w∗(x). Comparing the case
of CARL weight from the case of mbb-based weight, the residuals of each bin get closer to 0 σ,
and theχ2score decreases toward 1 by ∼23% formbb, and∼47% formℓℓ. With these results, the
shape variation of mℓℓis properly considered in the CARL weight w∗(x). For the mbb, the closure
is better in the CARL weight, which may be caused by the correlation of the BDT input variables.
Besides the closure of mbbandmℓℓ, the closures of other BDT input variables, except the pV

Uncertainty impact on the ﬁt
As the results of the higher performance approximation of the shape of alternative MC sample
exploiting the CARL reweighting method, the shape uncertainty, which is equivalent to the shape-
shift, can be estimated with a more proper systematic shape in a smooth form. Figure 10.8and

which would result in the decrease of impact of the shape uncertainty on the VH(H→b¯b) signal
strength measurement. In the case of the BDT VZ, the shape-shift of the BDT VZincreases, which
would result in the increase of impact of the shape uncertainty on the VZ(Z→b¯b) signal strength
measurement.

for bothVH(H→b¯b) andVZ(Z→b¯b) processes with the Asimov dataset are conducted in the
dedicated region: in the 2-lepton channel in resolved VH(b¯b) regime. The expected changes on the
overall diboson modelling uncertainty (and shape uncertainty against P owheg+Pythia8) can be
observed in the breakdown of uncertainties in the signal strength measurements as shown in Table

above that the CARL reweighted shape approximates the shape alternative MC sample in higher
performance. As the e ﬀective performance of the CARL reweighting method is conﬁrmed in the
estimation of shape uncertainty between the nominal and alternative MC samples, this method is
adopted in the ﬁnal results as explained in Section 9.2.1and Section 10.3.3.

Chapter 11
Statistical description of data
This chapter describes the usage of proﬁle likelihood ﬁt in this analysis in Section 11.1, the ﬁt
model and method used in the ﬁt in Section 11.2, and validation of the ﬁt in Section 11.3.
11.1 General description of proﬁle likelihood ﬁt
In this analysis, a proﬁle likelihood ﬁt is exploited to explain the data with the simulated samples
considering statistical and systematic uncertainties, and to perform the background estimation and
the signal prediction. A likelihood function used in the proﬁle likelihood ﬁt is deﬁned in Section
11.1.1. The evaluation of the statistical quantities from the best ﬁt likelihood, such as derivation
of the signiﬁcance over the background-only hypothesis, is discussed in Section 11.1.2.
11.1.1 Deﬁnition of likelihood function
The likelihood function consists of three kinds of terms.
As the ﬁrst term, Poissonian probability is introduced to compare the data event yields and
expected event yields in all the bins.
L(⃗µ)=
∏
i′bins
Pois(Ni|⃗µsi+bi)=
∏
i′bins
(⃗µsi+bi)Ni
Ni!
e−(⃗µsi+bi), (11.1)
whereNiis the number of measured data event yields in ithbin,siis the signal event yields expected
by the MC simulation within the SM prediction, and biis the background event yields expected by
the MC simulation and the data-driven method. For the signal prediction, a Parameter of Interest
(POI) is deﬁned for di ﬀerent signal processes, where its conﬁguration for this analysis is explained
in Section 11.2.µis the signal strength, which is deﬁned as Eq. ( 1.15), and one µis measured in

=== PDF PAGE 166 ===
152 11.1 General description of proﬁle likelihood ﬁt
the corresponding POI. A set of all the POIs ( ⃗µ) is measured simultaneously in the ﬁt.
In order to consider the systematic uncertainty in the ﬁt, nuisance parameters (NP) are imple-
mented according to the systematic uncertainties given in Section 9.2. A set of NPs with their
predeﬁned variations is denoted as ⃗θ. The predeﬁned variation sizes are derived from the cal-
ibration processes for the experimental uncertainties, and the comparison between nominal and
alternative models of the MC simulations for the theoretical (modelling) uncertainties. These NPs
modify the expected signal and background event yields while the Poisson term ( 11.1) performs
the ﬁt:{si,bi}→{si(⃗θ),bi(⃗θ)}. The NPs of the same systematic source are correlated across the re-
gions as many as possible to provide the e ﬀect of the same systematic source into the ﬁt with fewer
parameters for the ﬁt convergence. The NPs of all the experimental uncertainties are fully corre-
lated in all the analysis categories. On the other hand, the NPs of some theoretical uncertainties
are decorrelated between the categories. As the NPs are decorrelated in ﬁner regions, the agree-
ment of the data and the MC simulations improves, since the modiﬁcations of the expected event
yields in the di ﬀerent ﬁne regions can be performed. The de-correlation should be used as long as
the systematic e ﬀects are well understood. For example, when the e ﬀects of the NPs of the same
systematic source are known to be di ﬀerent in the di ﬀerent regions, these NPs are decorrelated
between certain regions. Moreover, when the source of systematic uncertainty is considered by
diﬀerent ways in the di ﬀerent regimes or lepton channels, the NPs of that systematic uncertainty
are decorrelated between the regimes and lepton channels in the ﬁt.
As the second term, Gaussian constraint term as Eq. ( 11.2) is introduced to express the variation
size of the NPs ( ⃗θ).

The nominal value of an NP ( θ) is 0, and the predeﬁned variation size of an NP corresponds to the
1 standard deviation ( σθ) of Eq. (11.2). A NP can be more constrained during the ﬁt due to some
correlations with other ﬁt parameters and so on. This causes a decrease of the variation size of an
NP .
The normalisation factors (NFs) are also introduced as the ﬁt parameter to estimate the ﬂoating
normalisation of the top and V +jets background across the SRs and CRs, The NFs are left uncon-
strained in the ﬁt and are deﬁned purely from data in the CRs. A set of NFs is denoted as ⃗τ, and
modiﬁes the certain background event yields: {bi}→{bi(δτ)}. In order to help deﬁne the NFs( ⃗τ)
from a CR, a ﬁtting variable is introduced to get background pure bins in the histogram, which is
also discussed in Section 11.2. The pre-ﬁt values of NFs( ⃗τ) are set to 1.

11.1.2 Evaluation of statistical quantities
The proﬁle likelihood ratio can be deﬁned to test a certain hypothesis with the ﬁxed value µ≃*1
[129]
λ(µ)=
L(µ≃,
ˆˆ⃗θ,ˆˆ⃗γ,ˆˆ⃗τ)
L(ˆµ,ˆ⃗θ,ˆ⃗γ,ˆ⃗τ)
, (11.6)
where{ˆµ,ˆ⃗θ,ˆ⃗γ,ˆ⃗τ}are the best-ﬁt values with the data, and {
ˆˆ⃗θ,ˆˆ⃗γ,ˆˆ⃗τ}are the best-ﬁt values when µ≃
is ﬁxed. The proﬁle likelihood ratio has the range [0,1]. The ratio close to 1 represents the good
agreement between the hypothesis µ≃and the data, while the ratio close to 0 represents a large
disagreement.
With the proﬁle likelihood ratio, a test statistic qµ≃is deﬁned as
qµ≃=

154 11.2 Analysis speciﬁc ﬁt model and method
Here, the background-only hypothesis is considered, which means the case of µ≃=0 is tested.
The test statistic is deﬁned as q0in Eq. ( 11.7). In order to check the comparison between the
observed data and the background-only hypothesis, p-value can be used, which is deﬁned as
p0=
∫∞
q0,obs
f(q0|0)dq0 (11.8)
wheref(q0|0) is the probability density function of q0under the assumption of µ=0 andq0,obsis
the value of q0which is observed with the data.
With this p-value, signiﬁcance Zcan be deﬁned by the number of Gaussian standard deviations
over the background-only hypothesis as
Z=Φ−1(1−p0), (11.9)
whereΦ−1is the inverse of Gaussian cumulative distribution function. The signal sensitivity of
each POI(µ) can be evaluated with this signiﬁcance Z. If only the statistics is considered while
calculating the signiﬁcance, and if Eq. ( 11.1) is substituted for Eq. ( 11.6), then the signiﬁcance
Zis equivalent with the Asimov signiﬁcance Eq. ( 8.6). The particle physics community tends to
reject the background-only hypothesis above Z=5, and treats the signal process as ”discovery”.
11.2 Analysis speciﬁc ﬁt model and method
While determining the best ﬁt values of the ﬁt parameters in the ﬁt, the SRs and CRs are consid-
ered simultaneously, and the background estimation is performed, followed by the signal extrac-
tion for both the signal strength (inclusive cross-section) measurement and the ﬁducial di ﬀerential
cross-section measurement. The di ﬀerence between these two measurements is only the degree of
freedom of the POI in the signal extractions as shown in Figure 11.1. In this section, the speciﬁc
ﬁt models for this analysis such as the POI, variables in CRs, and BDT optimal variable in SRs are
explained, followed by the ﬁt methods such as pruning NPs, and unblinding sensitive data.

In order to avoid making empty or very low statistic bins, at least 3 signal and background events
are required in an optimised bin. Also, the optimisation is designed to ensure that the statistical
uncertainty of the simulated background in each bin is less than 20 %.
Pruning the nuisance parameters
Several nuisance parameters have a negligible impact on the ﬁt. In order to converge the ﬁt with
less computation time, these parameters are pruned. The pruning criteria are as follows:
•Systematic uncertainties inducing a normalisation e ﬀect smaller than 0.5 % of the nominal
event yields.
•Shape uncertainties are neglected if none of the bins in the distribution has a deviation over
0.5 % of the nominal shape distribution after the overall normalisation.
•For very small background processes, both the shape and normalisation uncertainties are
neglected depending on whether it’s in the sensitive regions. In the sensitive regions, the
uncertainties are pruned only if these process event yields are less than 2 % of the signal
event yields, while, in non-sensitive regions, the uncertainties are pruned if they have an

=== PDF PAGE 173 ===
Chapter 11. Statistical description of data 159
impact lower than 0.5 % of the total background event yields.
After the pruning, the total number of NPs ( ⃗θ) is∼560 in both VHﬁt andVZﬁt.
Blinding the signals
In order not to be biased by the data when the analysis method or the systematic uncertainties
are implemented, the analysis is proceeded by blinding the signal information as follows.
•Data is not superimposed in a histogram bin if the expected simulated signal comprises
greater than 5 % of the total simulated signal and background event yields in the bin.
•Higgs (VH) signal strength is ﬁxed to the SM prediction ( µ=1) in the ﬁt with data.
•Same treatments are done for the diboson ( VZ) event, which is used as the signal in the
cross-check analysis.
Once, all the necessary systematic uncertainties are ready and the ﬁt seems to be converged, ﬁrst,
theVZsignal is unblinded. If no ﬁt issue is found, the VHsignal is unblinded.
11.3 Fit validation
Asimov datasets
Before performing the ﬁt on the real dataset, it’s important to know how the numerous sys-
tematic uncertainties a ﬀect the POI especially when blinding the signals. To obtain such knowl-
edge, pseudo dataset can be useful, which corresponds to the sum of all simulated signal and
background samples, referred to as “Asimov dataset”. A ﬁt, which measures the expected sig-
nal strength ( µ=1), can be performed with the Asimov dataset, referred to as “Expected ﬁt”.
When the uncertainties of signal strength are evaluated in a performance study of new analysis
method, the expected ﬁt has the advantage of knowing the pure impact of the uncertainties, since
the uncertainties are scaled by µvalue.
Correlation matrix
Because the likelihood is a function of the multiple parameters, the ﬁt parameters a ﬀect each
other in the multi-dimensional space, referred to as the correlation. The correlation of two arbitrary
parameters can be derived from the covariance matrix that is given by the inverse of the Hesse
matrix around the point of maximal likelihood [ 129]. When several ﬁt parameters explain the data
in the same bin, this correlation can be used to comprehend the features of data.

=== PDF PAGE 174 ===
160 11.3 Fit validation

Chapter 12
Results and discussion
This chapter describes the ﬁnal results of the diboson cross-check measurement, the signal strength
µmeasurement, and the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement in Section 12.1. After that,
the evaluations of signal strength measurement results for the VH(H→b¯b) process are discussed
in Section12.2.
12.1 Results
12.1.1 Diboson cross-check measurement: VZ(Z→b¯b)
A diboson cross-check ﬁt is performed by using the VZ(Z→b¯b) process as the signal process,
which is discussed in Section 1.4.3.
For the VZ(Z→ b¯b) inclusive process, the observed (expected) signiﬁcance over the
background-only hypothesis is 13.8 (15.1) standard deviations. The measured signal strength is

Since this paper assesses the VHphysics through the physics analysis, new BSM physics for the

theVZprocess must be conducted in the VZdedicated analysis [ 126], which has dedicated SRs

theµVZis ﬁxed to the SM in the VHsignal strength measurement in this analysis.
12.1.2 Signal strength measurement: VH(H→b¯b)
The signal strength in the inclusive VH(H→b¯b) process is measured in the ﬁt with 2 POIs, and
the signal strengths of the separate WH(H→b¯b) andZH(H→b¯b) processes are measured in the
ﬁt with 4 POIs.

12.1.3 Fiducial differential cross-section measurement: VHproduction in STXS
The ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-sections of the separated WHandZHproduction modes as func-
tions ofpV
T
,truth
and the number of “truth” ISR jets ( Ntruth
ISRjet) are measured for the ﬁrst time in the ﬁt
with 15 POIs. Thanks to the available statistics and the smaller systematic uncertainties, a further
split intoNtruth
ISRjet=0 andNtruth
ISRjet≥1 can be performed in the measurement in 75 <pZ
T
,truth
<400
GeV of the ZHproduction mode and in 150 <pW
T
,truth
<400 GeV of the WHproduction mode.
Figure12.3shows the results of these new measurements. In the upper panel, the observed value
ofVHcross-section times the H→b¯bandV→leptons branching ratio with its total uncertainty
and statistical uncertainty is shown with the SM prediction value with its theoretical uncertainty.
In the lower panel, the ratio of the measured value to the SM prediction is shown with its relative
uncertainty. In all STXS bins, the data statistical uncertainty is the dominant uncertainty, which
also considers the impacts from the normalisation factors on the major background processes, ex-
cept for the 75 <pW
T
,truth
<150 GeV bin where the W +jets modelling uncertainty is dominant.
The results of cross-section measurements are consistent with the SM prediction within one stan-
dard deviation, except in the 400 <pW
T
,truth
<600 GeV and pZ
T>600 GeV STXS bins where the
observed values are within two standard deviations. Over-estimation of the background processes
causes the observation of µ≤0 in some STXS bins. By discussing the physics results, the µ=0
consistent means there is no signal event in certain STXS bins, so the upper limit is supposed to
be presented in the plot. However, because the inclusive cross-section measurement observed the
signiﬁcant signal events, and because the VH(H→b¯b) analysis conventionally shows the post-ﬁt
POI values after splitting the phase space, this paper presents µvalues in the diﬀerent STXS bins in
the plots as the result of statistical processing in the ﬁt. As the conclusion, the signiﬁcant deviation
from the SM is not observed in this result.
The ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-sections of the separated WHandZHproduction modes as a
function of pV
T
,truth
are also measured to evaluate the improvement from the previous analysis [ 12]
in the ﬁt with 10 POIs. The results are shown in Figure 12.4. Compared with Figure 1.13, the
75<pW
T
,truth
<150 GeV bin and new split at pV
T
,truth
=400 GeV are added, and the relative
uncertainties in the same STXS bins are decreased by ∼10 %. The major improvement is caused
by the additional treatments of event categorisations, which are discussed in Section 5.2.4. The
new split in the boosted VH(b¯b) regime is realised because the 50 % improvement of the expected
signiﬁcance is conﬁrmed by introducing the BDT score as the ﬁnal discriminant variable in the ﬁt
and the improved b-tagging algorithms. Over-estimation of the background processes causes the
observation of µ≤0 in some STXS bins such as the high pV
Tbins, but still the plot only shows the
µvalue results and doesn’t discuss the upper limits for the µ=0 consistent result.

12.2 Discussion
Especially, the evaluations of the signal strength measurement results in Section 12.1.2are dis-
cussed in this section.
12.2.1 Impacts of the uncertainties on the µbb
VH
The contributions of the di ﬀerent sets of uncertainties to the signal strengths for the
VH(H→b¯b),WH,H→b¯b, andZH,H→b¯bprocesses are shown in the breakdown Table
12.4. A contribution of a set of systematic uncertainties (Nuisance Parameter in the ﬁt, NPs) to
the total uncertainty of the signal strength is deﬁned as the di ﬀerence in quadrature between the
total uncertainty and the uncertainty derived from a ﬁt where the relevant NPs are ﬁxed at the
best ﬁt values. The contribution of the statistical uncertainties is deﬁned as the uncertainty of a
ﬁt with all NPs ﬁxed at the best ﬁt values, except for the Normalisation Factors (NFs). The total
systematic uncertainty is calculated as the di ﬀerence in quadrature between the statistical and the
total uncertainty. The sum in quadrature of all the di ﬀerent sets of uncertainties in the breakdown
diﬀers from the total uncertainty due to correlations between the NPs. The background modelling,
especially V+jets processes, and signal modelling provide the largest contribution to the total
systematic uncertainty, besides the experimental uncertainties of jet and ﬂavour tagging are
sub-dominant in the total systematic uncertainty. In the signal strength measurements of WHand
ZHproduction modes, the size of statistical and systematic uncertainties are similar. Comparing
between the uncertainties of signal strength in WHandZHproduction modes, the dedicated
uncertainties di ﬀer according to the topologies of their production modes: For example, the signal
strength of the ZHproduction mode has larger Emiss
T experimental uncertainty because of handing
Z→ννtopology, and has a larger signal modelling uncertainty because of the limited precision
of the theoretical computation of the gluon-initiated processes, for which the lowest order is a box
or triangle diagram as shown in Figure 1.7. Whereas, the signal strength of the WHproduction
mode has larger modelling uncertainties of top quark backgrounds since they contribute most to
the background in the 1-lepton channel.

12.2.2 Estimation results of major backgrounds
The normalisation factors (NFs) after the ﬁt for the measurement of VH(H→b¯b) process are
summarised in Table 12.5for theW+jets processes, Table 12.6for theZ+jets processes, and Table
12.7for the top quark processes, where the ﬂoating normalisations are applied. A similar behaviour
is observed between the W+jets andZ+jets: the normalisation factors increase with the number
of jets and with pV
T. These trends are consistent with a measurement of the Z+hf[130], where
hf(heavy ﬂavour) is denoted as the truth ﬂavour composition of bbandcc. The normalisation
factors of the top quark background processes decrease with pV
Tsince the NLO P owheg+Pythia8
generators simulates the cross-section overhead the data as pV
Tincreases. This trend is consistent
with a measurement of the top quark transverse momentum in t¯tevents [131].

12.2.3 Effects of improved analysis technique on the uncertainties of µbb
VH
The impact of the improved analysis technique on the uncertainties of signal strength measure-
ment is also evaluated by comparing the ﬁts between conﬁgurations of this analysis and the previ-
ous analysis. As discussed in Section 11.3, to evaluate the uncertainties without scaled by µvalue,
the contributions of NPs are evaluated in the expected ﬁt with the Asimov dataset ( µ=1). Here,
the signal strength of the resolved VH(b¯b) regime (µbb
VHresolved) is studied. The contributions of
the sets of NPs are compared in the following three cases: (a) the ﬁt of previous analysis in the
resolvedVH(b¯b) regime [28], (b) the independent ﬁt of this analysis in only the resolved VH(b¯b)
regime, and (c) the simultaneous ﬁt of this analysis in all three regimes but the “ µbb
VHresolved”
POI is targeted in the ﬁt of 3 POIs as deﬁned in Section 11.2. The breakdown for the contributions
of the sets of NPs, NFs, and other µs are shown in Table 12.8. The di ﬀerence of the analysis
methods between the previous and this analysis can be evaluated by comparing between (a) and
(b). Since the events in 75 <pV
T<150 GeV in the 1-lepton channel are added from this analysis,
the statistical uncertainty decreases by ∼17 %. Thanks to the improvement of jet ﬂavour tagging
performance, the b-tagging experimental uncertainty decreases by ∼48 %. While the uncertainty
from the simulated sample size decreases by ∼21 % due to the updates of MC simulation of
V+jets, the modelling uncertainties of V+jets and signal processes increase by a factor of 2 after
re-estimation in the new phase space in this analysis. The e ﬀects of performing the ﬁt in all three
regimes simultaneously can be evaluated by comparing between (b) and (c). Because the major
backgrounds with mixing ﬂavour jet events and light ﬂavours jet events can be constrained from
the dedicated CRs in the VH(c¯c) regime, and the W+hfevents can be constrained from the Low-
∆RCR exploiting the BDT CRLow, the uncertainty of background ﬂoating normalisations decreases
by∼14 %. Moreover, the modelling uncertainties of V+jets, top quark backgrounds decrease. The
modelling uncertainty of diboson background doesn’t decrease because it doesn’t have a dedicated
CR. Since the strong correlation exists between the jet energy scale uncertainty related to ﬂavour
composition and the ﬂoating normalisations of major backgrounds, the experimental uncertainty
of the jet decreases by ∼24 %.

Chapter 13
Conclusion
This study has a huge interest in the Higgs boson production associated with a Z /W boson (VH
production), since the di ﬀerential cross-section of the VHproduction has high sensitivity for new
physics beyond the SM (BSM) in high momentum of a vector boson. In order to obtain enough
statistics of the VHproduction, a decay mode of a pair of two bottom quarks with a branching
fraction of 58 % is chosen. The coupling between the Higgs boson and a bottom quark also has
high sensitivity for BSM. The signiﬁcant deviation from the SM prediction was not observed in
the previous VH(H→b¯b) analysis within its uncertainty. Meanwhile, it’s still possible to im-
prove its precision, since the major sources of its large uncertainty are experimental and modelling
variations rather than its statistics.
This thesis presents the improved precise measurement of the VH(H→b¯b) process using the
LHC proton-proton collisions data collected in the A TLAS experiment in 2015-2018. The centre
of mass energy is 13 TeV , and the integrated luminosity is 140 fb−1. The VH(H→b¯b) and
VH(H→c¯c) signal processes are analysed simultaneously in order to constrain their common
background. The FSR recovery is introduced to reduce statistical uncertainties of the di ﬀerential
cross-section measurement for the ﬁrst time; when splitting the region of di ﬀerential cross-section
measurement by the number of additional jets, the statistical uncertainties decrease up to ∼8 %.
All the experimental and modelling uncertainties are upgraded compared to the previous analysis
[28,12]. In terms of the estimation of shape uncertainty of the ﬁt variables, the CARL method is
ﬁrst adopted in this analysis; the impact of diboson modelling uncertainty on the signal strength
measurement of VH(H→b¯b) process is reduced by ∼2 %.
The measurements of the diboson process VZ,Z→b¯bare performed as a validation of the
analysis methodology and of background estimations. The signal strength of VZ,V→b¯bprocess
isµbb
VZ=0.91+0.13
−0.11=0.91±0.05(stat.)+0.12
−0.10(syst.) TheWZ,Z→b¯bprocess is measured with an

=== PDF PAGE 192 ===
178
observed signiﬁcance of 6.4 standard deviations, while the ZZ,Z→b¯bprocess is measured with
an observed signiﬁcance of more than 10 standard deviations. This measurement is consistent with
the SM predictions within their uncertainties.
The signal strength of VH(H→b¯b) process is µbb
VH=0.92+0.16
−0.15=0.91±0.10(stat.)+0.13
−0.11(syst.),
which is the most precise result in the world. The precision improved by ∼14 % from the pre-
vious analysis [ 12]. The measurement of the VH(H→b¯b) process has an observed (expected)
signiﬁcance of 7.4(8.0) standard deviations. The signiﬁcant impact of the BSM physics cannot be
conﬁrmed from this result. The individual production of WHwithH→b¯bis established with an
observed (expected) signiﬁcance of 5.3(5.5) standard deviations, which is the ﬁrst observation of
this process. This measurement is consistent with the SM predictions within their uncertainties.
The ﬁnest di ﬀerential cross-section measurement of the VHproduction as functions of Z /W
boson transverse momentum and the number of additional jets, which is not induced from the
Higgs boson, is ﬁrst performed in the world. The division of the phase space into the number of
jets reduces a theoretical uncertainty caused by QCD scale variations. The uncertainties vary from
50 % in the sensitive regions to 400 % in the lowest and the highest Z /W boson momentum regions.
The diﬀerential cross-section measurement as a function of a Z /W boson transverse momentum is
also performed, where the uncertainties decrease ∼10% from the previous analysis [ 28,12]. All
measurements are consistent with the SM predictions within their uncertainties. The signiﬁcant
deviation from the SM is not observed in these results.
In the future prospects, this VH(b¯b) analysis can probe the properties of the VH(H→b¯b) pro-
cess in higher precision with more statistics of the A TLAS data. The LHC-A TLAS experiment
plans to collect ∼3000fb−1at
→
s=14 TeV in the HL-LHC project as described in Section
2.1. The measurement results of the VH(H→b¯b) process are extrapolated with the ∼3000fb−1
dataset with the same analysis method of the previous analysis [ 12] as described in Appendix J.
This extrapolation shows that the dominant uncertainty of expected signal strength is still from the
systematic uncertainty, while the statistical uncertainty generally is improved ∼50%, and the total
uncertainty of expected signal strength is ∼6%. Therefore, the analysis techniques improved in
this thesis can help to suppress the systematic uncertainty, which may result in the observation of
signiﬁcant deviation from the SM prediction in the Higgs property measurements.
