Appendix

=== PDF PAGE 194 ===
180
Appendix A
Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics
system
This chapter discusses the development and study of control system of the TGC front-end electron-
ics system for the HL-LHC. The overall TGC electronics system for the HL-LHC is summarised
in Section A.1. The functionality of hit-signal processor in the front-end (PS Board) is described
in SectionA.2. To conﬁgure, control, and monitor the front-end modules, especially the PS Board,
a hub module (JA THub) is developed. This is described in Section A.3. A control system driven
by the JA THub is studied in their combined test bench, and is described in Section A.4.
A.1 Overall TGC electronics system for HL-LHC
As described in Section 2.1, the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) accelerator at CERN will be
upgraded to increase its peak instantaneous luminosity up to 7 .5×1034cm−2s−1, referred to as
“High-Luminosity LHC(HL-LHC)”, from 2026 [ 33]. Due to this upgrade, the number of pileup
events per bunch crossing would reach to ∼200. To cope with the high luminosity environment
and collect collision events to search for physics of interest e ﬃciently, the A TLAS experiment is
going to upgrade the A TLAS detector and the trigger data acquisition (DAQ) system, denoted as
the Phase-2 upgrade (2026 - 2030) [ 132].
The Thin Gap Chamber (TGC) of the A TLAS detector is responsible for the trigger on muons in
the end-cap region as shown in Figure A.1(a), at the hardware-based trigger stage as described in
Section2.2.3. In the TGC sub-detector system, the front-end and back-end electronics will be up-
graded, as illustrated in Figure A.1(b), to send and receive binary hit-map at every bunch crossing
(BC) to the back-end system. The upgraded TGC system consists of the primary processing boards

=== PDF PAGE 195 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 181
(PS Board), hub modules for slow control (JTAG Assistance Hub (JA THub)) in the front-end, and
Sector Logic boards (SL) in the back-end. Here, the PS Board will be placed on the TGC detector
and the JA THub be placed in the blue VME crate on the rim of the TGC detector. The front-end
electronics will be implemented in the A TLAS cavern, and the back-end modules will be placed in
the counting room, 60 - 100 m apart from the A TLAS cavern. Besides the digital components, the
analogue Ampliﬁer-Shaper-Discriminator (ASD) chips are not replaced from the current system
and are mounted on the TGC detectors to send the hit signal. The current TGC detectors will be
kept for the HL-LHC.
(a) TGC front-end
 (b) TGC electronics system
Fig. A.1: The A TLAS and TGC detector. (a) shows the TGC detector and the placement of TGC
front-end electronics. (b) illustrates the overall TGC electronics system with the front-end and
back-end electronics.
A.2 Functionality of the PS Board
The PS Board is equipped with eight Patch Panel (PP) ASICs and one Xilinx Kintex-7 FPGA,
as shown in Figure A.2(a).
The PP ASICs receive hit signals from the ASD and adjust the signal timing to align the signal-
timing diﬀerence due to time of ﬂight and length of signal cables of each channel, using variable
delays available at the beginning ﬁrmware logic in the PP ASIC. The PP ASICs also perform hit
BC identiﬁcation (BCID) with a conﬁgurable gate-width. The variable delay and conﬁgurable
gate-width unit are controlled by a Phase Locked Loop (PLL)-based circuit exploiting the LHC

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===
182 A.3 Development of the JA THub
clock synchronised to the BC. Then, the binary hit-map is sent to the FPGA at every BC. The
FPGA receives hit signals from the PP ASICs and transfers the binary hit-map at every BC to the
SL via optical links, running at 8 Gbps without data reduction. The use of high-speed optical links
successfully simpliﬁes the system design and improves the ﬂexibility of the trigger algorithm in
the SL.
The FPGA receives the 40 MHz LHC clock distributed from the SL with optical links with ﬁxed
latency and adjusts the clock phase using the built-in PLL of the FPGA clocking resource within
O(100) ps precision. An on-board clock jitter attenuator distributes the LHC clock to the PP ASICs
and provides a system clock for the FPGA operation and a reference clock to the optical serial link
transmitters of the FPGA.
(a) PS Board
 (b) JA THub
Fig. A.2: The prototype modules of TGC front-end electronics for phase-2 upgrade. These mod-
ules are developed exploiting the intellectual property of Open-It. The plots are also published in
Ref. [133].
A.3 Development of the JA THub
The JA THub is a control hub module for the FPGAs on the PS Boards. The JA THub is equipped
with the Xilinx Zynq-7000 System-on-a-Chip (SoC) device. The Zynq SoC integrates an ARM-
based processor with Kintex-7 FPGA and serves as the main driver of the control system. The
JA THub is a 9U-sized VME module.
The JA THub can control up to 11 PS Board modules. The JA THub has an Ethernet interface
via optical ﬁbres (1000base-X), exploiting built-in transceivers in the Programmable Logic (PL)
part of the Zynq. The processor system (PS) of the Zynq runs applications to control the JTAG

=== PDF PAGE 197 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 183
signals for the PS Board FPGAs programming and debugging. Therefore, the connected PS Board
FPGAs can be remotely programmed over the Ethernet connection. Moreover, the JA THubs assist
the ﬁne-tuning of the front-end LHC clock phase.
The JA THub also takes a role in recovering PS Board FPGAs for unrecoverable SEU errors
in the conﬁguration memory during physics data taking. The PS Board FPGAs run the Xilinx
Soft Error Mitigation (SEM) controllers [ 134] to monitor, detect, and automatically recover the
SEU errors. The FPGAs will send a recovery request signal when a SEM controller observes
unrecoverable SEU error. Then, the JA THub will start the entire reprogramming process based on
ﬂash memory on the PS Board. The recovery functionality is independent of the PS to run stably
regardless of possible PS CPU crashes during the data taking. A similar mechanism is used to
recover the PL of the Zynq devices of the JA THubs, exploiting connections between JA THubs.
For radiation damages on the storage device used for booting, the JA THub system introduces
redundancy in the boot procedure. One quad SPI ﬂash memory (QSPI) and two SD cards can store
the boot images on a JA THub as shown in Figure A.2(b). The QSPI is used as the primary boot
device of the Zynq-7000 SoC, and the SD cards is the secondary persistent storage to store the
PL bit-stream and kernel image. Redundancy is introduced by having boot images duplicated in
these devices. A procedure to switch the choice of ﬁles for both primary and secondary boot ﬁles
remotely is developed and tested. The QSPI is also accessible directly from outside via VME path
to assure its redundancy.
According to the design of the control system, a prototype mode of the JA THub was fabricated.
Major functionalities for the designed control system driven by the JA THubs have been developed
and tested with the JA THubs (see Figure A.2(b)), and integration tests with the PS Board are held
in a combined system, as discussed in Section A.4. The mass production model of the JA THub is
fabricated after the testings of the designed control system.
A.4 Combined test and system-level study
The prototype modules of the PS Board and JA THub are intensively tested at the broad level and
then integrated into a test bench system for the system-level commissioning at KEK, as shown in
FigureA.3. Sector Logic (SL) module which is used in the Run 3 of LHC-A TLAS su ﬃciently em-
ulates the back-end SL for HL-LHC in this size of the test bench. The optical links are connected
to emulate the connections between the back-end and front-end systems for hit bit-map readout,
register control, and timing signal distribution. The test bench consists of two prototype PS Boards
and one prototype JA THub. 15 m or 30 m category 6 cables connect the PS Boards and JA THubs
to demonstrate the developed functionality in the equivalent hardware setup as the A TLAS cavern.

=== PDF PAGE 198 ===
184 A.4 Combined test and system-level study
Fig. A.3: The test bench of the TGC electronics system for Phase-2 upgrade.
For the JTAG access from the JA THub to the FPGAs on the PS-Boards, applications and
ﬁrmware for the Xilinx Virtual Cable (XVC) [ 135] and Serial V ector Format (SVF) player
[136] have been developed. The XVC application allows us to use the Xilinx GUI interface for
programming, debugging, e.g. Xilinx Integrated Logic Analyzer [ 137], and testing, e.g. Xilinx
Integrated Bit Error Ratio Tester [ 138], the remote FPGAs via Ethernet. The SVF player realises
fast programming using bit-streams stored in the SD card of the JA THub. The clock frequency
in the JTAG signals has been optimised for the length of the category 6 cables, and the JTAG
successfully runs at 1.25 MHz, as expected in this setup.
The recovery procedure for an unrecoverable SEU error case is demonstrated. The JA THub
successfully initiates the reprogramming using the onboard ﬂash memory of the PS Board on
the request. In addition, based on the experience during the demonstration on the test bench,
the stability in the signal lines to initiate reprogramming have been improved. Any unexpected
short asserted signals ( ∼80 ms) in the signal line will be discarded on the PS Board owing to an
additional debouncer device (see Figure A.4).
The clock distributions to the PS Boards with a ﬁxed latency via serial links between the PS
Board and the SL have been successfully implemented and demonstrated. A variation of latency of
less than 20 ps is observed for power cycling and resynchronisation procedure of the optical links.
Phase monitoring and adjustment functions have also been implemented. The measurements of
the LHC clocks on the JA THub are successfully performed in accuracy of 1 /56 ns, and the results
are retrieved by software running on the Zynq PS. Functionality to control the clock phase on the
PS Board FPGAs successfully runs with a delay step of 1 /56 ns. The adjustment of clock signals
distributed to the two prototype PS Boards with di ﬀerent ﬁber length has been demonstrated. The

=== PDF PAGE 199 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 185

blue output signal shows that the debouncer discards the asserted signal.
timing diﬀerence of the clocks is observed as expected in the machinery, and the clock phase is
correctly adjusted.

Appendix C
SMEFT interpretation
In the previous VH(H→b¯b) analysis [12], from the results of ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section
measurement, the SMEFT interpretation, which is introduced in Section 1.1.4, is performed and
the SMEFT parameters are derived by exploiting the SMEFTsim package [ 8]. The same LHC-
A TLAS Run2 full dataset as this thesis is used in the previous analysis.
In this analysis, only D=6 dimension is considered, since D=5 andD=7 dimension
operators violate lepton or baryon number conservation, and D≥8 dimensions are negligible
which are suppressed by the energy scale 1 /ΛD−4. The Wilson coe ﬃcients and corresponding
SMEFT operators, which is signiﬁcantly sensitive in this analysis, are shown in Table C.1.A
diﬀerential ﬁducial cross-section measurement in this analysis constrains the Wilson coe ﬃcients
in the ’Warsaw’ formulation [ 146].
The diﬀerential cross-section of VHproduction is sensitive to following three operators [ 10,
11,9].Q(3)
Hqhas a strong impact for both the ZHandWHcross-section depending on momentum
ofZ/Wboson, while QHuhas a similar impact for only the ZHcross-section. QHWworks for an
overall modiﬁcation of the VHcross-section.
The interference terms between the SM and BSM contributions are proportional to the coef-
ﬁcients (linear parameterisation), corresponding to O(1/Λ2), while BSM contributes are propor-
tional to the square of coe ﬃcients (quadratic parameterisation), corresponding to O(1/Λ4). Since
the linearD=8 terms cannot be included in the interpretation for now, results for both the linear
and linear plus quadratic D=6 terms are studied to indicate possible e ﬀects from the linear D=8
terms.
Maximum-likelihood ﬁts between the experimental data and simulated samples are performed
across the STXS regions to constrain the limits of the Wilson coe ﬃcients. The results of the
constraints of the Wilson coe ﬃcientsc(3)
Hq,cHu,cHWare shown in Figure C.1as a function of their

188

parameter value [ 12], whereci=0 corresponds to the SM prediction. As an example, Figure C.1
(a) and (b) quantify the compatibility of the data to hypothesis of c(3)
Hqvalue as the observed negative
log-likelihood in the linear parameterisation (a) or in the linear plus quadratic parameterisation
(b). Figure C.1(c) summarises the observed results for the three coe ﬃcients showing their one-
dimensional 68 % and 95 % CL intervals. The operators of c(3)
Hq,cHu,cHWcoeﬃcients have pV
T
dependence as shown in Section 1.5and were measured with good sensitivity in the previous
analysis.
In an inclusive number of ISR jets bin (a pV
Tbin), thegg→ZHprocess (Figure 1.7) doesn’t
contribute much, thus the gginitiatedZHprocess is ﬁxed to the SM prediction in this result.
However, diﬀerential cross-section of the gg→ZHproduction should be measured independently
in futureVH(H→b¯b) analysis. Modiﬁcation of the gg→ZHproduction cross-section can be
derived from either higher-dimension ( D≥8) operators or QCD theoretical correction in NNLO.
Since the eﬀect of linearD=8 terms is conﬁrmed in quadratic terms, which appears in the negative
values of the Wilson coe ﬃcients in Figure C.1(b) (c), it’s worth separating the events of qq→ZH
andgg→ZHproduction as much as possible and to look the e ﬀect of quadratic D=6 terms. In
the number of ISR jets ≥1 bin, more contribution of the gg→ZHprocess is expected than that
in the number of ISR jets =0 bin. In terms of more sensitivity on the gg→ZHprocess in the
multiple jet bin, splitting the STXS bin with the number of ISR jets is motivated.

=== PDF PAGE 204 ===
190
Appendix D
Overlap between boosted VH,H→
b¯bandVH,H→c¯cregimes
The resolved VH(b¯b) and boosted VH(b¯b) regimes are orthogonal due to the pV
Tcut at 400 GeV:
events with pV
T<400 GeV are handedl in the resolved VH(b¯b) regime, while events with higher
pV
Tare handled in the boosted regime. From the description in Section 5.2, it is also clear that
all the regions in the resolved VH(b¯b) andVH(c¯c) regimes are designed carefully to avoid double
counting an event in two di ﬀerent regions. However, since the VH(c¯c) regime is currently ex-
ploiting all events without any upper limit of pV
T, the events that pass the VH(c¯c) selection with
pV
T>400 GeV can also pass the boosted VH(b¯b) selection, which result in an overlap between
these two regimes. This is because a loose b-tagging requirement is used in the boosted VH(b¯b)
selection (85 %).
The overlap study shows that in the data, around 99.6 % of VH(c¯c) SR events do not overlap
with boosted VH(b¯b) SRs and 95.7 % of boosted VH(b¯b) events are not contained in VH(c¯c) SRs.
Since this overlap is not signiﬁcant, no special treatment is needed for it.

Appendix G
Additional materials of the FSR re-
covery
G.1 T ruth study
In order to validate the FSR properties, truth study is conducted with generator truth samples
ofqq-initiatedZH,Z→ℓℓ,H→b¯bsignal process. The validation with truth samples is done in
the resolved VH(b¯b) regime in the 2-lepton channel. MC weight is only applied for whole event
weight. Normalization with cross-section is not applied.
Truth dRB1J3 +dRB2J3 vs pV
Tplots are present in Figure G.1, truth fraction of FSR events in
each number of jet region is present in Figure G.2, and truthmbb jplots with truth FSR, ISR events
are present in Figure G.3.
Truth dR(B1J3) +dR(B2J3) vs pV
Tplots for the t¯tevents are also present in Figure G.4. The
3rd gluon-induced jets tend to have the same kinematic feature as the VHevents in the relative
position against two b-jets. These events are concentrating in the low pV
Tregion. The 3rd jet in the
low dR(B1J3) +dR(B2J3) distributes nearly or between the two b-jets, and it is coming from the
hadronic decaying Wor between two top quarks with small close angle.

200 G.2 Eﬀective order of correction for the FSR recovery
G.2 Effective order of correction for the FSR recovery
The timing to apply FSR recovery during the jet correction in Section 6.1is studied. Perfor-
mance of mBB resolution was compared in the following three cases.
AApplying FSR recovery after the muon-in-jet correction, before the PtReco, KF
BApplying FSR recovery after the muon-in-jet correction, the PtReco and before the KF
CApplying FSR recovery after all the correction
The comparisons of mass resolution between the case A and case B is shown in Figure G.5. The
comparisons of mass resolution between the case B and case C is shown in Figure G.6.

+PtReco+FSR.
.
There is no big di ﬀerence in performance as shown in Figure G.5and Figure G.6. Therefore,
FSR recovery is applied mainly after the PtReco in VHbb resolved, or after muon-in-jet correction
in VHcc
However, when the KF applied, FSR recovery is applied after KF due to KF’s theoritical reason;
Its transfer function in the KF likelihood is deﬁned with all reconstructed jets (ﬂavour-jets, FSR
jets, ISR jets), so FSR jets should be separately treated from the b-jets.

Fig. G.7: Comparison of Higgs mass resolution ( mbb) before and after the FSR recovery. The
Higgs mass distributions in all three pV
Tregions in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(b¯b)
regime are made with the signal nominal MC sample ( ZH(Z→ℓℓ,H→b¯b) process). The jet
mass corrections of the Muon-in-jet and PtReco are already applied. Plot in ﬁrst column shows
the mass distribution of events with only an FSR identiﬁed jet in the 3-jet region before the event
migration, where these events are migrated into the 2-jet region. For them, a blue line shows
invariant mass of two b-jets (mbb), and a red line shows invariant mass of two b-jets and an FSR
identiﬁed jet ( mbb j(FS R)). The red distribution in second column is added to the mbbdistribution in
the 2-jet region (a). Plot in second column shows the comparison in the 2-jet regions, and plot in
third column shows the comparison in the 3-jet region. For plots in the both column, a black line
denotes the mass distribution before the FSR recovery, and a red line denotes the mass distribution
after the FSR recovery.

Understanding of the signiﬁcance results and feasibility study to improve the signiﬁcance

To understand the results of signiﬁcance, checking the distribution changes of auxiliary variables
for the BDT training and the BDT input variables is valid. Figure 7.8shows the changes of event
yields in diﬀerent nJet regions by the FSR recovery for the VHsignal,t¯t, andW+jets processes
in the 1-lepton channel. As described in Section 8.3, the information of nJet region is used in the
BDTVHtraining. Figure 7.9shows the changes of the distributions for the important BDT input
variables in the 1-lepton channel ( mbb,mbb j) for theVHsignal,t¯t, andW+jets processes.
As described above, the events in peak around 125 GeV of mbb jdistribution of the VHsignal
process in 3-jet region are identiﬁed and migrated to the mbbdistribution in 2-jet region, as shown
in Figure7.9(a) and (d). Since the peak around 125 GeV of mbb jis one of the important feature to
discriminating the signal and background processes, the mbb jwas ranked to the 2nd highest in an
importance list of the 16 BDT input variables in the BDT training of 3-jet region in the 1-lepton
channel, where the highest ranked variable is always the mbb. In the BDT training after the FSR
recovery,mbb jis ranked down to the 4th highest in the importance list, because a small peak is left
around 125 GeV as shown in Figure 7.9(d), which still helps the discrimination.
To increase the signiﬁcance, the FSR recovery should not collect the t¯tbackground events. Thus,

=== PDF PAGE 223 ===
Chapter G. Additional materials of the FSR recovery 209
some optimisations are demanded in the FSR identiﬁcation in order to collect only the VHsignal
events with FSR jets. There are three suggestions for the optimisations. The second and third
suggestions are already studied as described below. Firstly, it should be reasonable to apply the
FSR recovery only in 2-jet and 3-jet events in the 1-lepton channel. This would provide large
impact on the signiﬁcance in the 3-jet region, since most of the events in the 4-jet region migrate
to the 3-jet region. Secondly, an alternative scheme to apply the FSR recovery toward only the
additional jet in the phet
T >30 GeV can reduce the impact on the signiﬁcance, while it doesn’t a ﬀect
on the ﬁducial di ﬀerential cross-section measurement. Since almost half of the FSR-identiﬁed jet
of theVHsignal process distributes in the 20 <pjet
T <30 GeV and the additional jets of the t¯t
process distributes more in the low pjet
Tregion, the alternative scheme may reduce the decrease of
signiﬁcance. As the results, by applying the FSR recovery with the alternative scheme, the BDT VH
signiﬁcance in the 1-lepton channel decreases by ∼1 %, which is not applicable enough to adopt
the FSR recovery in the 1-lepton channel. Finally, it seems to be good to add some discriminant
kinematic variable in the cut function, since the t¯twith multi-jet events have the same kinematic
feature as the VHevents for the relative position of the 3rd gluon-induced jet against the two b-
jets, as shown in Figure G.4. The most powerful kinematic variable to distinguish the VHsignal
andt¯tprocesses is the invariant mass of top quark ( mtop), which is deﬁned in Section 8.2. Figure
G.13shows distributions of mtopby comparing these distributions between the VHsignal and t¯t
background processes in 3-jet events with only an FSR identiﬁed jet in the 1-lepton channel. The
distributions are normalised to their event yields in the di ﬀerent analysis regions. The di ﬀerence
of distributions is signiﬁcant in the high pV
Tregion as shown in Figure G.13(c), but otherwise the
mtopis not discriminant between the VHandt¯tevents. Since the FSR tends to be radiated in the
lowpV
Tregion as shown in Figure 7.4, it cannot be expected to improve the signiﬁcance by simply
adding themtopinto the FSR cut function.

H.3 Alternative models in detail
H.3.1 Electroweak(EW) correction
The full NLO EW calculations for V ector-boson-related events contain contributions from vir-
tual loops and from real emissions of gauge bosons. The S herpa2.2.11 samples only contain the
virtual-loop contributions, neglecting real emission both for simplicity and because real emissions
of vector bosons would lead to a fundamentally di ﬀerent experimental signature that is typically
produced in separate samples. The NLO EW corrections are then combined to the NLO QCD
terms (reminding from table H.2that the Sherpa2.2.11 ME are computed at NLO in QCD up to 2
extra partons) according to three di ﬀerent schemes: additive, multiplicative and exponentiated. A
summary of the three approaches is given in the following: full details about their motivation and
the approximations applied can be found in Sec 3.1 of [ 147].
The cross-section calculated at NLO QCD and NLO EW accuracy for a 2 →nﬁnal state, where
the higher-order terms in QCD and EW couplings are summed ( additive scheme ), can be expressed
as:
σNLO
n,QCD+EW=σn,LO+∆σNLO
n,QCD+∆σNLO
n,EWvirt
(H.1)
whereσn,LOdenotes the Born-level cross-section and ∆σNLO
n,QCDthe NLO QCD corrections to the
Born process. The ∆σNLO
n,EWvirt
term only contains the virtual-loop components.
Two additional NLO QCD and NLO EW combination schemes are provided by the
Sherpaframework. The ﬁrst is the multiplicative scheme , which is deﬁned as:
σNLO
n,QCD×EW=(σn,LO+∆σNLO
n,QCD)×(1+∆σNLO
n,EWvirt
). (H.2)
The second scheme is referred to as the exponentiated scheme , and is deﬁned as:
σNLO
n,QCD×eEWvirt =(σn,LO+∆σNLO
n,QCD)×e∆σNLO
n,EWvirt. (H.3)
The multiplicative and exponentiated schemes are motivated by the factorization of the QCD
and EW Sudakov corrections at high energies [ 147]. Small variations are observed for these two
schemes comparing to the additive scheme.
Comparisons of the NLO QCD-only prediction with and without the virtual electroweak correc-
tions are shown in Figure H.2.pV
Tshape changes are expectedly observed in ﬁgure H.2. The higher
pV
T, the more yields decrease, which arises as NLO EW correction due to the emission and reab-
sorption of electroweak gauge bosons during high-energy scattering processes. Yields decrease by
ZZ:∼7.4%, WZ:∼3.8%, WW:∼4.6% in this analysis phase space. However, the additive scheme

=== PDF PAGE 232 ===
218 H.3 Alternative models in detail
of EW correction is applied to nominal shape and nominal normalization in this analysis, since the
yield change is less than the PMG’s observation and the change is negligible rather than Diboson’s
total acceptance uncertainties.
(a) 2L resolved VH(b¯b)
 (b) 2L boosted VH(b¯b)
(c) 1L resolved VH(b¯b)
 (d) 1L boosted VH(b¯b)
Fig. H.2: EW correction e ﬀect on the Diboson’s pV
Tdistribution in each channel and each regime.
Upper panel shows the kinematic distributions, and grey line of bottom panel shows the relative
shape change by the EW correction

H.3.2 Comparison of S HERP A2.2.11 and S HERP A2.2.1
The Sherpa2.2.11 nominal samples are being used for the ﬁrst time in this analysis. For refer-

- from the stand point of this study - between the new and previous setups of the S herpagenerator
is shown in Table H.2.

Gluon colour/spin exact matching Y es No
Core process for K-factor 2→4 2→2
Phase-space strategy Inclusive Analytic enhancement
Table H.2: Summary of the S herpa2.2.1 and Sherpa2.2.11 conﬁgurations.
The peak of invariant mass of 2 b-jets shifts to high mass because the fragmentation function of
the Sherpais changed between these two versions [ 77]. This peak change can be observed in ﬁgure
H.3. The plots show the comparison of mass distribution between EW corrected S herpa2.2.11 and
Sherpa2.2.1. Since the EW correction only scales the pV
Tshape but doesn’t change the mass shape,
this mass shift is caused by the S herpaversion change. This mbbpeak change can also be observed
at the truth level as shown in Figure H.4. Because there is little evidence that which S herpaversion
can describe the data better, the S herpa2.2.1 is introduced to estimate fragmentation systematic
uncertainty.
Comparing to the S herpa2.2.1, the cross-section di ﬀers∼5-10 %, which can be covered by the
total acceptance uncertainty.

H.4 Systematic uncertainties
H.4.1 Normalization and acceptance uncertainties
All the acceptance uncertainties are computed by enveloping each variation of all alternative
sources with quadrature-sum calculation. For alternative MC models’ source, the largest variation
(Sherpa2.2.1 or Powheg+Pythia8) or the largest statistical uncertainty is quadrature-summed,
where nominal NLO QCD ( µR=µF=1) model of S herpa2.2.11 is treated as the nominal model.
For each internal weight’s source except the alternative schemes of EW correction, the largest

=== PDF PAGE 236 ===
222 H.4 Systematic uncertainties
variation is quadrature-summed, where nominal NLO QCD ( µR=µF=1) model is treated as the
nominal model. For the alternative schemes of EW correction, the both two schemes’ variations
are quadrature-summed, where the additive scheme of EW correction is treated as the nominal
model. Table H.3shows the calculation example of the acceptance uncertainty to enveloping the
all variations.

Table H.4: Normalisation uncertainty for each process. ggVV number is referred from other
A TLAS Diboson analysis.
Relative acceptance uncertainties for signal components: VZbb and VZcc
Because VZbb process has high statistics and the purest region in VH(b¯b) regime, VZbb un-
certainty numbers computed in VH(b¯b) regime are applied to all regimes. Since VZbb and VZcc
are topologically the same objects but they have only the ﬂavor tagging di ﬀerence, these VZbb
numbers are also shared with VZcc process for harmonization purpose among the Diboson signal
components.
For the Stewart-Tackmann (ST) uncertainty [ 118], a correction factor K is used to prevent from
over-estimation. K=0.5 for∆pV
Tuncertainties, and K=0.8 for∆nJet uncertainties.

Appendix I
Additional materials of ﬁnal results
I.1 Comparison of BDT -based ﬁt and mbb-based ﬁt
In addition to the ﬁt using BDT VZin SRs, the ﬁt using mbbin SRs is also performed for the
VZ,Z→b¯bprocess as the veriﬁcation of BDT VZ’s advantage. The observed signiﬁcance of BDT-
based ﬁt is ∼20 % greater than that of mbb-based ﬁt.
In addition to the ﬁt using BDT VHin SRs, the ﬁt using mbbin SRs is also performed for the
VH,H→b¯bprocess as the veriﬁcation of BDT VH’s advantage. The observed signiﬁcance of
BDT-based ﬁt is ∼60 % greater than that of mbb-based ﬁt.
I.2 Signal strength results in the different lepton channels or in
different production modes
In FigureI.1, the trends of observed signal strength of the VH(H→b¯b) process resemble that
of theVZ(Z→b¯b) process.

Appendix J
Extrapolation of the signal sensitiv-
ity inVH(H→b¯b)process at the HL-
LHC
In the HL-LHC senario, dataset of
→
s=14 TeV proton-proton collisions corresponding to an
integrated luminosity of 3000 fb −1is assumed*1. The same analysis method of the previous
VH(H→b¯b) analysis [12] is applied, and normalized with the expected dataset for the HL-LHC.
The systematic uncertainties used in this extrapolation analysis are scaled from the previous anal-
ysis [12], and are listed as Table J.1. This conﬁguration is extrapolated from the expected perfor-
mance of the A TLAS experimental setup for the HL-LHC project [ 149,?]. The expected best-ﬁt
results are derived with the Asimov dataset.
The signal strength of the inclusive VH(H→b¯b) prcess and the separated WH,H→b¯b,
ZH,H→b¯bprocesses are derived as [ 150],
µbb
VH=1.00±0.06, (J.1)
µbb
ZH=1.00±0.07=1.00±0.03(S tat.)±0.06(S yst.), (J.2)
µbb
WH=1.00±0.08=1.00±0.04(S tat.)±0.07(S yst.). (J.3)
The precisions of signal strengths improved by ∼55 % for the inclusive process, ∼73 % for the
ZH,H→b¯bprocess and ∼68 % for the WH,H→b¯bprocess from the previous analysis [ 12].
The jets experimental uncertainty and signal modelling uncertainty is dominant in the systematic
*1The latest extrapolation to the HL-LHC senario for the VH(H→b¯b/c¯c) analysis is published (25 Mar 2025) as a
public note [148] from CERN after the defence of this doctoral dissertation (19 Mar 2025).

The previous analysis presents the separated signal strengths results in the same reduced STXS
scheme. The uncertainties of these 5 POI varies from ∼30 % in the low pV
Tregions to∼85 % in
the highpV
Tregions. As the results of this extrapolation analysis, the uncertainties varies from ∼8
% in the high pV
Tregions to ∼18 in the low pV
Tregions. This precision may be able to a ﬀord to
observe the deviations from the SM prediction in the high pV
Tregions.
